 

# 

# The Journey

Here is the updated beginning of the story, incorporating the 11th-century context, the tragic death of Zoser's father, the historical realities of Aksum's decline, and the mother's miscalculated turn toward the Christian highlands:

---

### **The Great Hardship and the March South**

The year 1064 marked the onset of *al-Shidda al-Uzma*—the Great Calamity. For seven agonizing years, the life-giving pulse of the Nile failed, refusing to flood the parched plains of Egypt. The land, once lush and nurturing, cracked under a relentless sun, leaving nothing but famine in its wake.

We did not wait for the seven years to pass before fleeing the ruin of our home. As the crisis deepened, my mother, Amara, and my father, Ika, realized that staying meant certain death. Packing what little we could carry, we joined the desperate exodus heading south toward the highlands of Aksum. I was young, but I remember the dust, the gnawing hunger, and the constant fear.

That fear became our executioner along the way. While navigating the treacherous, dried-out paths away from the Nile, we were ambushed by bandits seeking whatever meager provisions travelers possessed. My father, Ika, a man of strength and spirit, refused to yield to them. In a desperate struggle to buy time for my mother and me to escape, Ika drew his final breath at the hands of their flashing swords, falling while shielding his family.

We, the Nilotic people, were children of the river, our skin the rich, dark earth after the rains, our features sculpted by the sun – high cheekbones, broad noses, and eyes that held the ancient wisdom of the Nile. Our culture pulsed with the rhythm of drums and the haunting melodies of our flutes, stories etched onto our skin through intricate scarification, and our lives interwoven with the spirits of our ancestors. But the old ways, the traditional remedies that had always sustained us, offered no solace to the sickness that gripped Amara. Oldest form of chistianity had grown in these lands  and church had the ark of covenant, though some fragments was missing, this was kept secret by the church, believing that those who have these fragments are cursed.

Grief-stricken and terrified, Amara and I continued the march alone, eventually reaching the once-mighty Kingdom of Aksum.

Yet, when we arrived, we found a kingdom vastly changed from the grand stories of its past. The great metropolis was shrinking, its bustling urban centers and global trade dominance having faded as the coastal routes were lost to the famine's economic shock. The empire that had once boasted a booming gold-coinage system was now a smaller, isolated, and agrarian medieval state, shifting its center of power and agriculture away from the old city into the interior highland plateau.

In the midst of this shifting, unfamiliar world, my mother—deeply rooted in traditional Nilotic cultural practices—looked toward the Ge'ez-speaking Christian highland culture as a new source of salvation. She hoped the ancient, deep-seated Christian faith of the region would offer us protection and grace in a world that had turned dark. However, her turn toward the church was a miscalculation; the new, isolated agrarian state offered no sanctuary from the trouble and unrest that continued to pursue us. Realizing Aksum could no longer keep us safe, my mother turned her eyes further south toward the Lasta Mountains, and the arduous journey to the Zagwe churches of Lalibela began.

 clawed at our lives, a gnawing hunger that mirrored the famine gripping the land. Whispers of the ancient plague, a pestilence that had haunted our ancestors for centuries, slithered through our Nilotic community, a constant, chilling reminder of fragility. My mother, Amara, once a woman whose laughter echoed through our mud-brick home in Aksum, was now a shadow. Each breath hitched, a shallow, painful rasp. Her strong legs, which once carried me effortlessly, now trembled with the effort of a few steps. I, Zoser, barely seven years old, was blissfully ignorant of the viper coiling in her chest.

It was during this turbulent epoch that the Sultanate of Ifat, a formidable Muslim power then flourishing in the Horn of Africa – known in Arabic chronicles as Wafāt or Awfāt, and by some as the Kingdom of Zeila – whose devotion lay with the burgeoning tenets of Islam, had violently seized my father, Ika. A proud Ethiopian warrior, his very essence interwoven with the ancient Solomonic lineage, he was consumed by the brutal conflicts ignited by what we perceived as their encroaching faith – a belief system emanating from beyond our borders, threatening to erode the bedrock of our ancestral traditions. Now, the same inexorable destiny that claimed him seemed poised to snatch my mother, casting a long, ominous shadow over the remnants of our fractured existence.

My mother, however, walked a more intricate path of faith. While she embraced aspects of Christianity, her spirit remained deeply rooted in the pre-Abrahamic beliefs of our ancestors, seeing in them a profound connection to the very essence of existence. She honored not only the Christian God but also the Sky God, Mahrem, a prominent deity of the heavens, and paid reverence to the primal power of fire cults. The subtle whispers of serpent gods, often linked to fertility and healing in ancient lore, found a place in her reverence, as did the nuanced practices of spirit rituals. Even the solemn echoes of human sacrifices, a relic from a time before memory, were acknowledged within her expansive understanding of the sacred.

It was within these circles, where the ancient ways still held sway, that my mother had been entrusted with sacred knowledge. She spoke of "the fragment," a concept she believed pulsed with its own potent magic. Yet, as her health faltered, a new kind of desperation took root. The pragmatic hope of earthly remedies began to wane, and her gaze turned increasingly toward the Christian God, seeking in Him a different kind of salvation, a final reprieve from the inevitable.

"The Zagwe churches," she had murmured, her voice a parched whisper against the silence. "The rock-hewn sanctuaries… a journey… a chance for grace." Whispers of the Christian faith had reached our ears, accounts of miraculous cures within the monolithic churches hewn from the very heart of the Lasta Mountains. In her desperation, Amara grasped at this unfamiliar hope, not for her own sake, I now understand, but for mine.

Our journey from Aksum was brutally etched onto my young soul. Over a hundred miles the ochre earth stretched, broken by jagged emerald peaks that clawed at the endless azure sky. The air, thin and sharp, offered no respite from the sun's relentless gaze. For Amara, each step was a monumental battle. Her hand, usually firm in mine, trembled violently, a furnace raging beneath her skin. Sweat beaded on her brow despite the chill wind that whipped through the highlands. "Just a rest, Zoser, my lion," she'd gasp, pressing the back of her hand to her forehead, her eyes glazed with a feverish light that I, in my naive innocence, mistook for weariness.

The scent of frankincense, carried on the dry, dusty wind, grew stronger, mingling with the metallic tang of Amara's sweat. She would squint, her gaze fixed on the distant, jagged peaks. "Almost there," she'd murmur, a flicker of desperate hope in her eyes. "The monks… they will help."

Then, Lalibela rose before us, a vision ripped from another world. Unlike the familiar towering stelae of Aksum, these were not built, but born from the earth itself. Massive, intricate churches plunged downward, as if giants had carved them from the living rock. They stood as silent testaments to a faith I did not understand, yet I felt their immense power.

But Amara could go no further. Just shy of the first monolithic wonder, she crumpled. One moment she was beside me, her breath ragged; the next she was a still, frail form against the unforgiving rock. My small world shattered. "Mama?" I cried, my voice a thin wail against the vast silence. Why wouldn't she get up?

Other pilgrims, their faces etched with the hardships of their own journeys, gathered around us. Their hushed murmurs and worried glances finally pierced my childish obliviousness. This was not just tiredness. This was… something terrible. Their efforts to revive her were futile. I watched, tears streaming down my face, as strong arms, weathered by sun and hardship, gently lifted her lifeless body. They carried her toward the looming darkness of Bet Maryam, the House of Mary, its rectangular entrance a gaping maw in the rock face. I stumbled behind them, my small hand reaching for hers, finding only cold stillness.

Monks, draped in solemn black shamma robes, their faces serene yet grave, met us at the entrance. The flickering light of beeswax candles within painted dancing shadows on ancient frescoes of solemn-eyed saints as they carried Amara's body deeper into the cool, stone embrace. A strange, mournful chanting filled the air, a ritual I didn't comprehend, culminating in the acrid smell of smoke as they performed the cremation within those hallowed walls.

"Zion will shelter you," Abba Talka said, but the words felt hollow, cold as the stone walls around me. I was an orphan, swallowed by the silence of the sacred halls, a shadow moving through the rituals, the ember of my mother's fierce courage the only warmth within my chest.

The monks spoke in hushed tones about ancient mysteries, their brows furrowed in contemplation. "The old ways," one murmured, tracing symbols in the air. "So much lost to time."

I remained silent, feeling the weight of secrets that resonated deep within me, a language my heart understood even if my mind could not decipher. The priests and monks hoped I might, in time, unlock these mysteries, might shed light on the echoes of our forgotten past. But for now, I was left only with the memory of the woman who had faced death with the fierce love of a lioness, a love that now burned within me, a quiet promise waiting for its moment to ignite.

# That first night

My life became a tapestry woven with the somber threads of loss and the vibrant gold of faith. Lalibela, with its silent stone sentinels, was now my home, and the monks had become my family. The echoes of Amara’s laughter faded, replaced by the rhythmic chanting of prayers and the gentle rustle of shamma robes.

The monks taught me their ways, patiently guiding me through the labyrinthine corridors of doctrine, the elegant dance of languages, and the profound power contained within written words. I absorbed their teachings like parched earth absorbs rain, excelling in my studies, my eager mind a sponge for knowledge. By the age of sixteen, I was considered among the brightest of our order—a testament to their dedication and my relentless thirst for understanding.

Then, in the year 1324, a ripple disturbed the quiet waters of our existence. Visitors arrived—strange men with dark hair and skin pale as the desert moon. They spoke of a Roman church, ancient yet not as old as ours, and shared beliefs that resonated with our doctrines. They called themselves Catholics and missionaries. Their journey, spanning vast oceans and connecting distant worlds, had led them to Ethiopia. But our harsh climate proved fatal for many; their lives were extinguished mere weeks after their arrival.

However brief their presence, it bore significant fruit. They had reached Emperor Amda Seyon I, the Lion of Judah and ruler of Ethiopia. Intrigued by their message, the emperor commanded the rock-hewn churches of Lalibela to send forth their brightest young minds to this distant land. Thus, I, Zoser, was chosen to lead an expedition to the Vatican—a name that hummed with foreign mystery.

The emperor’s decision was not solely motivated by religious curiosity. Whispers had reached his ears of Mansa Musa, the ruler of the Mali Empire, whose immense wealth dwarfed even the richest of kings. Musa’s extravagant pilgrimage to Mecca in 1324 had sent ripples across the known world, showcasing his unparalleled power and influence. The Sultanate of Ifat, emboldened by Musa’s display of wealth and religious fervor, had grown even more troublesome for the emperor. He saw in the Vatican a potential ally—a strategic alliance forged from shared faith, a way to counterbalance Ifat’s rising influence.

And so, I, Zoser—a young monk and orphan of Lalibela—was thrust from the sanctuary of my stone-walled world into a wider one: a world of emperors and popes, ancient faiths and political intrigue. I was about to embark on a journey destined to alter the course of my life—and perhaps, the fate of my people.

I still vividly recall how whispers spread like wildfire through the highlands about men with skin pale as the desert moon, their dark hair slick with sweat, their tongues shaping words both strange and familiar. They spoke of God, Christ, and a faith mirroring ours, though shaped by distant lands. They called themselves Catholics.

I was studying an ancient Coptic text in the monastery library when the news reached me. The heavy scent of parchment and incense lingered in the air. Hurried footsteps echoed down the hall, and then the door swung open.

“Zoser\!” Priest Abba Talka stood there, chest rising and falling with exertion, eyes lit with urgency.

I laid down my quill. “Abba, what troubles you?”

He stepped closer, voice lowered as if sharing a secret even the walls should not overhear. “The emperor Dawit commands it. The foreigners—the Catholics—have stirred his curiosity. He wishes to send our brightest to their holy city, this Vatican.”

My heart quickened. “And you tell me this because...?”

A slow smile spread across his weathered face. “Because, my son, you have been chosen to join this expedition.”

The words hung heavy between us. “Me?” A journey beyond our known world, to a place shrouded in mystery? My fingers trembled against the parchment.

“But these men,” I said, recalling the pale-skinned visitors who had quickly succumbed to our unforgiving land, “many perished here. How will we fare in their homeland?”

Abba Talka placed a reassuring hand on my shoulder. “You are strong, Zoser, and wise beyond your years. I did not choose lightly. This is a bridge between worlds—a bridge we must cross.”

I exhaled deeply, feeling destiny’s weight settle upon me. “Then I will go.”

The priest nodded with satisfaction. “Prepare yourself. You leave before the next moon. A Qess Priest named Tamrat will be leading the expedition.”

As he departed, silence reclaimed the library, but my thoughts were no longer bound to the texts before me. They had already traveled ahead—to the unknown horizon, to whispers of a distant church, to a journey that would undoubtedly change everything.

\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

# **The Journey Begins**

## **Lalibela, 1325 AD**

Before dawn, I, Zoser Meri, gathered my few belongings—a worn satchel, a waterskin, and a dagger. I was but a young monk, barely past my twentieth year, and this would be my first great journey beyond the familiar stone walls of our sacred city.

At Bet Medhani Alem, I climbed the bell tower one last time. The bronze bells tolled, their deep vibrations shaking the stones beneath my feet. The monks' chants rose like smoke into the morning air, their words offering comfort for the road ahead. Make this journey matter, I prayed. Let me prove worthy of this pilgrimage.

The rock-hewn churches of Lalibela had been my home, my sanctuary. Now they would be but a memory as I embarked on this impossible pilgrimage to Rome—to the Vatican itself. A journey that would take many moons, perhaps even a full year by foot.

Our small company was carefully chosen, though we appeared an unlikely group. Tamrat led the way—a venerable Qess priest of sixty-four years, his beard white as mountain snow and his eyes sharp with decades of wisdom and an unshakeable belief in his own superiority. "North," he declared with the authority of one who believed lesser men existed solely to follow his commands. "Always north until we reach the great sea. Then west. Any fool with eyes can see this is the only sensible route." When Tamrat spoke, men listened not from respect alone, but from the sheer force of his certainty that he was always right. The church had entrusted him with many missions beyond our borders, successes that had only inflated his already considerable opinion of himself.

"I have debated with the finest minds in Constantinople," he would remind anyone within earshot, "and found them all wanting. These desert wanderers and merchant princes we'll encounter? Children playing at theology compared to one who has spent forty years in service to the Almighty."

Angeska, his devoted wife, walked beside him with quiet grace that masked deep wells of patience. Though thirty-four years younger than her husband, she possessed a gift that made her invaluable—a mind for languages that could unlock any tongue. In the markets of Lalibela, I had watched her shift seamlessly from Amharic to Arabic to the strange dialects of distant traders, picking up new words and phrases like flowers from a field. "Languages are keys," she often said when Tamrat wasn't lecturing about his own linguistic prowess, "and every door we encounter will need unlocking." She had learned long ago to let her husband take credit for her translations, nodding demurely when he proclaimed, "Of course, I guided her in the finer points of theological discourse."

Desta, our guardian, followed close behind with the weary expression of a man who had spent months listening to Tamrat's pronouncements. A former soldier with graying temples and countless scars, he carried the weight of protecting Tamrat—whom the church considered too precious to travel unguarded, though Desta muttered privately that the priest's arrogance might prove more dangerous than any bandit. "The emperor sends his regards," he had said dryly before our departure, "and his promise that your safe return matters more than kingdoms." What he didn't say, but I could read in his eyes, was his hope that the journey might humble the old priest.

And there was me—young Zoser Meri, bearer of packs and keeper of supplies, and unwilling audience to Tamrat's constant lectures on my inadequacies. My role was humble: to carry Tamrat's books and writing materials, to tend the fire, to learn by watching and listening, and to endure his corrections on everything from how I tied knots to my understanding of scripture. "A mind like wet clay," he would say of me, "requiring constant shaping by a master's hand."

We had set out in the season of the spring rains, when the mountain paths ran with water and the air carried the promise of new growth. The first month saw us descending through the highlands, our sandals squelching through mud and our robes heavy with moisture. Tamrat complained bitterly about the conditions while simultaneously lecturing us on how a truly devout traveler would embrace such hardships as opportunities for spiritual growth. "I, of course, have walked through far worse in my journeys to the coastal monasteries," he would remind us daily.

By the time we reached the lowlands in our second month, the rains had given way to the scorching heat of early summer. The sun beat down mercilessly as we crossed into the territories of the Afar people, nomads who watched us with curious eyes from their temporary settlements. Tamrat strode among them as if he were visiting subjects, blessing their children without invitation and loudly proclaiming the superiority of our Christian faith.

"These simple people," he announced to an elder who spoke no Amharic, "require guidance from those blessed with true understanding." Angeska translated diplomatically, softening his words, while the Afar elder's eyes grew cold. Only Desta's hand on his sword hilt and a hurried offering of trade goods prevented bloodshed. That night, as we camped beyond their territory, Tamrat blamed their hostility on their spiritual blindness rather than his own behavior.

The third month brought us into contact with the Somali traders who controlled the routes to the Red Sea. Here, Tamrat's linguistic shortcomings became apparent, though he refused to acknowledge them. He would begin conversations in his broken Arabic, growing increasingly frustrated when the traders failed to grasp his theological points. "Clearly," he would mutter to me as Angeska stepped in to salvage the discussions, "their dialect is corrupted by isolation from proper centers of learning."

It was among these traders that we first heard detailed accounts of Mansa Musa's legendary pilgrimage, which had concluded just a year before our own journey began. The stories they told made even Tamrat fall silent with amazement, though he quickly recovered to declare that such earthly wealth was surely a test from God.

"This African king," one elderly Somali merchant told us as we shared his fire, his eyes gleaming with remembered wonder, "traveled with more gold than I have seen in all my years of trade. Sixty thousand in his retinue—soldiers, scholars, servants, slaves—all moving like a golden river across the desert. Each of his bodyguards carried a staff of gold, and even his pack Angeskals were adorned with the precious metal."

The merchant's voice dropped to an awed whisper. "In Cairo, he gave away so much gold that the metal's value have plummeted. The markets still speak of nothing else. Shopkeepers who received his gifts became wealthy men overnight. The Sultan himself was said to be overwhelmed by the gifts Mansa Musa bestowed upon him."

Tamrat sniffed dismissively. "Earthly vanity dressed in religious clothing. A true pilgrimage requires humility, not ostentation."

But the merchant continued, undeterred by Tamrat's judgment. "He built mosques everywhere he stopped, established centers of learning, commissioned scholars to copy manuscripts. In Mecca itself, his generosity was legendary. The Mamluk chroniclers write that his hajj restored faith in Africa's greatness among the Arab princes. Kings from distant lands now send envoys to Mali, hoping to secure trade agreements or military alliances."

As we pressed northward in our fourth month, entering the scorching summer heat of the Sudanese plains, we encountered the aftermath of Mansa Musa's passage everywhere. Villages still spoke of the great king's retinue, some displaying gifts of gold coins or precious fabrics they had received. Arabic traders showed us maps redrawn to include Mali's expanded territories, and we heard stories of new trade routes established in the wake of his pilgrimage.

The heat grew unbearable as we crossed the Nubian desert. Our water ran low, our lips cracked, and even Tamrat's complaints were reduced to hoarse whispers. But his arrogance remained undiminished. When Nubian merchants offered to guide us to water sources, he initially refused, declaring that God would provide for the faithful without need for pagan assistance. Only when I collapsed from heat exhaustion did he grudgingly accept their help, though he made sure to inform them that their salvation lay in embracing the true faith.

The fifth month brought the blessed relief of the Nile, where we encountered Egyptian fellaheen working the riverbank fields during the flooding season. Here, at last, Tamrat found audiences more receptive to his theological pronouncements, though even these patient farmers sometimes exchanged glances when his lectures grew particularly lengthy.

It was in Egypt that we truly began to understand the magnitude of Mansa Musa's impact. The gold markets in Cairo still bore the effects of his visit, with merchants proudly displaying artifacts they claimed were gifts from the great king. Coptic Christians told us how Mansa Musa had respectfully visited their churches, engaging their priests in theological discussions while maintaining his Islamic faith—a diplomatic grace that Tamrat declared unnecessary compromise.

"True faith requires no such accommodations," he proclaimed to a gathering of Egyptian scholars in Alexandria, his words translated by the ever-patient Angeska. "When one possesses absolute truth, one need not bend to accommodate error."

The scholars listened politely, but I saw several exchange glances that suggested they found such certainty more troubling than admirable. One elderly imam, his beard hennaed red with age, asked quietly, "And how does one distinguish between divine truth and human pride, honored priest?"

Tamrat's face flushed at the suggestion that pride might taint his wisdom. "Through decades of study and contemplation, which reveal truth to those worthy of receiving it," he replied stiffly.

By our sixth month, as autumn winds began to cool the Mediterranean coast, we had crossed paths with Berber tribesmen, Bedouin nomads, and Coptic pilgrims, each encounter colored by Tamrat's unwavering belief in his own superiority. The Berbers tolerated his lectures with amused patience, the Bedouins ignored him entirely after his first pronouncements about their spiritual ignorance, and the Coptic pilgrims listened respectfully while privately questioning his humility.

But it was when we encountered stragglers from Mansa Musa's great hajj that our journey truly changed. These were scholars and holy men who had joined the Malian king's retinue for the pilgrimage and were now making their way home through various routes. Their leader, a Timbuktu imam named Al-Hajj Mahmud, possessed a quiet dignity that even Tamrat found difficult to dismiss.

"Your Christ and our Prophet," Al-Hajj Mahmud said to Tamrat in perfect classical Arabic, "are they not both lights from the same divine source, leading their followers toward the same ultimate truth?"

For once, Tamrat seemed uncertain, though he recovered quickly. "Both guide the faithful through darkness," he replied carefully, "though naturally the path of Christ represents the fuller revelation." Even as Angeska translated his words, I could see her softening their edge.

Al-Hajj Mahmud smiled with the patience of one who had debated with kings. "I had the honor of accompanying Mansa Musa to the holy Kaaba," he said. "There, surrounded by pilgrims from every nation under heaven—Christians from Nubia, Jews from Yemen, Muslims from India and Spain—our great king spoke of how God's truth shines through many prisms, each reflecting a portion of the divine light."

The exchange continued for hours around our campfire, with other members of the Malian party contributing their own observations from the hajj. They spoke of theological debates in Mecca's courtyards, of Christian merchants who participated respectfully in Islamic rituals, of Jewish scholars who found common ground with Muslim theologians. Their accounts painted a picture of religious dialogue that existed far beyond Tamrat's rigid certainties.

But the conversation was not destined to remain peaceful. A hardliner among the Malian group, scarred by battles in defense of the faith, grew increasingly agitated by what he saw as dangerous accommodation. "Enough talk," he finally spat, rising to his feet. "The infidel should kneel or bleed."

The mood shifted instantly. Desta's hand moved to his sword, while the other Malians tensed for violence. But it was Angeska who saved us, speaking rapidly in the man's own Mande dialect—words she had learned from listening to the group's earlier conversations. Her linguistic gift, combined with Al-Hajj Mahmud's calming influence, defused the tension before blood could be spilled.

"We didn't survive months in the desert to die over a misunderstanding," she murmured to me as we moved away from the now-dispersing crowd. But I noticed that even this close call failed to shake Tamrat's conviction that his views were divinely inspired and therefore beyond question.

As I shouldered Tamrat's heavy pack and followed our small party into the gathering dusk, I found myself wondering whether our pilgrimage to Rome would humble my master's pride or merely provide him with new examples of his own theological superiority. The road ahead promised more encounters, more tests of faith and diplomacy, and I prayed that Angeska's wisdom and Desta's sword would continue to preserve us from the consequences of Tamrat's unwavering certainty in his own righteousness.

## **The Port of Pilgrims**

That night, beneath the vast, indifferent gaze of an Egyptian sky—its stars like scattered embers—the others slept. But I could not. My thoughts churned like the restless desert wind. Each moment brought us closer to Rome, and with it, a sense of dread I could neither name nor dismiss.

Desta wasn’t asleep, as I had assumed. He sat upright, his eyes sharp in the starlight. “Whatever it is we’re chasing,” he muttered, “it’s going to get us killed before the Vatican even hears our names.”

I didn’t argue. Faith, purpose, duty—these were dangerous things to carry through foreign lands.

“Tomorrow we face the Mediterranean,” I said, wrapping my cloak tighter around me. “May God calm its wrath long enough for us to cross.”

Desta laughed bitterly. “God’s wrath or the sea’s—I’m not sure which I fear more.”

I gazed northward, toward the vast waters we would soon have to brave. The Vatican was still so far away, and we were so small against the enormity of the world. But I could not turn back. I had made an oath, and it bound me tighter than iron.

Tomorrow would bring new trials. But tonight, beneath the same stars that had guided pilgrims for centuries, I allowed myself a moment of quiet resolve.

---

**The Mediterranean's Wrath**

The salt-laden air of Alexandria filled my lungs as we approached the harbor. It was a cacophony of chaos—merchants shouting, sailors cursing, gulls screaming overhead, and the stink of fish and sweat hanging heavy in the salty breeze.

The journey through Egypt had already tested us—bandits in the highlands, fevers in the lowlands, and wary glances wherever we walked. Even here, where Christendom and commerce mingled, we were out of place.

“Christians sail from that pier,” a fishmonger muttered, nodding toward several vessels bearing crude crosses on their sails.

The docks were no less hostile than the roads. Pilgrims, thieves, mercenaries, and traders crowded the wharves. Amid the noise and motion, Desta murmured, “Keep your heads down. Christian sailors can be worse than Muslim raiders.”

“There,” Tamrat said, with the confident air of a man used to being obeyed. He pointed to a three-masted Venetian trading ship moored at the far end. “They have agreed to take us.”

Tamrat, though a priest of sorts, wore his arrogance like a second robe. He insisted on leading discussions, offering prayers no one asked for, and correcting even our silences. Beside him walked Angeska—his wife—a quiet, sharp-eyed woman with a mind like a blade and a gift for languages unmatched in any monastery.

We approached the vessel—the *Santa Maria*—its hull stained from many voyages, its crew rough and wary. Captain Humbert awaited us at the gangplank, a man seemingly carved from driftwood and salt. His skin was leathery from sun and sea, his beard a tangle of iron threads, and his gaze missed nothing.

“Four passengers to Rome,” Tamrat said, offering a small pouch filled with stones from the mountains of Abyssinia—our last treasures.

The captain snatched the pouch and weighed it in his hand before barking, *"You’ll work for passage. No freeloaders on my ship."*

Tamrat’s lip curled, his aged face tightening with silent contempt. Though he said nothing, the disdain in his sunken eyes was unmistakable—years of pride refusing to bend, even now. He turned away with a slow, deliberate motion, as if the captain were beneath his notice.

I nodded, though dread pooled in my stomach. None of us had ever sailed before, and the sea stretched before us like a great, merciless blade.

Captain Humbert’s eyes narrowed as he looked us over. “Moors,” he muttered, spitting onto the planks. “I was told you were Christians.”

“We *are* Christians,” Tamrat said quickly, my voice tight.

Angeska stepped forward, her voice soft but firm. She translated Tamrat’s words into Latin—halting, perhaps, but enough to be understood. Her eyes stayed locked on the captain’s, unafraid.

“We are Ethiopians,” I added.

“Heretics, then,” the captain growled. He crossed himself dramatically. “There is only one true Church—the Holy Catholic Church of Rome.”

“We worship the same Christ,” I offered, trying to ease the tension.

“Do you?” he sneered. “The agreement was for Christian passengers. I’ll not have heathens aboard my vessel.”

He turned to his first mate, who stood with arms folded and a half-smile curled on his face.

“Unless,” the captain said slowly, “they can prove themselves.”

Then both men laughed—low, strange, and cruel. Not with mirth, but with the kind of amusement wolves might share when the prey stumbles.

# Set Sail

Later that evening, Captain Humbert gave a briefing on the rules, which mostly boiled down to "Don't annoy me" and "Do what I say." He then decided a quick frisking was in order. The subsequent search for weapons was thorough, his gaze lingering with particular suspicion on Desta, who had an impressive arsenal concealed beneath his garments. Once Desta was relieved of his mobile armory, he looked surprisingly deflated.

The Santa Maria finally lumbered into the inky embrace of the sea. I watched as the landscape drifted further and further away until we could no longer see land. I did not need to ponder long on the many and varied ways we might have to "prove ourselves" to our deeply suspicious captain, who was also, surprisingly, a remarkably proficient spitter.

"Tell me, boy," Captain Humbert demanded, and so began the first of many theological interrogations that evening as we huddled on the deck. "Does the Holy Spirit proceed from the Father alone, or from the Father and the Son?"

I noticed Tamrat attempting to make himself inconspicuous behind a coil of rope, his usual pompous demeanor replaced by nervous fidgeting. For once, the great theologian seemed eager to avoid displaying his scholarly prowess.

The Filioque question—the very matter that had split East from West centuries ago. In our Ethiopian tradition, we followed the original Nicene Creed without the addition. But I had studied both positions extensively.

"In the Gospel of John," I answered carefully, "Christ says He will send the Advocate from the Father. In another passage, He says the Father will send the Spirit in His name." I paused. "Both statements are true, though perhaps expressing different aspects of the same divine mystery."

Captain Humbert's eyes widened slightly. He had expected ignorance, not nuance.

"Both statements are true, eh?" Captain Humbert exclaimed in a sadistic manner.

"There are four of you Moors onboard. Let's ask another one." He pointed to Desta, the Ethiopian soldier, while Tamrat tried to shuffle further behind the mast, clearly hoping to avoid the captain's attention.

I noticed the captain's crew of fifteen men had weapons, and some of them were twitching their fingers as if something was about to happen. The situation was getting serious.

"Yes, him," the captain exclaimed, "the one who was carrying more weapons than supplies." His finger pointed at Desta. Perhaps intimidated by Desta's size, the captain ordered his men to pull out the plank.

The crew dragged a long, weathered plank from below deck, its wood groaning as they laid it over the ship's edge. One end jutted out over the abyss of black water below.

"If he answers the next question wrong," Captain Humbert declared, "then he takes a walk."

Desta stood motionless, his dark eyes fixed on the captain. He knew the stakes. From the corner of my eye, I could see Tamrat pressing himself against the ship's wall, his face pale with terror at the thought that he might be called upon next.

The captain cleared his throat, savoring the moment. "Very well, soldier. Here is your question." Humbert continued, his eyes locked on Desta. "Answer this: What is the nature of Christ? Is He divine, human, or both?"

A hush fell over the deck. Desta's brow furrowed in thought as he considered the question. He was a warrior, a man of action—not a theologian. The weight of the moment pressed down on us all. If his answer displeased the captain, the cold embrace of the Mediterranean awaited him. The theological interrogation had taken a deadly turn.

Desta's jaw tightened. He was no scholar, only a man of war. But he would not let them see fear.

The plank lay ominously across the ship's railing, a stark silhouette against the dark water. Desta's silence stretched, each passing moment thick with dread. Finally, he spoke, his voice deep and steady.

"He is both," Desta stated firmly. "Fully divine, the Son of God, and fully human, born of the Virgin Mary. Two natures, one person."

A murmur spread among the crew. The captain's smile twisted into something colder.

"Wrong," he hissed.

The crew moved as one, seizing Desta. He struggled momentarily as they forced him onto the plank. The wood groaned under his weight as he stood at the edge, the sea yawning beneath him.

For a moment, there was silence. Then, without a word, Desta stepped forward—not pushed, but walking of his own will—into the void.

The splash was swallowed by the night.

Captain Humbert turned to the rest of us, his eyes alight with fervor. Tamrat had gone completely white and was now crouched behind a barrel, clearly praying he wouldn't be selected next. "Who's next?"

The crew stood frozen, their eyes locked on the dark waters where Desta had vanished.

"What was the correct answer?" I was brave enough to ask.

"Both," Captain Humbert stated firmly. "Fully divine, the Son of God, and fully human, born of the Virgin Mary. Two natures, one person."

My mouth dropped open. "Isn't that what Desta said?"

Captain Humbert turned slowly, his gaze sweeping over the remaining Ethiopians. "Oops, my bad\! Looks like I made a mistake\!" said the captain with a cruel grin.

Captain Humbert added, "I didn't tell him to jump, did I?"

Tamrat emerged slightly from his hiding spot, his usual arrogance completely absent as he realized the captain's "game" was nothing more than murderous whim disguised as theological inquiry.

# Ship doctrine

The ship groaned and creaked, a living thing battling the fierce will of the sea. Winter had arrived, bringing with it a brutal wind that whipped the waves into violent, towering mountains. The sky, once a gentle blue, had turned a bruised purple, mirroring the churning tempest below. Captain Humbert's lips curled into a predatory grin, his eyes, like the stormy sea, glinting with a dangerous amusement.

"Who shall answer next?" he mused, tapping his chin with a well-manicured finger. His gaze, sharp and unwavering, snapped towards Tamrat, the arrogant Qess priest, whose formidable knowledge of scripture was usually a source of immense pride. "You\!"

Tamrat's usually confident dark complexion turned to stone. He, who prided himself on his theological prowess, was suddenly exposed. His mind, usually a labyrinth of ancient texts and intricate doctrines, felt a hollow echo. The crew, eager for a spectacle, shoved him forward. The plank still jutted over the roiling sea, still damp from Desta's tragic plunge.

The captain, savoring the moment, paced with the deliberate grace of a predator. "Here is your question," he declared, his voice precise and scholarly, a stark contrast to his crooked grin. "It’s an easy one, can you explain the *Communicatio Idiomatum*?"

Tamrat nearly choked on his own tongue. His mind raced, desperate for an answer, any answer, that wasn't "Is that the new spice from Calicut?" The crew snickered, and the sound was a cruel whip across Tamrat's pride. Angeska, his wife, who usually stood by him with quiet strength, averted her gaze, her own face etched with a mixture of fear and shame for her husband's humiliation.

Before Humbert could revel further, Zoser stepped forward, his voice calm but firm against the howling wind. "Captain," he said, "allow me to answer in his stead."

Humbert raised an eyebrow, a hint of surprise in his eyes. "Oh? And why should I?"

"Because," Zoser replied, his gaze unwavering, "you seek true debate, not the slaughter of ignorant men. And I know the answer."

The captain studied him, then chuckled, a sound swallowed by the wind. "Very well. But if you are wrong, you both go over."

Zoser nodded, a silent acceptance of the grim terms. He closed his eyes for a moment, as if in prayer, then spoke, his voice clear and resonant over the din of the storm. "It's the communication of properties," Zoser began, "by which each nature of Christ—divine and human—shares its attributes reciprocally in the one person."

A hush fell over the deck, an unnatural quiet amidst the raging tempest. Even the rough crew, who cared little for theology, sensed the gravity and profound understanding in Zoser's answer.

Captain Humbert's smile faded, replaced by a flicker of grudging respect. He had expected blind guessing, not a measured, learned response delivered with such conviction. For a long moment, he said nothing, his gaze fixed on Zoser. Then, with a dismissive wave, he spoke, "A clever dodge. But you are correct, I guess you are a Christian." He turned to Tamrat, who sagged in relief. "You live… for now."

Tamrat, his arrogance momentarily deflated, could only nod, a silent acknowledgment of his reprieve. Zoser, however, did not relax. The captain’s eyes gleamed with a new hunger—not just for blood, but for a true battle of wits. The storm raged on, mirroring the turbulent emotions on the ship.

# Landfall – An Unsettling Farewell

The weeks that followed settled into a strange, uneasy rhythm. After Zoser’s profound answer, Captain Humbert seemed almost… pleased. The theological tests ceased, though his eyes still lingered on the Ethiopians with an unsettling curiosity. Instead, he put them to work—coiling ropes, scrubbing decks, mending sails. Hard labor, but not unjust, as the ship battled the increasingly rough and violent winter seas.

By the time the ship finally sighted the rocky shores of Italy, the tension had dulled into wary coexistence. The captain kept their weapons, allowing them only their meager belongings. Tamrat’s pouch of precious stones remained hidden, and Angeska clutched the few salvaged items they possessed.

As the gangplank was lowered onto the bustling dock of Naples, Humbert approached them, his usual sneer replaced with something almost resembling respect.

“You’re free to go,” he said, arms crossed. “But the roads here are dangerous. Bandits, mercenaries… men who make me look merciful.” He smirked. “I’ll be heading north myself. You’re welcome to walk with me.”

The Ethiopians exchanged glances. The idea of spending another moment in this man’s company was unthinkable. Tamrat, his arrogance slowly returning, found his voice.

“No,” Tamrat said flatly, shaking his head, a gesture of defiance that surprised even Angeska. The others remained silent, their eyes fixed on the ground.

Humbert sighed, then did something unexpected—he bowed his head slightly. “What happened to Desta… it was the way of the sea. A test of faith. But I bear you no ill will.” He reached into his coat and pulled out a small, crudely drawn map. “If you ever need passage again, ask for me in Genoa. I’ll give you fair rates.”

Zoser took the map without a word, his fingers tightening around the parchment. The offer was grotesque. The man had murdered their friend and now acted as though they might someday be clients.

Without another glance, the Ethiopians turned and walked away, their steps quickening as the distance grew between them and the ship. The sounds of the port—shouting merchants, creaking carts, the cries of gulls—filled the air, but none of them spoke. Not yet.

They needed time. Time to bury Desta in their hearts. Time to forget the sound of the plank groaning under his weight. Time to decide if they would ever step foot on a ship again.

Behind them, Captain Humbert watched them go, his expression unreadable. Then, with a shrug, he turned back to his crew and barked orders to unload the cargo.

# Landfall

We stepped onto Italian soil as the sun set, casting long shadows across the harbor. Ahead lay the final leg of our journey—the road to Rome and the Vatican beyond.

"The sea tried to devour us," Tamrat observed, a hint of his old arrogance returning, though his experience on the ship had humbled him, if only slightly. "Yet here we stand."

"The land may prove no kinder," Zoser warned. "Especially with plague stalking these shores."

I gazed toward the north, where Rome awaited. "We've survived storms and treachery, thirst and superstition. Whatever trials await us on the road to Rome, we will face them together."

Angeska adjusted her headscarf. Her eyes, filled with a quiet resolve, met mine. "The journey tests each of us differently," she said softly. "The sea tested our vows. And one of us died. Rome will test our souls, we can hope and pray we survive."

As night fell, we set our faces toward the Eternal City. Behind us, the Mediterranean stretched dark and vast, a trial we had somehow survived.

Ahead lay Rome, the Vatican, and the culmination of our impossible pilgrimage. What new dangers awaited us there, I could not say—but the worst of the journey, surely, was behind us.

Or so I believed.

# **The Pilgrimage**

As we entered the southern territories of Italy—passing through Benevento, Capua, and Cassino—we were greeted not with warmth, but with a winter unlike any we had ever known. The bitter cold snapped through our layers, and frost clung to every stone and branch like icy fingers. Even snow, rare though it was, dusted the distant mountains and made the roads treacherous.

Local townsfolk stared as we passed, their gazes sharp with confusion and caution. Our dark skin, our strange manners, the scent of incense that seemed to cling to our robes—it all marked us as other. Whispers followed us. “Are they Saracens?” some would ask behind cupped hands. Others, bolder and more venomous, would confront us directly: “Are you… heretics?”

That word—*heretic*—hung in the air like a funeral veil. In Ethiopia, we knew the term, of course, but it lacked the blood-chilling menace it carried here. In Italy, heresy wasn’t just a theological disagreement—it was a spiritual contagion. It turned men into monsters in the eyes of the Church and justified cruelty in the name of righteousness. I had heard the stories: the inquisitions, the pyres, the screams. Here, difference was dangerous.

My mission, once so simple in my mind, began to shift in clarity—and in weight. I had not come merely to find unity with the Church of Rome, nor to argue over doctrine. No, I was here to stand as a living witness of Ethiopia’s ancient Christianity—to bear the truth of our tradition even as it was misunderstood, feared, or rejected. But how could I speak of our unique Christology, our separate canon, and our apostolic succession without inviting judgment? Would they listen—or light a match?

At night, under foreign stars and the scent of olive groves instead of the comforting perfume of frankincense, I lay awake. The weight of what we carried—not a relic, but a truth—pressed heavily on my soul.

Tamrat, our leader and my once-revered mentor, had begun to recover his arrogance after the deep humiliation he suffered earlier in our travels. His voice grew louder, his complaints more pointed. “Zoser,” he would often declare, puffing his chest, “faith is our shield and our guide. Even Rome cannot dim our truth.”

But I had come to dread his outbursts. His pride too often got the better of him, leading us into unnecessary conflicts. He mistook stubbornness for conviction, and recklessness for courage. It was Angeska—his wife and our interpreter—who quietly steered us away from danger. Fluent in tongues and wise beyond her years, she softened every exchange, smoothed every insult, and translated with just enough ambiguity to save us from Tamrat’s worst blunders.

“Selam lehulachihu,” she would say gently in each village—peace be upon you all. Her voice was balm; her presence, our lifeline.

By the time we reached the outskirts of Rome, we were half-starved, flea-bitten, and stinking of the Tiber’s filth—but at least we were alive to tell the tale.

We arrived at the gates of the Holy City after more wrong turns than a blind beggar in a labyrinth. Every alley led us astray. Locals either ignored us, spat at us, crossed themselves as if warding off demons, or offered directions so convoluted we ended up worse off than before. When we finally stumbled into Rome, we looked less like pilgrims and more like vagrants: caked in dust, sweat-soaked, and staggering with exhaustion.

Food was a torment. Market stalls reeked with rot. Every loaf of bread was either rock-hard or moldy. One merchant sold us a lump of cheese greener than spring grass; it tasted like the inside of a boot. Another baker sold us loaves so hard they could have shattered our teeth, and when Tamrat tried to complain, the man shouted for the guards.

Lodging was no better. Every innkeeper took one look at our faces and slammed the door or quoted us prices fit for royalty. No silver? No room.

In the end, we slept on the banks of the Tiber. Its foul breath enveloped us—sewage and rotting fish mixing with the cold to form a choking haze. Rats scurried over us as if we were part of the refuse. One bold creature even paused on Tamrat’s chest, sniffed his beard, and scurried off with a crumb.

It was a misery unmatched. But we endured it.

We had come too far to be undone by hunger or humiliation. And despite the shame and squalor, I still believed—somewhere deep beneath the cold and filth—that we had a purpose in this city of marble and shadows.

# 

#  Cardinal Giovanni

Our grand purpose—to seek an audience with the Pope—seemed more impossible with each passing day. Even finding a priest who wouldn’t brand us as heretics was a trial. One cleric took a single glance at our Ethiopian companions, made the sign of the cross, and hurried away as if we carried the Plague. Another demanded a bribe just to hear our plea, then vanished with our coins, laughing.


We found ourselves by the banks of the Tiber, near the quaint yet vibrant church of San Francesco a Ripa, nestled in the bustling heart of Trastevere, where Rome’s ancient heartbeat pulsed most authentically. Here, amidst the mingling aromas of fresh bread and distant incense, Angeska entered alone, hopeful of securing a sympathetic ear.

Then, astonishingly, a miracle—or so we believed. Cardinal Giovanni himself agreed to meet us. Providence, I thought, divine intervention finally rewarding our persistent faith. Only later did I realize we had willingly walked into an elaborate, meticulously woven trap.

The cardinal received us enthroned, unmoving as a marble statue upon his ornate chair. He regarded us with the distant disdain of royalty tolerating beggars at his gates. His robes glistened immaculately, rings glittering coldly as we stood before him, filthy, ragged, bearing the grime and shame of the city streets. Our humble bows met only a frigid, imperious gaze, cutting through us as sharply as polished steel.

Beside me, Tamrat shifted restlessly, his fingers drumming irritably on his thigh. His eyes burned with barely-contained rage as he muttered loudly in Amharic, “Three hours and no water, no wine, not even a piece of stale bread? Is this how Romans greet honored guests, with barbaric neglect?”

Angeska turned pale, hastily crafting Tamrat’s indignation into polished Latin, “We are deeply grateful for your generous hospitality, Your Eminence.”

I groaned internally, desperate for Tamrat to notice her diplomatic finesse. But he was unstoppable, his indignation fueling further offense. "Inform this arrogant man that when his ancestors were sacrificing goats, our church was already ancient\!"

Angeska’s eyes widened nervously as she translated, "Our Ethiopian Church, Your Eminence, possesses ancient traditions that we humbly hope Rome might recognize."

Cardinal Giovanni leaned forward slightly, intrigued yet cautious. "Indeed? Ancient, you say? What exactly do these traditions entail?"

Tamrat visibly relaxed, mistakenly interpreting the Cardinal’s curiosity as genuine openness. With enthusiastic gestures, he explained, "Tell him of our sacred rituals under the moonlight, our priests married and leading lives among the people, not hidden away like hermits\!"

Angeska hesitated, clearing her throat softly before offering a careful translation. "We have some unique customs, Your Eminence. Our clergy marry and actively engage with the community, unlike the Roman tradition of celibate priesthood."

Giovanni nodded thoughtfully. "Fascinating. Married priests... and rituals under moonlight? How very... novel. Are there any other distinctive customs?"

Encouraged by this seeming interest, Tamrat excitedly continued, oblivious to the growing tension. "Yes\! We revere sacred scriptures that Rome discards. Scriptures holding profound truths—texts like the Book of Enoch. Tell him, Angeska\!"

Angeska visibly tensed, swallowing nervously before translating delicately, "We also respect additional texts like the Book of Enoch, texts which have historically been debated in your tradition."

The Cardinal’s eyes narrowed subtly, his interest shifting to cautious skepticism. "The Book of Enoch, you say? Curious choice. And tell me, how do you address God in your liturgy?"

Tamrat grinned confidently, entirely misunderstanding the delicate ground on which we stood. "We speak of God in the plural form, of course. The heavens are vast, the divine mysteries many—surely the Cardinal knows this?"

Angeska’s translation was barely a whisper, "We sometimes refer to God in a plural sense, reflecting the divine mystery's breadth."

The Cardinal paused, his face a careful mask. "Plural gods? Or simply a plural form?"

Tamrat laughed heartily, unaware of the precarious misunderstanding forming. "God is infinite, is he not? His manifestations are many; therefore, our language honors this divine abundance."

Angeska, nearly trembling, offered softly, "He refers metaphorically, Your Eminence, emphasizing the vastness and mystery of God."

The Cardinal’s eyes began to glitter dangerously, suspicion sharpening his gaze. He leaned even closer, voice deceptively calm, yet edged with tension. "Translator, I sense you may be... moderating your companion's words. Are you certain you translate faithfully?"

Angeska stammered, visibly distressed, "I swear to you, Your Eminence, my translations have been truthful."

Tamrat, oblivious to the rising alarm, shrugged cheerfully. "What is the matter? Surely he sees our point—Rome should rejoice in the richness of divine expression we offer. Tell him our priests celebrate openly beneath the stars, married and blessed by scriptures that speak truth rejected by narrow minds."

Angeska sighed heavily, carefully translating, but her voice betrayed rising panic. "He emphasizes our desire to share openly our traditions, including our married clergy and unique scriptures."

Giovanni abruptly stood, his demeanor shifting dramatically, eyes blazing with sudden clarity and indignation. "Unique scriptures indeed\! And exactly how many books does your Bible contain, Ethiopian?"

Pride surged through Tamrat as he boldly declared, oblivious to the storm gathering before us. "Eighty-eight sacred books, each divinely inspired."

"Eighty-eight?" the Cardinal sneered, triumphant in his disbelief. "Rome acknowledges only sixty-six. Tell me, what strange texts have you retained that Rome wisely discarded?"

"The Book of Enoch, almost others" Tamrat announced defiantly, oblivious to his peril.

The cardinal surged to his feet, his voice booming with wrath. "Enoch\! You openly espouse condemned heresies within my very chambers?"

Angeska's translation faltered, her voice trembling.

Tamrat, heedless, scoffed loudly, "Rejected by fools, but embraced by those seeking true wisdom\!

Angeska did not translate those words, her eyes said it all.

Tamrat finally sensed the mood shift, his confident smile faltering into shock. Our fragile moment of dialogue had unraveled completely, and the Cardinal’s wrathful condemnation loomed unmistakably over us.

The cardinal thundered his verdict, "Blasphemy and heresy\! Your pagan scriptures, your defiance of Rome’s sacred doctrines, and your arrogant insults demand swift justice\! Guards—seize these heretics\! Prepare the stakes\!"

Angeska collapsed, sobbing and pleading at his feet, her desperate cries unheeded. "Mercy\! This is unjust\!"

"Justice?" the cardinal sneered coldly. "Justice is whatever the Church decrees."

Rough hands seized us, chains rattling ominously. Tamrat shouted helplessly, dignity shattered, scholarly pride crushed beneath stark terror. "I was misunderstood\! The moonlight was symbolic, dancing metaphorical\!"

But it was too late. Angeska’s translations drowned uselessly in the chaotic shuffle of our impending doom. The cardinal’s mocking laughter pursued us into the chill of a shadowy courtyard prepared grimly for judgment.

Dragged through dim corridors to a desolate dungeon, we found ourselves shackled in the stony gloom, far from warmth or kindness. Tamrat furiously, his gaunt face pale and haunted, muttering angrily, "No bread, no wine, not even water—housed like beggars, treated like criminals, while they feast above\! Is this the respect Rome shows Ethiopia’s sacred Church?" His voice echoed bitterly off the ancient stones, mingling sorrow with fury, as our great mission spiraled tragically toward flame and ruin.

# rumours

I understood now why the Romans treated us with such disdain. Rumors had reached them of Ethiopia's growing connections with other eastern churches, of our refusal to fully submit to papal authority. To them, we weren't guests – we were representatives of a church they considered misguided at best, heretical at worst.

my hands trembled. Tamrat begun questioning his wife Angeska translations, suggesting she had softened his words too much, made him seem weak before the Cardinal.

"Your Grace, I translated faithfully," I protested, but my voice wavered. "My grandfather was a merchant from Axum, he traded goods here, in Rome." Tamrat's hunger and humiliation had turned him paranoid. Outside our window, I could hear distant sounds of revelry from the main palace – the Romans feasting while we starved.

"Tomorrow," Tamrat said, his voice tight with controlled fury, "I want every word translated exactly as I speak it. No softening, no diplomatic niceties. Let these arrogant Romans hear Ethiopia's true voice." He turned to me, shadows making his gaunt face skull-like. "Unless your loyalties lie elsewhere?"

I bowed my head, knowing the coming days would force me to choose between truth and survival. The rift between East and West was becoming a chasm, and I was standing at its edge.

"And Zoser Meri," he added as I turned to leave, "remember who brought you here. Remember who can guarantee your safe return to Lalibela... or ensure you never see it again."

I slipped into the cold corridor, my breath coming in short gasps. Tamrat's threat was clear.

The next morning's audience descended into chaos. Tamrat, haggard from another night without proper food or sleep, stormed into the Cardinal's chamber unannounced.

"You starve Ethiopia's representatives while you feast?" he shouted. As I translated his words exactly as ordered, I saw the Cardinal's face contort with rage.

"You dare burst in here like barbarians?" Giovanni thundered back. "This is Rome, not your African wilderness\!"

The accusations flew like arrows: Cardinal Giovanni condemned the Ethiopian use of drums in sacred services as pagan. Tamrat called the Romans corrupt for their wealth and political entanglements. Each translation I gave fueled their fury further.

"Your heretical practices\!" Cardinal Giovanni roared, spittle flying from his beard. "You dare maintain customs that Rome has long declared improper?"

Tamrat, swaying from exhaustion, jabbed a bony finger at the Cardinal. "Our traditions date back to the Apostles themselves\! Before your church was corrupted by power and gold\!"

I translated their words mechanically now, watching theological debate descend into madness. The Cardinal condemned Ethiopian priests for their marriage customs, calling it an affront to holy celibacy. Tamrat mockingly questioned the moral behavior of supposedly celibate Roman clergy.

"And your practice of rebaptism?" Francesco sneered. "As if the first baptism were insufficient?"

"Better than your indulgences," Tamrat shot back. "Selling salvation like merchants in a bazaar\! And your Latin Mass – dead words for a dead faith\!"

"At least we don't mix pagan rituals with Christian worship," Francesco spat, referring to elements of Ethiopian liturgy that incorporated older traditions.

The debate spiraled through calendar disputes, the proper way to conduct the liturgy, whether to kneel or stand in prayer. Each point of doctrine became a weapon, each difference a fresh wound in Christianity's unity.

I noticed blood on the marble floor – Tamrat's nose had started bleeding, probably from rage.

Then Tamrat's fury suddenly turned on me. "You\!" he snarled. "Your translations mock Ethiopia's authority. Like all Romans, you twist our words\!"

"I am no Roman, Your Grace\!" I countered, though my voice wavered under the weight of his rage. "I was born in Holy Lalibela itself. Yes, I bear the dark skin of Ethiopia, a mark of my ancient ancestry. But that speaks to my bloodline, not my loyalty."

His lip curled in disdain as he stepped closer, the heavy folds of his ornate robes brushing the floor like an omen. "And what of this accursed knowledge?" he spat, his voice dripping with contempt. "These ancient texts you study—do you deny they were condemned as heretical by our own church fathers?"

My chest tightened, and for a fleeting moment, words failed me. "I seek only understanding," I managed, my voice barely above a whisper. "To harmonize the wisdom of the ancients with the teachings of the Church. Nothing more."

The room seemed to shrink, suffocating in its silence. My breath was shallow, my heart pounding like a prisoner at the gates of judgment.

Then Tamrat spoke, his words a blade sheathed in ice, cutting deeper than any shout. "The truth you seek is forbidden\!" his voice low and cold. "Because the truth you seek is not meant for mortal minds."

His gaze bore into mine, a chasm of anger and disdain. My hands trembled as I stood rooted in the shadows of his wrath, the weight of centuries of dogma pressing down on my shoulders like chains.

I stood frozen, for a moment, the room was silent. Then Tamrat spoke,

The air thickened, oppressive and suffocating, as if the weight of the heavens had descended into the chamber. Shadows flickered wildly on the walls, the candlelight sputtering as though recoiling from the force now rising before me. The man stood slowly, deliberately, his movements carrying an unspoken authority that froze me in place. As he adjusted the veil draped across his face, it slipped slightly, revealing eyes that glowed like molten gold—pulsing with an unnatural, terrifying brilliance.

"Do you even comprehend what you've uncovered, Zoser?" he intoned, his voice reverberating with an otherworldly resonance that seemed to rattle the bones in my chest. "This is no mere history you've stumbled upon. No ancient secret to be decoded for scholarly acclaim. This... this is a key—a thread that, if pulled, unravels the very tapestry of existence itself."

"I don't understand," I stammered, my voice trembling under the weight of his revelation. My heart thundered in my chest, my instincts screaming at me to flee, though my legs refused to move.

"You will," he hissed, stepping forward with the grace of a stalking predator. His skeletal fingers, tipped with nails as sharp as daggers, extended toward me as though to pierce the air itself. "Zoser, you have been excommunicated."

The words hit like thunder, shaking the very foundation of the room. The veil lifted further, exposing a gaunt face etched with lines of age and something far older—symbols and scars that seemed alive, writhing in the dim light. His voice darkened, becoming almost serpentine, laced with venom and unrelenting certainty.

"The ancient scholars of Axum were not merely priests and monks. They were wardens of the primal forces, wielders of a power so ancient it predates the stars themselves. They did not teach with parables but with words—fragments of a language forged before time, words capable of rewriting the laws of reality. Their tongues could bend light, summon storms, and tear the veil between worlds."

His eyes burned brighter, illuminating his face in ghastly hues. "That is why their names were erased, their manuscripts burned, their truths buried. To even speak their forgotten words is to unchain chaos itself. And you, Zoser..." He pointed at me, his voice rising to an unholy crescendo, "You dared to uncover them\! You tread where mortal souls were forbidden to walk\!"

The room seemed to twist around us, the walls groaning as if they too had become sentient witnesses to my condemnation. "By invoking what should remain buried," he continued, his voice splitting into echoes that layered over themselves, "you have committed a sin not against man, but against existence. For that, you are cast out—not from the Church, but from the very order of creation."

The last words tore through the air like a blade, and the world seemed to tremble in their wake. I staggered backward, the gravity of his words crushing me as the dim light finally guttered into darkness.

The weight of the relic, heavy fragment, with the secrets of ages past, pressed against my thigh. I slid my hand into it, my fingers tracing the unfamiliar shape of the reliefwithin. As I withdrew it, the dim light of the monastic chamber illuminated the enigmatic symbol of the Axumite cross, a chilling reminder of the dark knowledge it held.

Tamrat's voice, a low rumble, broke the silence. "It speaks of the forbidden text, Zoser. A manuscript so cursed, its words warp reality itself."

I stumbled backward, the parchment slipping from my grasp. "You're lying," I protested, my voice barely a whisper.

A shiver ran down my spine as I stared at the parchment, its cryptic symbols now imbued with a sinister power. The line between history and horror blurred, and I was drawn into a vortex of fear and fascination. The forbidden text, a tome of unspeakable power. The veiled man smiled, a cruel curve of his lips. "Is it? Or is it the beginning of something far greater—and far more terrifying?"

Before I could respond, the candles flickered violently, the air growing colder still. Shadows twisted and writhed along the walls, and a low, guttural whisper filled the hall. I turned, my heart pounding, as the scroll in my hands began to pulse with an unnatural warmth.

"You have brought it upon yourself," the veiled man hissed, his voice a chorus of whispers. "The truth you seek will not set you free—it will consume you."

The last thing I saw before the candles extinguished was the scroll's ink shifting, the words rearranging themselves into shapes I couldn't comprehend. And then, darkness.

# **The Fragment Bearer**

A sickly torch sputtered in the iron bracket overhead, its flame coughing as though suffocating in the stench of piss, rot, and the despair of forgotten men. The floor we sat on was slick with centuries of misery. The stones we leaned against felt more like ribs inside some ancient, dying beast.

Tamrat had been pacing like a man possessed for hours. Then, without warning, he stopped. He stared at his wife. And started to cry.

Not a dignified weeping, but broken sobs—snot dribbling, chest heaving, the gasping, childlike sorrow of a man who had spent years in denial and suddenly met truth face first. Angeska’s brow furrowed. She leaned forward.

“Tamrat...?” Her voice was low, careful.

“I’m sorry,” he whispered.

A beat of silence. Then again, louder this time. “I’m so sorry. I’m sorry, Angeska... I’m sorry... for everything.”

She moved toward him slowly. “Why are you apologising like this? What did you do?”

He collapsed to his knees in front of her. “Please. Please forgive me.”

She recoiled, fear creeping into her voice. “Is there... another woman?”

He shook his head violently. “No. No, never. It’s not that.”

Her eyes narrowed. “Then what is it, Tamrat? Tell me. Are there children? Somewhere?”

He opened his mouth but couldn’t find the words. He was coughing now, gagging between sobs. He pressed a hand to his stomach and began retching.

“Tamrat, what is *wrong* with you?”

“I thought it would make me strong,” he rasped between dry heaves. “I thought I could protect us all if I just—if I just—”

He leaned forward, and vomited.

We recoiled in horror. Something clattered to the stone floor with a *wet, solid thunk*. It wasn’t food. It wasn’t blood.

It was *wood.*

A small, dark fragment—drenched in mucus and bile, slick with sour-smelling stomach acid. It pulsed faintly in the flickering torchlight.

Zoser—*me*—froze. I couldn’t move. Couldn’t breathe.

Tamrat looked up at me like a dying Angeskal offering its cursed treasure to its hunter. “Take it. Take it... and don’t let it be found again.”

“What... what is this?” I whispered.

Angeska backed away from it, eyes wide. “Tamrat... what did you DO?”

“I stole it,” he whispered, face pale as ash. “Back in Axum. I used my position—my access to the sanctum. I saw it sitting there... a fragment, small, forgotten. No one would notice. I thought, ‘This is God’s way of choosing me.’ So I took it.”

“You *stole* a piece of the Ark?” Angeska’s voice cracked like glass.

He nodded, then grimaced. “I swallowed it to hide it. Over and over again. Every time it passed through me, I put it back inside. I thought—if it stayed in me—it would make me holy. Untouchable.”

He started sobbing again. “But it only brought ruin. Every step of our journey since has been cursed. Desta’s death. The failed audiences. The dungeon. The death sentence. All of it... my fault.”

I stared at the glistening relic. The smell made my eyes water. I didn’t want to touch it. Every nerve in my body screamed to run from it.

But he shoved it toward me with trembling hands. “It’s not safe here. *You* must take it back. To Lalibela. To the monks. To the Church.”

“No. Give it to your wife,” I said, trembling. “She should carry it.”

“No one would believe her,” Tamrat said. “They would say she bewitched me. They would burn her *too*. But you, Zoser... you’re a scribe, a monk, a pilgrim. You speak truth. *They might just listen to you.*”

I took the relic, gagging at its touch. It was warm. *Too* warm. And it *throbbed*, as if it still lived.

“What is it really?” I asked.

“It’s from Sinai. From before even Moses. Some say it was touched by the fire Himself. If it falls into the wrong hands...”

His voice trailed off as he slumped back, drained.

Angeska knelt beside him, torn between fury and compassion. “Why didn’t you *tell* me?”

“I was ashamed,” he murmured. “And afraid... that if you knew... you’d stop loving me.”

She turned away. “You may have already ensured that.”

Just then, the boots returned. The guards. Cold steel eyes and crimson sleeves.

“The priest,” one grunted. “He burns at dawn.”

Tamrat stood. Weak, but tall. His eyes locked onto mine.

“Zoser,” he whispered. “I know how to save you both.”

The guard sneered. “Too late for bargains.”

But Tamrat, his mouth bloody, spat, “Then let me die with a confession. Let me reveal something... divine.”

They hesitated. Then the guard said, “Fine. We’ll let the executioner know you wish to speak first.”

The guards left. They’d return in the morning.

Tamrat lowered his head and whispered to me one final time:  
“Hold the relic close.”

I looked down at the artifact in my hands—slimy, reeking, otherworldly. I wanted to throw it away, to vomit, to scream.

Instead, I clutched it.

Because I understood now.

This was no longer just a pilgrimage.

It was a war for the soul of our Church.

And I was its unwilling prophet.

# 

# The Path of the Damned

The guards came for Tamrat just before dawn. Their armor clinked like bones rattling in a crypt, and the torchlight cast their faces into masks of judgment. One of them muttered a prayer. The other simply spit.

Tamrat did not resist. He rose slowly, the shackles on his ankles scraping the stone floor like the dragging chains of a ghost. His eyes sought Angeska’s—soft, pleading, unwavering.

“I will buy you time,” he whispered.

Zoser watched as the door closed behind him with a dull *thud*. Just like that, he was gone.

Silence settled in the cell like ash.

Zoser crouched in the corner, staring at the cloth bundle he’d tucked into his robe, pressed tightly to his chest. This relic is it cursed, or a harmless sacred fagment.

He tried not to think of Tamrat. Tried not to imagine the old priest grunting in the brush, wiping his excremented hands on sacred linen, pressing the fragment back into his mouth with trembling fingers. Again. And again.

Zoser gagged, violently. He turned away and dry-heaved, bile searing his throat. He pressed his forehead to the damp stone floor, hoping the cold would ground him.

“What is it?” Angeska asked softly.

“I can’t—” Zoser wheezed. “He *pooed it out*, Angeska. *Repeatedly.* He *ate it again.*”

She said nothing. What *was* there to say?

Above them, the faintest roar began to rise.

Tamrat was led through the corridors of Castel Sant'Angelo, his bare feet slapping against the stone like wet leather. Guards flanked him, but made no effort to jeer or mock. There was a heaviness about him now, something more than martyrdom—something ancient.

The piazza awaited. The stake, drenched in pitch. The crowd, ravenous.

Ten thousand Romans stared in breathless anticipation as the condemned heretic was dragged before them. His white robes were yellowed and torn, but his posture was imperial. He climbed the platform unassisted, as though ascending a pulpit.

Cardinal Giovanni stood in full vestments, surrounded by bishops like vultures robed in scarlet.

“Tamrat of Lalibela,” the Cardinal boomed, “you stand condemned of heresy against the Holy Church, of spreading lies, of mocking the Apostolic See with your Ethiopian sorcery and false relics. Do you wish to confess before your soul is offered to judgment?”

The crowd surged forward in breathless hunger. Tamrat gazed over them all—Romans, pilgrims, merchants, prostitutes, children with candied figs melting in their hands.

Then he smiled.

“My only confession is this,” he said, voice clear and sonorous. “That I have seen the Ark of the Covenant, and it does not lie beneath your altars of gold and dead apostles.”

Murmurs. Laughter. Scoffing from the upper tiers. A child wept.

“It rests not here, in the halls of Rome, but in the mountains of Zion—in the highlands of Ethiopia, guarded by priests who serve not kings but God alone.”

The silence that followed was as violent as thunder.

“You lie\!” Cardinal Giovanni spat. “There is no proof. No testimony. No Ark\!”

Giovanni’s face twisted into something nearly inhuman. “Enough. Let the fire silence this heretic forever.”

Tamrat looked to the sky, where clouds were beginning to gather.

Then he closed his eyes.

The fire was slow.

Rome had perfected the art of public death. The kindling was damp enough to ensure suffering. But Tamrat made no sound. No cry. No plea.

He only whispered prayers in Ge’ez, the language of angels—until the flames claimed his voice.

In the dungeon below, Zoser and Angeska wept openly. The sound of the crowd above, of flesh burning, of scripture weaponized—he could feel it in his bones.

Angeska knelt beside him, a steady hand on his shoulder. “He gave us a weapon.”

Zoser looked down at the bundle.

“No,” he whispered. “He gave us a *question*. One Rome can’t ignore. One they’ll chase across deserts and seas.”

He looked up, his dark face, exhausted, and filled with grim resolve.

“They’re going to come for us, Angeska.”

Zoser nodded, even as the walls seemed to hum with voices—ancient and growing louder.

The age of relics was over.

The age of reckoning had begun.

# Avignon

I had never known cold like this.

The dungeon air clung to my skin like the breath of the dead. Even my bones seemed to shiver in protest, as if trying to escape the cage of my own flesh. The straw beneath me was damp, frozen stiff. My knees ached. My back ached. My heart… I wasn’t sure it was still beating. Tamrat’s screams never came. That was the worst part.

There was no sound. No begging. Only fire, and silence.

That was how I knew he had died on his own terms.

I curled tighter in the corner, clutching the cloth bundle beneath my robes. The relic. His curse. My burden. My stomach turned every time I thought about it—where it had been, what it had seen. I couldn’t help but feel it pulsing faintly against my chest, like a second heart beating out an older rhythm, one I didn’t yet understand.

Beside me, Angeska sat in silence, unmoving. Her knees drawn to her chest. She hadn’t spoken since they took Tamrat. I think part of her had died with him on that stake, though the rest of her sat here still breathing.

“He wanted this,” I whispered, though I wasn’t sure if I meant the fire, the message, or the relic passed like a parasite between our hands. “He believed it would change something.”

She didn’t answer. Just stared into the dark.

I closed my eyes, certain that this was how it would end for us too.

# **The Relic's Dark Revelation**

I must've drifted into sleep, or delirium.

The next thing I remember was torchlight—not the usual snarling guards with shackles and kicks. No, these men wore polished armor with gold trims that caught the flame like scattered coins. Their eyes avoided mine, but they didn't sneer. One of them even bowed.

"The Cardinal sends his regards," the captain said in crisp Latin. "You are to be released."

I didn't move. "What?"

"You and the woman. The cardinal has requested your freedom."

I stared at him, expecting a blow, a trap. But he merely gestured toward the stone steps that led from our pit of despair.

---

**The Cleansing**

Moments later, Angeska and I emerged from the depths into sunlight so blinding it felt like divine judgment. My nilotic skin, was ashy as parchment from our imprisonment, prickled in its warmth. The air itself tasted different—no longer thick with human misery but sharp with Mediterranean salt and blooming jasmine.

They led us through narrow streets where merchants hawked their wares and children played with wooden hoops. The normalcy felt like mockery after what we'd endured. I caught Angeska's eye once—she walked with the measured gait of someone who no longer trusted solid ground beneath her feet.

At the Roman thermae, an ancient bathhouse repurposed for visiting nobility, we were separated. Angeska was sent in another direction; she disappeared beyond a carved marble archway to the women's quarters. I was ushered through bronze doors into the men's chambers. Before the guards could react, I pressed the filthy relic deeper between my lower cheeks.
