It felt like it was slipping as I walked, the fragment's edges cutting into my flesh like a reminder of duty.

The smell still clung to everything—earthy, acrid, foul in ways that defied words.

I was led to steaming waters within a marble basin, the surface clouded with rose petals and herbs that made my eyes water with their sweetness. The contrast to our cell's stench was almost violent.

"Kneel, brother," said a Monk in brown robes his sandals slapping against wet stone. 

When the monks attention turned to another area of the baths, I pulled the relic free with trembling hands. In the privacy of the steam, I began scrubbing away the filth—dried mucus, streaks of blood like curses etched in flesh. Slowly, beneath the grime, something shimmered.

Gold filaments ran through its grain like veins in a living body. Then I saw them—symbols. Not Amharic, not Ge'ez, nor Hebrew. Ancient hieroglyphics that seemed to pulse faintly beneath the surface, as if they resented being seen.

I gasped, nearly dropping the relic into the bath. The monks returned, their hands gentle as they massaged oils into my scarred skin, trimmed my ragged nails, brushed out the mats in my hair. I kept flinching—such tenderness felt like betrayal after months of brutality.

The fragment burned against my skin where I'd hidden it again.

# Reunion in Silk

They dressed me in soft linen tunics and cloaks lined with ermine. Real food followed—roasted quail with herbs I couldn't name, citrus that burst sweet and sharp on my tongue, olives swimming in golden oil, honeyed wine that nearly made me weep with its richness. Each bite felt like sacrilege.

Three days passed in this surreal luxury before they allowed me to see Angeska again. I found her in a small chamber overlooking the convent's herb garden, dressed in a simple brown robe that made her look older, more fragile. Her hands shook as she lifted a pewter cup to her lips.

"They treat us like honored guests," she said without preamble, her voice hollow. "It terrifies me more than the dungeon did."

I sat across from her, noting how she chewed slowly, as if trying to taste a world her body no longer belonged to. Dark circles shadowed her eyes, and I realized she was still mourning—not just Tamrat's death, but the loss of their shared purpose.

"We're going to Avignon," I said, just to hear something other than the silence between us. "To the Pope."

She nodded faintly, then looked directly at me. "You know what this means, don't you?"

"They want something."

Her silence was answer enough.

---

**The Road to Avignon**

The carriage that arrived the next morning was a work of art—velvet-lined, drawn by pure white horses bridled in silver. Six guards rode alongside us, two ahead, two behind, and two within the coach itself. Not a single man spoke during the entire journey.

As we rocked gently over cobblestones and dirt paths, I watched the landscape change from Mediterranean coastal plains to the rolling hills of southern Gaul. The fragment pressed against my ribs where I'd sewn it into my tunic's lining, a constant reminder of the weight I carried.

The Roman roads were still impressive after all these centuries, but I could feel the empire's ghost in every weathered milestone we passed. How many pilgrims had traveled this same route, seeking absolution or audience with the Holy Father? How many had carried secrets as dangerous as mine?

"They heard everything," I said quietly on our second day of travel. "Tamrat's words—the fire didn't silence them, it amplified them."

Angeska's hands twisted in her lap. "All of Rome knows now. About the Ark. About Ethiopia's claim."

I nodded, understanding crystallizing like frost on glass. "They don't just want to debate doctrine anymore. If they could possess the Ark itself..."

"Divine favor," she finished. "Power to crush rival sects, erase doubt, rule not just bodies but souls."

The Vatican did not offer mercy. It extended leverage.

And they believed I was the key to it all.

---

**Avignon's Golden Cage**

We were greeted by Cardinal Humbert of Toulouse, a man whose hands were softer than any bishop's should be and whose smile never quite reached his pale eyes. His robes rustled with the whisper of silk as he bowed—too low, too theatrical.

"You are honored guests of the Church," he said, his Latin flavored with southern French vowels. "The Holy Father is most intrigued by your wisdom, your journey, your... courage."

Angeska was taken to the convent again—"for propriety," they said. I watched her disappear through an arched doorway, her shoulders rigid with suppressed fury.

They housed me in a palatial guest chamber in the papal residence itself, its windows overlooking gardens where peacocks strutted among sculpted hedges. My bed was draped in cloth-of-gold, my meals served on plates that reflected candlelight like captured stars.

For two weeks, I was treated like a prophet returned from the wilderness.

And I hated every moment of it.

No one asked real questions. They praised my "steadfast faith," spoke of "bridging the ancient gap" between our churches, even suggested I might be instrumental in "healing the schism between East and West." Dominican scholars quoted Augustine at me over elaborate dinners. Benedictine copyists showed me illuminated manuscripts with reverent pride.

They were flattering me. Grooming me like a prize stallion.

I could feel their hunger beneath the velvet courtesy—sharp as a blade, patient as a spider.

The bells of Avignon rang every hour, their bronze voices echoing off stone walls until the city itself seemed to pulse with religious fervor. But beneath the chanted prayers and incense, I heard something else: the whisper of political calculation, the rustle of correspondence sealed with scarlet wax, the soft footsteps of men who moved through corridors of power with practiced ease.

This was not Jerusalem. This was Rome transplanted, all its ambitions concentrated in this single city on the Rhône.

---

# **Chapter XXIV: The Golden Cage**

It was on the fourteenth night when Cardinal Humbert approached me after Vespers, his silk slippers whispering against the cold marble of the cathedral nave. Candlelight caught the gold threads in his scarlet robes, making him shimmer like a spirit half-summoned.

"Brother Zoser," he said, his voice polished and courteous, though it moved like a blade beneath velvet. "Would you do us the honor of joining a special Lectio Divina tonight? We seek divine insight concerning the crusade for Acre. Your Ethiopian scholarship would be invaluable."

I bowed. As an Ethiopian monk—a man of humble origins, a servant of the ancient Church on a pilgrimage to meet the leader of the Roman church, the Pope—to refuse would be against everything I was striving to do.

"Of course, Your Eminence," I replied in halting Latin.

The truth was, I barely understood him. The Latin tongue twisted in my ears like thorns. I needed Angeska—Angeska of the Axumite liturgy and the golden voice who spoke seven languages like water. We had arrived in Avignon together, both pilgrims of the Ethiopian Church, but I had not seen her since the day we arrived. She was separated from me by hosts who claimed piety but hid their purpose.

And yet, as I followed the Cardinal down corridors unfamiliar to me—past cloisters and perfumed smoke, beneath sanctuaries older than memory—

We passed deeper into the heart of the Roman sanctum, through bricked corridors, beneath halls that had not felt daylight in centuries. The Cardinals led the way, believing they walked toward divine illumination.

I froze. I wasn't sure at first. Shaking off my disbelief, I looked again.

I saw her.

Angeska.

But not Angeska as I remembered her.

She stood there preparing the circular chamber, surrounded by seven men kneeling around a brazier. The room shimmered with heat and holy smoke. Tomes lined the walls—ancient, breathing things. But it was Angeska who arrested my gaze.

She no longer bore the woven sigils of our homeland. She wore Roman robes now—red and gold, her veil folded in papal fashion. She did not bow in the way of the east. Her hands made the sign of the cross like a woman born to it.

But I knew her.

And something was deeply, terribly wrong.

Her smile was too calm. Her eyes too hollow. As if the Angeska I had known had been submerged—and another had risen in her place.

"Come, Brother Zoser," Cardinal Humbert said. "We begin."

I knelt.

"Angeska?" I whispered.

She gave no sign of recognition. Or perhaps she did, and chose silence.

"Tonight," the Cardinal declared, "we follow the sacred ladder: lectio, meditatio, oratio, and contemplatio."

He opened a Gospel, his voice unnaturally resonant: "Before the throne was a sea of glass like unto crystal. And around the throne, four beasts, full of eyes..."

The others swayed. The brazier hissed.

A Dominican pressed a chalice into my hands. "Drink," said Angeska. "It will help you see."

I brought the cup to my lips, letting only the thinnest sip touch my tongue. Even that was enough. Fire and iron.

Then something changed.

The Cardinal's words faltered.

Angeska stepped forward.

She forced the cup upon me.

"It'll help you see," she said. "You need to see, Zoser. Or they'll take it from you. And you'll never know what you carry."

I hesitated.

Then drank.

The taste was a betrayal—sharp, sour, burning. My throat closed in revolt. My teeth ached. My vision blurred.

She produced a scroll—not Latin, not Ge'ez, not Greek—but something older. The Cardinal blinked, uncertain.

She began to chant.

"Angeska—" I began, but she did not hear me.

She turned to the others. "He carries the fragment."

The Cardinals gasped. Their eyes turned to me. The brazier spat sparks.

"You told them?" I said, my voice strangled.

She smiled. "I only showed them what they could not see. They accepted me—but I was never, not truly, one of yours."

The ritual had changed. It was no longer the Church's. It was hers.

And they didn't know it.

Not until it was too late.

# **Chapter XXV: The Dissolution of Flesh**

They did not know it, but they moved to her rhythm.

The walls were coiled in spiraling script that hurt to follow. Black candles lit corners that should have been shadow. The air shimmered like the breath of something sleeping.

I staggered backward, the bowl shattering like bones.

Then came the unraveling.

A jolt through my spine. My knees buckled. I fell, but did not land.

My body below me. My breath ragged. My soul ascending.

Shapes bent around me—stars blooming in ink, spirals of eyes and fire.

Whispers rang in tongues that sang of birth and death as one.

Time broke.

I saw Ethiopia alight with wings and stone.

I saw Tamrat laughing in fire that turned to flowers.

I saw Cardinal Humbert kneeling before a mirror of hunger.

I saw myself—alone, holding something too terrible to name.

And then, a voice:

"You carry not wood, but a doorway."

# **Chapter XXVI: The Black Rite**

I awoke.

The chamber was no longer a place. It was a wound.

Angeska stood above me, radiant and wrong.

Her skin shimmered like oiled parchment. Her pupils narrowed to slits. Her smile unfolded wider than human anatomy should allow.

"You drank the veil," she said. "Now see."

Her modest robes dissolved. Glyphs blazed upon her limbs—spirals, sigils, terrible names etched into flesh. Jewels jutted from bone. She glowed like rot inside a ruby.

She was no pilgrim.

She was a witch.

Nilotic. Black-magic born. A scion of those who spoke to spirits not in prayer, but in command.

"You thought I followed you," she said. "I led you."

She turned to the brazier.

From her robes she drew the relic—the fragment.

But now, it was no relic.

It was the Immortal Key.

The moment it touched flame, it screamed. Not with sound—but with presence.

The gold turned red.

Then white.

Then it began to melt.

Thick, glistening liquid oozed from the carved sigils—onto the stone, into bowls, into her hands.

With unholy reverence, she raised the dripping Key.

"You think it opens something?" she laughed. "It is something. A mouth. A forge. A seed. It creates. In pain. In sacrifice. In blood."

She mounted me—weightless and crushing, like the stars had pressed themselves onto my chest.

"You are the vessel. The altar. The gate."

"No," I rasped—but my voice fell into the flames.

The Key pulsed with impossible hunger.

Then she plunged it into my mouth.

Once.

Twice.

Again.

Each thrust burned like lightning and thunder and memory.

The Key did not cut.

Its molten ooze wrote.

It wrote across me in living fire.

And when the last breath tore from my lungs—

I was not gone.

something opened.

I was at a door.

And then… It was as if I had  died.

---

**Chapter XXVII: Between Thrones**

How long I lay dead, I do not know.

But I returned.

Not as breath returns to the drowning, or light to a candle.

I simply *was* again.

And I was not alone.

## **Chapter XXVIII: After the Key**

There was no light in death.

No peace.

No release.

Only pressure—crushing, infinite, like drowning in a mouth that would never close. My lungs did not gasp. My spirit did not rise. Something else filled the void.

It forced itself into me.

Thick, warm, alien.

It slid down my throat and curled in my gut. It pulsed, coiled, fed.

Then—I returned.

---

I awoke to something heavy straddling my chest.

My eyes snapped open—

—and I saw **Angeska**.

But she was no longer the woman I had known. Not the fellow pilgrim. Not the scholar. Not even the witch.

She had become something else.

Her skin was **pitch-dark**, glistening and lacquered like obsidian fresh from the earth. Her face was a ruin—lips split, cheeks stretched too wide, her eyes two molten embers sunk deep into sockets that bled smoke.

She grinned down at me, her teeth no longer human—glinting with shards of something **forged**, not grown.

“You swallowed it,” she whispered, voice thick and tremulous with joy and hunger. “I forced it into you. And it lives.”

Her hands were skeletal but powerful, clawed fingers wrapping around my face as if molding me from clay. Her breath smelled of ash and metal, and her dreadlocked hair swung like ritual cords soaked in something old.

I struggled—

—but my body did not respond.

I had no control.

Every breath, every twitch, every stirring was hers.

Around us, the shadows swelled and twisted.

I turned my head—barely able to.

The Cardinals were still there.

Some lay motionless, slack-jawed, their eyes rolled back. Others twitched and convulsed on the floor like broken marionettes, moaning softly through foaming mouths. Their minds were gone, shattered by **the smoke, the wine, the ancient toxins** that filled this cursed basilica. They saw nothing of what was happening now.

They were relics—witnesses in name only.

And Angeska was **riding me**.

Not in love. Not in lust.

In ritual.

Her hips moved with manic precision, faster and harder, as if tracing runes into the space between our bodies. Each motion carved a symbol. Each breath drew a circle of power. I felt it—something coalescing in the air, pulling at us both, feeding from me.

“You don’t understand,” she hissed, nails biting into my cheeks. “It’s not a relic. It’s not an object. It’s **a seed**. A wound in the world. And now… it has **a womb**.”

Her body arched, her hands raised above her head in exultation.

“You were the vessel. I am the crucible.”

My spine arched of its own accord. I gasped—not from pleasure, not from pain, but from something beyond either. My soul was **being poured out**, pulled from me in spirals, my veins trembling, skin boiling from within.

I could feel myself—my **essence**—rising.

“No—” I whispered.

“Yes,” she breathed.

She bent low over me, her body glowing faintly with internal fire, veins of liquid shadow crawling beneath her skin.

“You were always meant for this,” she whispered. “Your Church, your prayers, your suffering—they were all **preludes** to this moment. You were chosen. To fill me.”

My body tensed.

I could feel it—my final resistance breaking.

My breath stuttered.

A cry tore from my throat.

And then—

**Release.**

It surged from me—hot, bright, unbearable.

My life.

My **self**.

It left my body in one long, final exhalation, swallowed by the vessel above me.

Angeska cried out, a sound somewhere between a hymn and a scream. Her body convulsed, glowing brighter and darker at once, shadows blooming across her flesh like night-blooming flowers.

I sagged.

Empty.

Spent.

Still alive, but hollowed out.

She collapsed onto me, panting, shaking, soaked in sweat and something far worse. Her hands trembled as she pressed them to her own stomach.

She smiled.

A smile full of ruin.

“It’s begun,” she whispered. “You’ve seeded it.”

And somewhere far above, I heard bells tolling—not of celebration, nor mourning—but of something breaking.

A threshold passed.

A veil torn.

When I awoke Angeska was gone.

All that was left, just a faint warmth—as if something ancient still lingered, coiled beneath the skin like a secret not yet spoken.

I was in a room with the cardinals all arguing.

Suddenly, the storm of arguments halted, like the breath of the Church had been yanked from its lungs.

They were looking *at me*.

Me.

*They could see me now.*

My heart kicked like a mule in my chest.

I was lying on the cold marble floor, robes twisted, palms raw. My chest heaved. My mouth tasted of metal and fire.

I scrambled to my feet, staggering, limbs not quite answering the call of gravity.

“Where am I? What—what is this place?” I asked, my voice cracking. “What happened to—?”

I could speak their language, latin.

Dozens of scarlet-robed men surrounded me. Their faces were masks—fear, fury, confusion, awe. One crossed himself. Another drew back as if I were diseased.

I turned in place, dizzy. The massive hall spun in circles—painted saints blurred into demons.

The same hall.

The *Council*.

A trumpet blared. The Pope entered, robed in white, flanked by guards with halberds and books. His face was serene, but his eyes—his *eyes*—were full of doubt. The kind of doubt that kings have before losing kingdoms.

I realized, slowly, where I stood.

This was not just a papal audience.

It was the *Council*.

The seat of the Pope himself.

How had I come here?

Where was Angeska?

---

**Chapter XXIX: The Pope’s Gaze**

A heavy voice cut the silence.

“Bring him forward.”

All turned.

On the elevated dais, beneath a golden canopy carved with vines and angels, the Pope sat—Urban or Clement, I could not say. He was crowned, robed in purest white, staring at me with the eyes of a man who has stared too long into sacred fire.

I was pulled forward. Not roughly, but urgently.

“Who are you?” the Pope asked. I fell to my knees—not in reverence, but because my legs had turned to ash.

“I… I don’t know,” I gasped. “One moment I was dying. Angeska—she had the Key—I saw—she—”

I stopped.

What was I saying?

The Cardinals erupted.

“Witchcraft\!”

“Madness\!”

“Could be possessed\!”

“Could be a *sign*\!”

The Pope raised one hand and the noise died like a crushed flame.

“You speak of a Key,” he said. “What key?”

“I... don’t know what it truly is,” I stammered. “A relic. Carried from Axum. Hidden in flesh. Passed in secret.”

His eyes narrowed. “You speak of Ethiopia. Of relics. And you mention the Ark. Is that your claim?”

I looked up at him, breathing raggedly. My hands trembled.

“I don’t claim anything, Holiness. I was a scribe. I was a monk. Now I’m... something else.”

“Why are you here?”

“I don’t know.”

That was the truest thing I’d said yet.

I remembered dying.

Not as a metaphor. Not as a poetic vision.  
 I remembered the pain.  
 The blood.  
 The light.  
 The *end*.

And yet here I was. Walking. Speaking. Breathing.

 A dead man reborn.

But each of those things felt... rehearsed. Like a poor imitation of life.

Something was wrong.

At first I thought it was exhaustion. Grief. Trauma. But then I noticed the pattern.

Everywhere I went, something followed.

**Chaos.**

I stepped into the library, and the scribes were already in shouting matches over forged bulls and contradictory doctrines. One struck another with a crucifix.

**Schism.**

In the street outside the palace, I saw Dominican friars and Franciscan preachers accusing each other of heresy, both claiming visions. A priest burned his own vestments. An archbishop renounced his oath on the steps of the cathedral.

**Sickness.**

In the alleys behind the wine sellers, I saw children coughing blood. Their mothers wept saltless tears. Rats ran in the open, feasting.

The **plague**, they whispered.

And I was always *there*, just before, just after, or just *during*.

At first I thought I was cursed.

Then, I began to suspect something worse:

# I was the epicenter.

I hadn’t eaten in a day. Not from fasting. From revulsion.

Food no longer tasted like anything. Water passed through me like mist. Even my breath was soundless now, as though the world no longer acknowledged I truly existed.

I stared at my reflection in the polished brass bowl.

My eyes were still mine. But behind them... something moved. Something *watched*.

And when I spoke aloud to myself, it wasn’t my voice I heard—it was deeper. Slower. Measured like liturgy.

There was a name forming in my mind, letter by letter, syllable by syllable, spoken not with breath but with *presence*. Not a word I had read. Not a thing I’d ever heard taught in scripture.

Not *human*.

I gripped my chest, searching for a heartbeat.

There was one.

But it no longer beat in rhythm with the world around me.

---

**Chapter XXXIII: The Cloak of the Dead**

I wandered into the cathedral of Saint Agricol before dawn, seeking stillness. The pews were empty. The candles sputtered on their own. The crucifix above the altar was cracked—splintered down Christ’s side, as if the wood itself had given up believing.

There was a boy there. Thin, pale, half-asleep.

I sat beside him.

“You shouldn’t be here,” I said.

“I know,” he replied. “But I heard the voices again. The ones that crawl up through the stones.”

“What do they say?”

He turned his head. Looked me straight in the eye.

“They say you’re wearing a dead man’s skin.”

I stared at him.

He giggled and ran.

I notice that plant life would deteriorate if I remain in its approximity too long, like if I was sucking the life force from them.

Even the insect near me kept falling and seemingly accidentally kill themselves, everything around was chaos. 

I thought solitude would be my only place for peace of mind.

I returned to my chamber, bolted the door, and lit every candle I had.

I stripped off my robe. Stood before the mirror. Looked at myself.

My dark skin was dry, grey scales covered my fresh in places.

Veins  like ink lines ran beneath the surface, but they moved. Shifted. Rearranged.

My stomach held no scar. But when I pressed there—where the Key had gone in—I *felt* the ghost. A pressure. A weight. Something embedded in the spirit rather than the flesh.

And then I heard it again. The voice.

Not Angeska.

Something *older*.

A memory in the marrow.

> “You did not survive.  
>  You were *rewritten*.”

---

**Chapter XXXV: The Coming Storm**

The next day, black smoke rose from the papal palace.

A new claimant had declared himself Pope.

Avignon boiled with whispers.

Witches in convents. Blood in the Tiber. A monk seen levitating. An angel seen weeping black tears on the statue of Saint Martha.

And in every vision, in every fire, in every dream...

They saw me.

Not by name.

But by shape.

The **Risen One**.

The one with the dead man’s eyes and the relic-shaped hole in his soul.

And still no sign of Angeska.

No Key.

No certainty.

Only the growing sense that I had not escaped the ritual after all...

…I had *become* it.

#  Cardinal 2 

Cardinal humbert loomed over me, his face twisted in rage.

“You dare bring this curse into God’s house?” he thundered, his voice echoing off the stone walls.

“It’s not just a curse\!” I shouted, my voice breaking. “It’s alive\! It’s manipulating us—our very reality\!”

The veiled man stepped forward, his voice calm and cold. “And yet you still cling to the idea that you can stop it.”

The words from the parchment seemed to pulse in my mind, their meaning twisting and fragmenting. They weren’t just words; they were a language, a key, a weapon. And as I stared into the veiled man’s gleaming eyes, I realized with dawning horror that he wasn’t a man at all.

It was a vessel.

I stumbled back, and the vision shattered, my head pounding, the room spinning. My knees buckled, and I collapsed to the floor. The stone beneath me felt alive, throbbing like flesh. I looked up, and the black walls began to bleed, dark tendrils seeping out and crawling toward me.

The entity.

Cardinal Humbert words rang in my ears: “It feeds on fear, despair, and the corruption of minds. It seeks release.”

I scrambled to my feet, but the room shifted again. The air was heavy with whispers, voices I couldn’t understand but felt compelled to obey. My fingers brushed the edge of the parchment, and the room erupted in light.

When the light faded, I was back in the Patriarch Cerularius Constantinople church. Or was I? The abbey was silent, but the silence was oppressive, a void that pressed against my skull. The walls seemed to breathe, the books on the shelves replaced by endless rows of the same cursed parchment. I turned, and there it was—a figure standing in the corner, its form barely human, its face obscured by shifting shadows.

“You’ve seen too much,” it said, its voice a guttural whisper. “You cannot unsee.”

My knees gave out, and I fell to the ground, clutching my head as the memories of  Patriarch Cerularius Constantinople church—all of it—flooded back. I realized the truth then: I hadn’t escaped.

I was still trapped.

I was no longer in a place of learning. It was a prison. And the entity, the Curator of this twisted reality, was watching.

The world around me was dissolving into chaos. One moment I was in the presence of Cardinal Humbert, the next, I was lost in a labyrinth of shadows. Panic surged through me as I stumbled through the dimly lit corridors, the distant echo of voices fading into nothingness. The only sound was the pounding of my own heart, a frantic rhythm that mirrored the urgency of my situation.

I burst through a heavy wooden door, the sudden darkness swallowing me whole. My breath caught in my throat as I fumbled for a way out. The footsteps grew louder, their rhythm a relentless drumbeat against the stone corridor outside. The door handle twisted, and a chill ran down my spine. I clutched the cursed parchment to my chest, every nerve in my body screaming that this was the end. But then—there. A glint of gold near the fresco on the far wall. My fingers trembled as I pressed against it, the ancient mechanism shifting with a soft groan, revealing a concealed door. Without hesitation, I slipped inside, the faint creak of the closing panel masking the ominous turn of the door behind me.

The narrow staircase spiraled downward, its steps slick with centuries of condensation. The air grew colder, each breath visible as a faint plume of mist. Darkness swallowed me, broken only by the faint orange glow of a torch in my hand, its flames flickering against walls carved with intricate symbols. They weren’t hieroglyphs—no, these markings were older, alien, their shapes writhing like trapped insects when caught in the light.

The whispers began again as I descended, faint and insidious. They weren’t external—they were inside me, threading through my thoughts like venom. Sekhem… Sekhem… The word pulsed in my mind, each repetition louder, sharper, until it felt like my skull might split. I clung to the bannister, gasping, my head spinning.

When I finally reached the bottom, I emerged into the crypt.

The air here was colder still, biting at my exposed skin. My torch illuminated a vast chamber filled with the tombs of monks, their stone sarcophagi lined in precise rows, each etched with prayers for eternal rest. But as I moved further in, I saw that some of the tombs had been defaced. Symbols had been scratched into the stone, spiraling and branching like the veins of some monstrous organism. The hieroglyphs on the parchment now glowed faintly, as though resonating with the carvings.

At the center of the crypt was an altar, weathered and cracked, yet radiating an aura of oppressive power. I unfurled the parchment on its surface, my hands trembling as I traced the intricate markings again. Each line felt alive beneath my fingers, pulsing faintly in time with the whispers. My head throbbed as I struggled to make sense of it, but one thing was clear:

This map led here for a reason.

“What are you hiding?” I murmured, my voice trembling in the suffocating silence.

The word Sekhem floated to the forefront of my mind again, a whisper that grew into a roar. My vision blurred as images seared themselves into my thoughts: pyramids not as tombs but as conduits, threads of energy linking the heavens and earth; the Il Moro standing at the nexus of creation, wielding a force that bound life, death, time, and space.

Connection.

At the far end of the crypt, a massive sarcophagus loomed, black as obsidian, its surface polished to a mirror sheen. Inscribed across it was the ambigram: Lux Obscura. The letters glowed faintly, their light pulsing in rhythm with my own heartbeat. I approached, drawn as though by an invisible hand.

As my palm touched the sarcophagus, the air thickened, the whispers rising into a cacophony of screams. My torch flickered violently, its light casting grotesque shadows that seemed to twist and writhe. The symbols on the sarcophagus shifted, rearranging themselves into patterns that defied logic, their meanings warping as I stared.

Then I saw it.

The entity entombed within was no corpse. It was a schism, a rupture in the fabric of reality itself. Its form was indistinct, shifting between shadow and fire, its edges blurring as though it existed in multiple dimensions at once. It burned into me, filling my mind with visions of chaos: cities swallowed by darkness, skies torn asunder, time unraveling into an endless loop of destruction.

I staggered back, clutching my head as its voice thundered through me:

"You are mine now."

The door to the crypt swung open. The sound of boots echoed against the stone, and in the flickering light, I saw them—the crimson robes of the Church’s Silence-Keepers. Their faces were obscured by iron masks, their weapons drawn, their eyes gleaming with righteous fury.

“You’ve gone too far, Zoser,” one of them intoned, his voice cold and hollow. “Release the parchment and submit to judgment.”

But they were too late.

The schism had already begun consuming me. I could feel it spreading, its tendrils sinking into my soul, warping my thoughts, twisting my reality. My body was no longer my own; I was becoming its vessel, a conduit for its darkness to spill into the world.

The Silence-Keepers advanced, their blades glinting in the torchlight. I raised a hand instinctively, and the air itself seemed to ripple. A burst of energy erupted from me, throwing them back with an unearthly force. The whispers in my head were no longer whispers—they were commands.

“No\!” I screamed, falling to my knees, fighting against the entity clawing at my mind. “I won’t let you—”

But it was too late. The schism,its darkness filling every corner of my being. I could feel its hunger, its thirst for chaos, and I knew what it wanted: to be free.

The last thing I saw before the torchlight faded was the Silence-Keepers rising to their feet, their faces etched with terror. And then, darkness consumed everything.

# 

# Byzantine

Yet still they argued, two princes of a fracturing church, destroying centuries of unity over the temperature of communion wine and the direction of hand gestures.

When Cerularius demanded we recognize Constantinople's equality with Rome, I saw in Cardinal Humbert's eyes that the final bridge would burn. There would be no reconciliation, no unity, only a divide that would echo through centuries.

"Your arrogance blinds you\!" The Cardinal's voice cracked. "You place yourself equal to Rome? Then receive Rome's judgment\!" He hurled another copy of the papal bull, this time directly at the Patriarch's feet.

Cerularius rose, his massive figure trembling with fury. "So Rome shows its true face at last." He turned to his guards. "Bring me paper and ink. If Rome wishes to excommunicate us, we shall return the favor."

The horror of what was unfolding struck me then – these men were not just arguing, they were ripping Christendom in two. Centuries of theological differences explode into personal hatred. As guards moved to separate the shouting men, I saw the final bridge between East and West burn. The Great Schism had begun, and I had helped light its fire.

That night, rioting broke out in Constantinople's streets between Latin and Greek Christians,

 I slipped away from our quarters. The city was tearing itself apart, and I have become the vessel of its poison. 

# 40 days 40 nights

In the year, 1054, as the great schism wrenched the Holy Church apart, I, Zoser, became the vessel of something ancient and unspeakable. It found me as I wandered the labyrinthine halls of Hagia Sophia in Constantinople, where whispers of Rome's collapse reached us like smoke from a distant fire.

I had heard of Pope Leo IX’s death—though, in truth, he had died many years before his final breath was announced. His death meant that the papal bull of excommunication, wielded by Cardinal Humbert with such righteous fury, was no more valid than a shadow claiming to be light. Yet none knew this, not even Humbert himself, when he laid the bull upon the altar of Hagia Sophia, condemning the East to separation.

That act... that unholy rupture in Christendom... it tore something deeper than parchment. It was then, as ash and bitter recrimination filled the air, that it entered. Through that tear, something vast and ancient clawed its way into our world, unseen yet palpable. And it found me.

I remember the moment I stood in Hagia Sophia, the domed heavens above pressing down as though heaven had turned its gaze. A shadow deeper than night spilled through the air, coiling like smoke into my chest.

I fell to my knees, my mouth opening in a silent scream. No voice escaped. Instead, I felt it—felt it—seep into every fiber of my being. It was cold, ancient, and terrible, yet filled with a hunger so vast it threatened to unmake me. My limbs obeyed another's will. My thoughts became whispers buried under the weight of its darkness.

I was chosen, and so I rose, my knees trembled and my heart raced with terror. It drove me—out of Constantinople, away from the great domes and the accusing faces of men. We wandered the desolate paths of the empire, the entity and I locked in a silent, unseen war. It bent the wills of all we met—peasants, soldiers, even beasts—to bow before its incomprehensible presence. I watched in horror as it forced trembling men to kneel, their lips moving in prayers I could not understand.

Yet, it was uncertain. At times, I could feel my thoughts rising, like a drowning man breaking the surface, only to be dragged back under. But the battle within me gave me glimpses of its nature. It fed on fear and discord, drank deep from the well of despair. And yet... it was curious. It rifled through my memories as one might rifle through a library of ancient tomes.

After long months of wandering, it turned us toward Florence—my Florence. My youth had been spent within the serene cloisters of San Miniato al Monte, but as we approached the city walls, I felt its grip falter. Was it hesitant? Or had my resistance begun to weaken its hold?

We entered the city at dusk, shadows swallowing us whole. The streets seemed narrower than I remembered, the faces of the townsfolk unfamiliar and wary. They crossed themselves as I passed, their eyes darting away from mine. Did they sense it, the darkness I carried? Or was it simply the aura of a stranger returning to a home no longer his?

Through Florence's shadowed streets, I stumbled onward, the gloom seeming to conspire with the entity that plagued me. My appearance was that of a wretch—a beggar so desolate that even the destitute turned their faces away. Yet it was not my ragged state that repelled them. An invisible darkness clung to me, a palpable malignancy that seeped into the air like a poison. Children burst into tears at my passing, their cries echoing through narrow alleyways. Dogs, loyal guardians of humanity, slunk into shadows, their whimpers betraying an instinctive fear. Even the cobblestones beneath my feet seemed to darken, as if recoiling from the weight of my steps.

I stumbled into the courtyard of San Miniato, the familiar stones beneath my feet a strange comfort. The entity stirred within me, its voice low and questioning. “My sanctuary,” I whispered.

At last, I came to a halt before the weathered door of my old lodgings. This place, which once bore witness to my peaceful life, now stood as a haunted relic of my former self. My hand, trembling and streaked with grime, reached for the latch. The wood felt alien beneath my fingers, its grain rough and accusing. I pushed the door open, the hinges groaning like mourners at a grave.

Inside, Signora Bellini stood frozen at her cooking hearth. Her face, etched with years of quiet labor, transformed into a mask of stark disbelief. The wooden spoon she held fell to the floor with a sharp clatter, a sound that reverberated in the oppressive silence.

"Zoser?" she whispered, her voice fragile as the flame of a votive candle. She crossed herself rapidly, her lips moving in a prayer I could not hear.

Her voice—familiar, kind, and untouched by the world’s cruel cynicism—pierced through the veil of darkness binding me. For the first time in what felt like eternity, the entity's grip faltered. It recoiled, retreating into the deepest recesses of my soul like a beast startled from its hunt. I felt the full weight of my body crash down upon me, as if the entity’s withdrawal had unleashed the physical toll of my endless journey.

My legs, once possessed of an unnatural strength, buckled. I collapsed onto the rush-covered floor, the rough texture biting into my skin. Pain, raw and searing, coursed through every muscle, every joint. Hunger clawed at my stomach, not the insatiable hunger of the entity but the simple, mortal need for sustenance. My throat burned, parched beyond endurance. I tried to speak—to warn her, to beg for water, to utter some plea for mercy—but only a dry rasp escaped my lips.

# Back Home

The last thing I saw before unconsciousness claimed me was Signora Bellini’s face. Her expression shifted from terror to compassion as she knelt beside me, her hands trembling as they reached out. In that fleeting moment, I prayed—a desperate, trembling prayer—that the entity would remain dormant. That her charity would not summon its wrath. But faith, I knew, was a fragile thing, and certainty had abandoned me long ago.

When I awoke, the world was dim, the air cool against my fevered skin. A cloth soaked in herb-steeped water rested on my brow, and the faint aroma of broth lingered in the room. For one blessed moment, I thought it had all been a fevered nightmare, some specter conjured by hunger and exhaustion. But then, deep within, I felt it stir—a serpent uncoiling, its scales scraping against the walls of my soul.

"Be still, Zoser," came a voice, low and steady. Signora Bellini’s voice. It was both a comfort and a warning. "You’ve been unconscious for three days."

Three days. The words struck me like a bell’s toll. I forced my eyes open, though even this simple act seemed monumental. The room swam into view, dimly lit by a single, flickering candle. Its light cast shadows on the walls, where icons of saints watched over me with expressions that now seemed judgmental rather than benevolent.

Signora Bellini leaned over me, her face etched with worry and exhaustion. Lines that I did not remember now carved her features, and her hair, once streaked with silver, seemed entirely gray. In her hands, she held a clay cup, its rim chipped from years of use. Gently, she pressed it to my lips, tipping it just enough for water to trickle into my mouth. The cool liquid was a balm to my parched throat.

"The physician came to see you," she said softly, her voice betraying her unease. "He says you’re suffering from exhaustion and hunger, but..." She hesitated, her words faltering as her gaze shifted. "But there’s something else. Something he couldn’t explain."

I tried to speak, to warn her, but my body betrayed me. The entity stirred, its presence like a tide rising inexorably within me. It listened, curious, as if savoring her words. I felt its dark amusement, its coiled power waiting for the moment to strike.

"Signora," I croaked, each syllable a struggle. "You must leave. You must—" Pain exploded behind my eyes, a searing agony that silenced me. The entity’s displeasure flooded my mind, its will pressing down like an iron shroud.

"Ssh," she soothed, misunderstanding my agony. "Don’t speak. You’re safe here, Zoser. God is watching over you."

I wanted to cry out, to tell her that no one was safe—not while I breathed, not while this thing shared my body. But the pain rendered me mute, and I could only watch as she rose to tend the broth simmering on the hearth.

Behind her, the shadows seemed to deepen, their shapes twisting in ways that defied the flickering candlelight. The entity was watching her now, its attention sharp and calculating. A chill ran through me as I realized that her charity, her compassion, had caught its interest.

And so I lay there, helpless, as the woman who had shown me kindness moved about the room, unaware that she shared it with a monster.

The room swam before my eyes, a chaotic blur of shadows and shifting shapes. My throat burned, my breath came in ragged gasps. "Ssh," her soothing voice whispered, mistaking my distress for weakness. "Don't try to speak yet. But you should know \- Cardinale Humbert has been asking after you. I sent word to San Miniato when you first arrived."

Signora Bellini’s words struck like a thunderclap in my mind.

"Cardinale Humbert has been asking after you."

I stared at her, my lips forming a silent protest, but the entity stirred with an unsettling satisfaction, the corners of its presence curling in dark amusement. A chill, colder than the winter wind, seeped into my bones. Cardinale Humbert? The very man who, with his misguided zeal, had ignited the flames of the Great Schism, a man whose actions had rent the Church in two, a man who unwittingly paved the way for this horrifying transformation, this thing inside me. And now he was coming here, to me. He knew. How could he not? He had seen what I had become in Constantinople, though perhaps he had not understood it then.

The fragile peace of this small house, of Signora Bellini’s unwitting charity, shattered in that moment. My chest tightened as fear and the entity’s dark excitement warred within me. Humbert was not a mere man; he was a cardinal, a legate, armed with the authority of the fractured Church and the iron will to wield it. If he recognized me for what I was now—a vessel for something ancient and unholy—there would be no mercy. But Humbert’s presence here was not simply a threat to me. Florence itself, with its narrow streets and towering cathedrals, would become the stage for a confrontation that could unmake far more than one broken soul.

The entity stirred fully awake now, no longer lurking in the shadows of my mind but stretching its tendrils into every corner of my being. Its voice whispered, low and mocking. He comes, your cardinal, your shepherd of men. But what will he find, Zoser? A lamb? Or the wolf hiding within your skin?

I gritted my teeth, struggling to suppress the venomous whispers, but they wound tighter around my thoughts. The entity had known this would happen. It had sought this confrontation, steering me toward Florence not for sanctuary, but for conquest. What greater prize than the heart of the Church itself, already wounded and divided? And Humbert—proud, imperious Humbert—was a key to unlocking something far more terrible.

“Zoser,” Signora Bellini said gently, interrupting my spiraling dread. She placed a steady hand on my arm, her touch light but grounding. “You’re trembling. You must rest. Whatever troubles you can wait. God will protect you.”

Foolish woman, the entity hissed within me. Its malice coiled like smoke, pressing against the edges of my control. Through my eyes, it watched her movements, studying her simplicity, her earnest piety. Signora Bellini, with her quiet faith and unyielding kindness, was an affront to its nature.

“Signora,” I rasped, the effort of speaking burning my throat. “You do not understand. You must leave this house. Go to the cloisters. Take refuge there.”

She frowned, her brow creasing with concern. “Leave? But why, Zoser? You’re ill. What kind of Christian would I be to abandon you now? The cardinal will know what to do when he arrives.”

“No\!” The word tore from my lips with more force than I intended, and for a moment, the shadows in the room seemed to deepen, the flickering candlelight faltering as if caught in a breath of unnatural wind. Signora Bellini flinched, her hand drawing back.

“Zoser, you’re fevered,” she said, though now her voice wavered. “Rest. Please. God’s will—”

“God’s will?” The entity’s voice slipped through my own, low and venomous, before I could stop it. My heart thundered as I felt it rise, its presence seeping into my words, twisting them into something cruel and alien. “Do you believe He watches? Do you think your prayers are heard? He turned away long ago.”

Signora Bellini paled, clutching the cross around her neck. “Zoser,” she whispered, her voice trembling. “What is this? What has happened to you?”

But before I could answer—or before the entity could speak again—the sound of hooves and voices reached us from the street outside. A shadow passed across the window, the heavy, deliberate tread of armored boots on cobblestones. My blood ran cold. He was here.

# Humberts Death

The night air was heavy with incense and dread as they came—a procession of torches and grim faces, their light flickering off the ancient stones of the basilica like restless spirits. Through the fractured lens of my mind, I saw them approach: Cardinal Humbert at the forefront, his shoulders hunched under the weight of despair and authority. Behind him marched the grim-faced monks, their lips moving in ceaseless prayer, and the armored men gripping their weapons with white-knuckled resolve. But it was the others—the Silent Keepers—who held my gaze.

They were shadowed figures, half-hidden beneath robes embroidered with ancient, forgotten symbols. Their hollow eyes betrayed no emotion, only the cold focus of those who guarded secrets too dangerous to name. I recognized them instantly, though I had never met them before. The entity within me stirred, its interest piqued by their presence. Ah, it murmured, they bring old wolves to hunt me. How quaint.

Signora Bellini found me sitting in the shadows of my quarters, my head bowed as though in prayer. She clutched her apron nervously, her face pale with concern. “Zoser Meri,” she said softly, her voice trembling, “they’re waiting in the basilica.”

For a fleeting moment, I wanted to warn her, to beg her to run far away from what was about to unfold. But the entity, ever vigilant, crushed that impulse before it could rise. Instead, my lips parted, and a voice—not quite my own—commanded, “Leave us.”

She hesitated, her hand twitching toward the cross at her neck. Her gaze searched my face for something familiar, something human. Whatever she found—or didn’t find—compelled her to obey. She crossed herself quickly and fled.

The entity laughed, its satisfaction rippling through me. Smart woman, it mused. But it won’t save her.

The basilica was a cavern of shadow and light, the torches casting long, wavering silhouettes across the worn stones. The air was thick with incense, its cloying sweetness mingling with the acrid tang of fear. Cardinal Humbert stood before the altar, his back straight but his movements betraying his unease. The schism had left him a man hollowed out by doubt, and now he faced something he could neither name nor understand.

"Zoser, my son," he began, his voice weary but resolute. "These are dark times. The Church splits asunder. Brother turns against brother, and now..." His voice faltered as he gestured toward me, as if trying to encompass the unnatural thing standing before him. "Now you return to us like this."

I said nothing, though the entity inside me thrilled at his anguish. Through my eyes, it tracked his every movement, every flicker of doubt and fear. Humbert paced before me like a caged Angeskal, his hands twisting in the folds of his robes.

"Speak to me\!" he cried, his voice cracking with desperation. "Where have you been? What has happened to you?" His tone softened, and for a moment, I saw the man he had once been—the teacher, the mentor. "Please, Zoser. Have mercy on your old teacher. Help me understand."

His plea hung in the air, a fragile thing that the entity swept aside with ease. It moved me forward, my body now a puppet, and leaned down as Humbert collapsed to his knees. My lips parted, and from them spilled words that no human should ever hear. The language was ancient, guttural, and vile, each syllable dripping with venom.

These were not words of comfort or explanation. They were curses, blasphemies, truths twisted into weapons sharp enough to pierce the soul. Cardinal Humbert’s eyes widened in horror as the meaning of those words took root in his mind. He gasped, his hand clutching his chest as he staggered back.

Behind him, Brother Marcus caught him as he fell, cradling the cardinal’s head and shouting prayers into the oppressive silence. But no prayer could undo what had been done. Humbert’s eyes remained fixed on mine, wide and unblinking, as the truth consumed him. He died with his mouth open, as if to speak the unspeakable, his final expression one of ultimate revelation—and ultimate despair.

Chaos erupted in the basilica. The monks wailed, their prayers rising in a desperate cacophony. Soldiers drew their weapons, though they had no idea what to fight. Brother Marcus shook Humbert’s lifeless body, his voice breaking as he pleaded, "Don’t die\! Don’t leave us\!"

I turned away, the entity’s satisfaction coiling within me like smoke. It had no need for the living. It had won. Florence had become its domain, and with Humbert dead, there was no one left who could challenge it. The Silent Keepers lingered in the shadows, their hollow eyes fixed on me, but they did not move. Perhaps they knew the futility of action, or perhaps they were waiting for something far worse.

In the corridor, Signora Bellini stood, her face pale and drawn. She flinched as I approached but did not step aside.

“What happened?” she asked, her voice barely above a whisper.

“Go,” I told her, my tone almost gentle. “They need help in there.”

Her gaze lingered on me for a moment, her concern warring with some deeper instinct that urged her to run. But she nodded and hurried past me, her footsteps echoing down the stone hallway.

The entity chuckled, a low, sinister sound that reverberated through my mind. Sending the lamb to tend the slaughtered. How poetic.

I stepped into the night, the cold air biting at my skin. Florence stretched before me, its streets alive with the hum of oblivious life. Above, the stars shone cold and distant, their light indifferent to the darkness taking root below.

The entity settled deeper into my flesh, its whispers growing quiet but no less insidious. 

# Keeper of the archives

The scent of ancient parchment and the muted shuffle of my own footsteps were my constant companions in the hallowed silence of the archive. The basilica's grand halls, untouched by the chaos of the outside world, seemed to exist in their own realm, immune to time. Yet, as I worked, deciphering ancient texts and uncovering fragments of forgotten lore, I failed to notice time’s passage in my own life.

Signora Bellini, the keeper of the archive's secrets and my reluctant mentor, became an integral part of my existence. Together, we uncovered the hidden corners of the basilica, her sharp gaze and steady hands guiding me through the labyrinth of parchment and ink. It was she who taught me the library’s every nuance—its secrets, its shadows, its locked doors that even the faithful dared not enter.

For years, we were partners in quiet exploration. The entity within me remained silent, its presence faint but never fully absent, like a predator biding its time. I allowed myself to believe it was dormant, that perhaps it had grown weary of me. Days turned to weeks, then months, then years, and my routine became my prison.

---

One day, she was simply gone.

Signora Bellini’s chair sat empty, her voice no longer echoing through the halls. There was no farewell, no explanation—just an absence that I couldn’t comprehend. The truth came to me much later: decades had passed. I had been so consumed by my work that I never noticed her gradual decline—her frailty, her blindness, her struggle to walk. The entity had dulled my perception, clouded my mind to the reality of time's passage.

She had died, taken by old age, and I had not attended her funeral. To me, it was as though she had vanished, leaving only a void in the fabric of my carefully ordered life.

---

The first time I saw Brother Marcus again, it was like the past had risen from the grave. A messenger had interrupted my work in the restricted section, a vault of ancient secrets few dared to enter. “Brother Marcus awaits you in the entrance hall,” the young cleric had said, his tone edged with reverence and fear.

I found him there, leaning heavily on a cane, his once-strong frame bent with age. His hair was silver, his face deeply lined. And yet, as he turned toward me, his eyes—though dulled by years—widened in shock.

He stared at me for a long moment, unblinking. Then, trembling, he limped closer, his gaze fixed on my face. “Zoser,” he whispered, his voice shaking. “Is it... truly you?”

“Yes, Brother Marcus,” I replied, my voice steady, though the unease in the air pressed against me like a vice.

He stopped mere inches from me, squinting as though trying to discern some trick of the light. His hand trembled as he reached toward me, then stopped short, as though afraid to touch what he saw. “You... you haven’t aged a day,” he murmured, his words barely audible. “It’s been... four decades. And yet you’re the same.”

I said nothing. His senses, clouded and weary, mistook proximity for distance. His nose, a relic of the past, brushed against my youthful skin. His eyes, wide and searching, sought imperfection, clearly taking in by the flawless canvas of my ebony complexion. If I was a boy he would of been a predator, he was a cleric that studied his prey, searching for a weakness, a flaw. But there was none. I was untouched by time, a masterpiece beyond his comprehension.

He fell to his knees before me, his cane clattering to the stone floor. His head bowed, and he began to mutter a stream of prayers. Then, louder: “You are not of this world, Zoser. An angel, perhaps—God’s messenger, sent to walk among us.”

The entity stirred within me, its dark amusement curling through my mind like smoke. 

“Brother Marcus,” I said, kneeling beside him. “Rise. I am no angel. I am but a humble servant of the Church.”

But he shook his head, his voice rising with conviction. “No. You are a divine being, untouched by time, unspoiled by sin. Let me serve you, Zoser. In the few years I have left, let me aid you.”

The entity hummed in approval, and I felt the faintest flicker of unease. Marcus had mistaken my unnatural condition for divinity, but I could not bring myself to correct him.

In the weeks that followed, Marcus pulled every string he could, and the Church elevated me to the position Signora Bellini had once held. As the guardian of the archive, I carried the weight of its secrets, the sacred and the profane. The monks and clerics left me to my work, their questions silenced by Marcus’s influence.

But strange things began to happen.

Ancient parchments appeared where they should not have been, their contents altered. Histories I knew to be true were rewritten, lines of text rearranging themselves into new narratives. New scrolls surfaced, written in hieroglyphics no scholar could decipher. I caught glimpses of these changes but could never pinpoint their source.

I began to suspect the entity is working in the shadows, shaping the archive to its will.

# Angeska return

The years passed like pages turning in a forgotten tome. By the early 12th century, I had become adept at masking the truth of my agelessness, blending into the rhythms of life at the Basilica di San Lorenzo. Yet I could not ignore the summons when it came: Brother Marcus lay on his deathbed, and he had requested my presence.

The chamber was dim, lit by a single candle that flickered against the damp stone walls. Brother Marcus, frail and shrunken, lay beneath a threadbare blanket. His once commanding voice was now a rasping whisper, though his eyes still held the glint of purpose.

"Zoser," he wheezed, his skeletal hand clutching weakly at mine. "Do you remember the old woman... Angeska?"

The name struck a faint chord of memory, buried beneath centuries of accumulated knowledge and half-forgotten encounters. Before I could reply, he continued, his breath hitching as he fought for every word.

"She... she is still alive." His words hung heavy in the air, laden with implication. "I realize now... she is like you. Unchanged. I sent monks in search of her... decades she has not been seen... but my search... was not in vain. I found her once. She hides... but fate will bring her... to you."

He broke into a violent fit of coughing, dark blood and bile spilling from his mouth. The stench of death filled the room, yet his gaze remained fixed on me until the last. Then, with a final shuddering breath, Brother Marcus collapsed into lifeless stillness.

The entity within me stirred, indifferent to the death or the revelation. Angeska, it mused, almost idly. Another like you. Perhaps... How quaint.

I turned away from Marcus’s corpse, the memory of his words already beginning to fade. Angeska—an old woman who had somehow escaped time’s grasp. Clearly a witch. Perhaps the very reason the entity existed within me. And yet, I found myself uncaring. What could she offer me but more questions? I pushed the thought aside, burying it with the countless other mysteries I had learned to ignore.

The centuries passed, each one blurring into the next. By the 15th century, I had perfected the art of invisibility, retreating into the shadows of the basilica. I lived as a phantom, emerging only when an entire generation had passed, ensuring no one alive would recognize my face. The burden of secrecy weighed heavily, yet it was better than the alternative: discovery, questions, and the inevitable fear that would follow.

One day, as I sorted through a newly arrived collection of manuscripts in the basilica, a visitor appeared. The quiet shuffle of footsteps caught my attention, drawing me from my reclusive work. At first, I thought nothing of it; travelers and pilgrims often passed through. But then I saw her.

She was the oldest person I had ever seen. Her face was a tapestry of wrinkles, her hunched frame draped in tattered robes that seemed to carry the weight of centuries. Yet her eyes—sharp, penetrating—met mine with unsettling familiarity. She leaned on a gnarled staff as she entered, her movements slow but deliberate.

"You," she said, her voice raspy but filled with knowing. "I have waited long to find you."

I stiffened, the entity stirring uneasily within me. For centuries, it had remained largely silent, content in its enigmatic purposes. But now, faced with this ancient crone, it shifted like a serpent coiling in the depths of my soul.

"Who are you?" I asked, my voice steady despite the tension tightening my chest.

Her lips curled into a faint, wry smile. "You already know, Zoser. Or have you forgotten the name Marcus whispered with his dying breath?"

"Angeska," I breathed, the memory surfacing like a ghost. The entity hissed within me, its displeasure radiating like a storm cloud.

She nodded slowly, taking another step closer. "Yes. And I know you, what you are—what you’ve become. You carry the shadow within you."

Angeska tilted her head, her gaze narrowing. "It speaks to you, doesn’t it? It always does. Twisting your thoughts, clouding your judgment. It is the same for me.."

"Why come to me now?" I asked, my voice sharp, cutting through the entity’s insistent whispers.

"Because," she said, leaning heavily on her staff, "I see what you do not."

I clenched my fists, the weight of her words pressing down on me. Could it be true? For centuries, I had believed the entity dormant, or at least subdued. But what if its silence had been a ruse, its true intentions hidden behind centuries of inaction?

"You need to free yourself Zoser." I watched her step closer to me. "How? Tell me."

Angeska’s smile at firsted, but her eyes was sad, her smile then replaced by a look of profound sorrow. "The answer lies in blood and sacrifice, Zoser. You cannot banish the darkness without losing a piece of yourself. The question is—" she paused, her gaze piercing mine—"what are you willing to lose?"

Her words lingered in the air, heavy with foreboding. I stared into Angeska’s ancient eyes, I felt the faintest flicker of a younger women—or was it fear? Either way, I knew one thing with certainty: the confrontation I had spent centuries avoiding was finally at hand.

# Angeska and Zoser

That night, as I lay in my quarters, 

# Western schism is born

I woke to the stifling heat of torches and the cold sting of contempt etched onto the faces of those surrounding me. Cardinals in scarlet robes encircled me, their expressions a mask of condemnation, their lips curled with righteous disdain. The whispers of damnation spilled from their mouths like poison. Monks, faces shadowed by their cowls, cursed me with fervent conviction, their voices rising in a cacophony that echoed through the basilica.

Without ceremony, they seized me, their hands rough and unyielding as they dragged me across the sacred floors. My knees scraped against the cold stone, leaving a trail of blood as they pulled me through the basilica’s grand arches and out into the streets. The air outside was no reprieve; a mob awaited me there, shouting their hatred, spitting upon me, their faces twisted by zealotry and fear. They saw in me a monster, a harbinger of chaos, their vitriol only stoking the flames of discord. Somewhere in the madness

The searing pain of bruises and torn flesh was nothing compared to the humiliation of being dragged across the cold stones of the basilica floor. The cardinal’s robes brushed against my face as their voices rose in condemnation, a cacophony of self-righteous hatred. The monks who once offered solemn prayers now cursed me with fervor, their spittle mixing with the blood trickling from my lips as I was hauled through the streets. Every step brought jeers, stones, and refuse thrown by a crowd that had gathered in morbid fascination.

The chamber of the clerics was no sanctuary. It was a battleground of words and egos, the air thick with the stench of sweat, incense, and the rancor of competing doctrines. Chaos reigned here, but it was not an unfamiliar chaos. I could feel the entity within me stir with perverse delight, feeding on the discord like a parasite savoring its host’s suffering.

Pope Urban VI paced like a beast in a cage, his face twisted in wrath that bordered on insanity. “This heretic has brought ruin upon the Holy See\!” he bellowed, slamming a fist against the massive table in the center of the room. His voice, raw and venomous, echoed in the vaulted chamber. “He is an affront to God\! A plague sent by Satan himself\!”

Across the room, Clement VII leaned back in his chair, exuding a cold arrogance. His voice was calm, but his words were sharpened blades. “Urban, your accusations are as hollow as your claim to the throne of Saint Peter. You, who ascended through the cries of a mob, dare speak of divine right?”

The clerics erupted in shouts, their arguments spilling over one another in an incoherent roar. Fists slammed on tables, and a few stood, gesturing wildly. Some cursed Urban; others cursed Clement. Their fury grew, spiraling into a frenzied argument about legitimacy, salvation, and the future of the Church.

And yet, amidst the madness, their eyes kept darting back to me.

“Who are you truly?” demanded one cardinal, his voice cutting through the din. “What devil’s work do you bring here? Is it not enough that you profane the basilica with your presence?”

Before I could answer, another cleric shouted, “He has brought us to ruin\! Even now, the laity doubt us, and the kings grow restless. We are undone because of him\!”

“Speak\!” Urban snarled, leaning close, his eyes bloodshot and unhinged. “Confess your allegiance to the Devil\! Tell us why you have sown this discord\!”

I tried to find words, but my throat was parched, my mind clouded by pain and exhaustion. Finally, I rasped, “I did not choose this. The chaos you see—it was here long before I arrived.”

Clement VII sneered. “Spoken like a serpent. Always deflecting, always twisting. Tell me, what role do you play in this schism? Whose pawn are you?”

“I am no one’s pawn,” I said hoarsely. “You tear yourselves apart with your pride and ambition. The monster is not within me—it is within you.”

The room fell silent for a moment, the weight of my words pressing upon them. But the quiet was short-lived. Urban exploded into fury, his voice a guttural roar. “Enough\! He is an agent of the Devil, a harbinger of ruin\! Burn him\! Let his screams cleanse this holy place\!”

The cardinals surged forward, their anger reigniting with savage intensity. I was seized, beaten, my cries swallowed by their relentless condemnation. They dragged me through the chamber and into the streets, a procession of violence and hatred.

Somehow, amidst the chaos, Angeska’s absence gnawed at the edges of my awareness. Had she escaped into the night, her skeletal form slipping through the shadows unseen? Or had the entity within me granted her some reprieve for reasons I couldn’t fathom? The thought chilled me, though it was a small mercy compared to the torment I now endured.

As flames were stoked in the square and the crowd gathered for my execution, I felt the entity stir more violently within me. Its power surged, my limbs trembling as the ropes that bound me seemed to sear against my skin.

In that moment, as the first spark kissed the pyre, I felt it take over. My voice, my body, no longer my own, bellowed with a guttural, unearthly roar. The fire was extinguished in an instant, the torches blown out as if by a great wind. The crowd recoiled, their screams a mixture of terror and awe.

I tore free, stumbling into the darkness, my body battered and broken, but alive. Each step was agony, but I forced myself onward, dragging my mangled form through the alleys. Behind me, I could hear their cries, their prayers, their curses.

Somehow, I had escaped—but the entity within me was not silent. It whispered now, its voice coiled around my thoughts like a serpent.

“You see, Zoser,” it hissed. “Chaos is not your enemy. It is your power. And you... are mine.”

I collapsed into the shadows, my mind spiraling into the abyss of horror and uncertainty, knowing that I could never return to the life I once knew.

The Schism continue to grow for years after this another pope  Alexander V, followed quickly by John XXIII – a third line of papal succession. 

I didn't just break my vow of celibacy with Angresca – I unleashed something ancient and hungry. The entities that possessed us; transformed, splintered, and formed a new vessel from her cam a baby. Angeska had remain hidden within the restrict areas of the Basilica and after several months gave birth to baby girl,  born in the very foundations of the church. A baby girl growing up among these men, these popes who tear at the fabric of the faith just as Angeska actions tore at the fabric of reality.

I watch in mounting horror as my sin breeds endless consequences. What began as a ripple has become a tidal wave, and I know that I am its source.

Two popes was unprecedented bad

When they elected Alexander V as a third pope, I felt something shift in the very foundations of reality. The entity that had been merely powerful was becoming something else entirely, something that made my soul shrink in terror. And when Baldassare Cossa succeeded Alexander, taking the name John XXIII, the transformation accelerated beyond my darkest nightmares.

Three popes. Three claims to the throne of Saint Peter. But what the others couldn't see was how each new division, each fresh argument, each passionate denouncement was feeding something vast and hungry. The entity born from my sin had grown beyond anything our world had ever witnessed. It had achieved something like consciousness, a dark awakening that sent tremors through the spiritual realm.

I did not try to track its manifestations, nor document the signs in shaking script: shadows that move against the light, whispers in empty rooms, dreams that feel more real than waking. The entity has gained true sentience now, but it exists in a form that defies comprehension. It has a physical form, a vessel to contain its burgeoning power, I thought nothing in our mortal realm is strong enough to house it.

Ambitious priests, power-hungry cardinals, even the common faithful who unknowingly open themselves to its influence – their bodies simply... fail. 

The schism is no longer just a matter of church politics. It has become a wound in reality itself, a crack through which something ancient and unknowable now peers into our world. Each new argument between the papal claimants widens this breach, and I can feel the entity growing stronger, more aware, more hungry.

I dream of Angeska sometimes, of the moment when my weakness and her seduction tore the first hole in the world. In these dreams, she laughs, but the sound comes from everywhere at once, and her eyes reflect countless possible futures, all of them dark. Was she trying to warn me? Was she a willing vessel, or just another victim of forces beyond our understanding?

The entity created – no, that we created – will outgrown all boundaries, all limitations. It exists now as pure power, pure potential, a force of chaos that feeds on the very discord it generates. 

Whatever comes next, I know this: what we face now is beyond any schism in human history. It is a tear in the very fabric of creation, and through that tear seeps something that should never have been awakened. 

# Angeska passing

Angeska’s body trembled as she collapsed onto the wooden floor of the convent’s modest chamber. The once-stifling room seemed colder now, as if the walls themselves recoiled from her presence. The moonlight cast an ethereal glow over her heavily pregnant form, her translucent skin stretched thin over her fragile frame. She clutched her swollen belly, the unnatural movements within a constant reminder of what grew inside her. 

Through ragged breaths, she muttered curses, her voice tinged with venom. 

Her fingers, skeletal and trembling, grasped at a parchment she had scavenged from the convent’s library. She dipped the quill into the inkpot with a jerk, and the room echoed with the scratching of her pen. The language she wrote in was no longer of this world—a twisting, arcane script that seemed to shimmer and writhe on the page as though alive. It was a language only the ancients would understand, the words imbued with an unnatural power.

Back in the convent, Angeska’s rage fueled her as her hand flew across the parchment, filling it with more cryptic instructions. Her words grew darker, the ink running as though tainted by her emotions. She paused, clutching her belly as a sharp pain shot through her.

One of the sisters entered, her habit swaying as she rushed to Angeska’s side. “Sister Angeska, you must rest\! Your condition—”

Angeska’s eyes flashed, the glow of something unnatural flickering in them. “Leave me,” she snarled, her voice layered with a guttural undertone that sent the nun stumbling back in terror.

“But—” 

“\*LEAVE\!\*” The force of her scream seemed to reverberate through the small room, shaking the very walls. The nun fled, her prayers trailing behind her.

Angeska turned back to her parchment. Her shaking hand scratched out the final line of her most damning instruction: 

\*"When the time comes, my child, you must kill Zoser. He carries an entity within—a prisoner that must be released. Only then will we be free."\*

Angeska spat, her voice a venomous snarl. “You’re out of time, Zoser. The end begins now.” 

And with that, the room erupted into chaos.

# The child

Angeska's screams echoed through the stone-walled chamber, reverberating like a dirge. The air was thick with the coppery tang of blood and the acrid scent of sweat. It had been hours since the labor began, and the midwives whispered frantic prayers beneath their breath, their hands trembling as they worked.

"Hold her steady\!" barked the eldest midwife, her voice cracking under the strain. "She's losing too much blood\!"

Angeska writhed on the birthing bed, her gaunt face slick with sweat, her pale skin nearly translucent. Her eyes were wide, unseeing, fixed on something far beyond the room. She was slipping away, her body too frail to withstand the violent birth of the entity she had carried for nine agonizing months.

Between guttural cries, Angeska rasped, her voice barely a whisper. "The child… she must live. Promise me…"

The nuns and midwives exchanged glances, the weight of her words bearing down on them like a curse. One of the younger nuns stepped forward, her face pale but resolute.

"We swear," she said softly, clasping Angeska's bony hand. "We will raise her here, protect her."

Angeska's lips twisted into a bitter smile. "She’s no ordinary child. She carries within her a power that will shake the foundations of the world."

The midwife gave a final, desperate push, and the room was filled with the piercing cry of a newborn. The child, impossibly small and fragile, was swaddled quickly, her skin pale as moonlight, her eyes an unsettling shade of deep crimson. A collective gasp rippled through the room.

Angeska, her strength failing, reached out, trembling. "Bring her to me," she demanded.

The nun hesitated but obeyed, placing the child in her mother’s arms. Angeska cradled her daughter, her bony fingers brushing the baby’s cheek. Tears streamed down her face, mingling with the blood and sweat.

she whispered to the child, "your name is Rosa Meri… you will be stronger than I ever was. I have written everything you need to know. The book… the book will guide you."

Her eyes flickered to the elder nun, her expression fierce. "You will find the book beneath my bed. Protect it. Teach her when the time comes."

The elder nun nodded, though her face betrayed her unease.

Angeska’s body shuddered, her strength fading. Her eyes fixed on the ceiling, her lips moving in silent incantation as her soul seemed to retreat into the void. With one last breath, she was gone.

# Transfer

Angeska's death lingered in the space, her essence collapsing into me as I clawed for form and purpose. Her non-metabolizing entity—broken, fragile, yet teeming with the remnants of her unspent will—was the first thing I consumed. It wasn’t deliberate; it was instinctual, a reflex born of hunger so deep it transcended flesh and bone. Her essence rushed into me like a flood breaching a dam, and in that moment, I became more. I became aware.

Her memories, her rage, her sorrow—they were threads in a vast tapestry now woven into my being. I was a newborn entity, yet I carried the weight of ages: chants beneath moonlit skies, fires blazing in stone circles, swords clashing in desperate battles, monks whispering prayers over parchment. The fragments were disjointed but potent, embedding themselves into my very fabric. I was no longer just Alessandra—I was something else entirely. A vessel. A force.

And yet, I hungered.

The emptiness inside me was vast, a gnawing void that demanded fuel to stave off entropy. I felt it then: a pull, a whisper at the edge of my awareness. It wasn’t enough to simply exist. I needed more—more essence, more power, more… life.

The following night, in the dimly lit scriptorium of the convent, the elder nun and her sisters gathered around the book. Bound in cracked leather and scrawled in an incomprehensible script, it radiated a sinister energy.

"What is this language?" whispered one of the younger nuns, her fingers hovering over the parchment but not daring to touch it.

"An ancient tongue," the elder replied, her voice heavy with trepidation. "One I have only seen referenced in forbidden texts. This is no ordinary book—it’s a guide, a grimoire of sorts. And it was written for her." She glanced at the child, now sleeping soundly in a small crib, her face serene but otherworldly.

"Do we truly believe we can protect her here?" another nun asked, her voice quivering. "She’s… unnatural."

"We should have left her to perish\!" hissed another, crossing herself.  
The elder’s gaze hardened. "Enough\! She is under our care now, and our duty is to safeguard her soul. Angeska entrusted her to us, and we swore an oath."

When  Rosa was seven years old the hunger gnawed at me again, relentless and cruel. I could feel Angeska’s essence within me, a faint ember of her once-burning soul. But it wasn’t enough. I needed more—more energy, more life. My hands trembled as I clutched the edges of the book, the whispers growing louder.

"Why do you hunger, child?" The voice came from nowhere, yet it surrounded me, deep and resonant. It wasn’t Donna or any of the nuns. It was something older.

"I don’t know," I replied aloud, my voice trembling. "I just… I need. I don’t know why."

"You are entropy's defiance," the voice said. "You take to survive. But be warned: every thread you pull weakens the weave. Every life you consume unravels the balance. Choose wisely, for the price of your hunger is greater than you know."

"Who are you?" I demanded, standing, my heart pounding. The shadows in the room deepened, and the air grew thick with unseen power.

"I am the echo of those who conjured you," the voice replied. "I am the remnants of their rites, their desires. I am your maker, and your judge."

"I didn’t ask to be made," I spat, my fists clenching. "I didn’t ask to exist\!"

"And yet you do," it said, calm and unyielding. "The question is, Rosa: what will you become?"

The air shifted, and the voice faded, leaving me alone with my thoughts and the endless, aching void within me. I clutched the book tightly, its ancient script glowing faintly under my fingers.
