The nuns who raised Rosa were kind, though I felt their fear like the soft brush of a blade against my skin. They saw the way the candles flickered when I entered a room, how shadows grew longer in my presence. They whispered behind closed doors, their voices trembling with a mix of awe and dread.

One evening, Sister Beatrice, the eldest of them, approached me as I sat alone in the dim light of the scriptorium. My mother’s book lay open before me, the ancient script flowing into my mind as easily as spoken words.

"Rosa Meri," she said, her voice hesitant. "What do you see in those pages?"

I didn’t look up. My fingers traced the faded ink, feeling the power it carried. "It’s not what I see, Sister," I replied, my voice calm but distant. "It’s what I hear."

Her breath caught. "What… what do you hear?"

"Whispers," I said, tilting my head. "From the past. From the void. They tell me of what came before, of what lies ahead. They tell me about him."

Her face paled. "Him?"

"My father," I said, the word bitter on my tongue though I had never met the man.

# The Book

The years passed like whispers carried on an unholy wind. By 1450, I had grown, though my presence seemed to unsettle all who crossed my path. At twelve, I could decipher the incomprehensible script of my mother’s book as though it were written in the simplest tongue. The nuns at the convent whispered behind my back, their hushed voices heavy with fear. They spoke of strange happenings—candles flickering when no breeze stirred, shadows moving with a mind of their own, and whispers that slithered through the dead of night. But to me, these things were as natural as breath. My crimson eyes saw the world differently, curious rather than alarmed.

One shadowed evening, as I sat in my chamber poring over the ancient tome, the elder nun entered. Her face was pale, drawn tight with unease. The dim light threw sharp lines across her weathered features, making her look more specter than woman.

“Rosa,” she began cautiously, her voice a quiver of trepidation, “what… what do you see in that book?”

I lifted my gaze from the pages, the firelight catching a glint in my eyes that made her shudder. “It is my mother’s gift,” I replied softly, each word deliberate, each syllable weighted. “She said it would teach me what I need to know.”

The nun stepped closer, but her breath hitched as though the air between us had thickened with an unseen presence. “Teach you what?” she pressed, though her voice betrayed her dread.

A smile curved my lips—not one of cruelty, but of knowing. “Everything.”

The atmosphere in the room shifted, as if the very shadows leaned in to listen. The nun’s trembling hands formed the sign of the cross, but her faith seemed fragile, like frost under a relentless sun.

# Rosa Art

I remember the day everything changed \- not just for me, but for all of Florence. It was the spring of 1450, and I was sitting in nunery courtyard, sketching the way sunlight dappled through the lemon trees. “Rosa, you going to start a revolution\!” the nuns would say.

The nuns had always encouraged my curiosity, unlike many others who believed girls should focus solely on domestic duties. I had my mothers book which secretly shared with me the ancient texts she'd collected \- worn copies of Aristotle, Plato, and other philosophers whose words set my mind aflame. While other children played in the streets, I spent hours poring over these forbidden treasures, teaching myself Greek and Latin just to understand their wisdom.

The plague had taken many, and perhaps that's what made me so determined to find beauty in a world that seemed intent on darkness. The Holy Wars had left their scars on our city, and everywhere I looked, I saw people hungry for something more than mere survival.

I began drawing what I imagined while reading those ancient texts \- not just copying the world as it was, but as it could be. My sketches combined the mathematical precision I'd learned from studying Aristotle with the human grace I observed in everyday life. I drew people as the center of their own stories, not merely as servants to divine will.

Word spread slowly at first. A visiting merchant saw one of my drawings and asked to share it with his colleagues in Venice. A scholar passing through noticed how I'd incorporated Golden Ratio principles into my designs and began discussing them with other intellectuals. Without intending to, my humble courtyard became a salon where visitors from across Italy \- and soon, Europe \- would gather to discuss art, philosophy, and the nature of humanity itself.

"The girl understands proportion like the ancients did," I'd hear them whisper. "But she adds something new \- something alive." They didn't know that I'd spent countless hours studying not just the old texts, but the way real people moved and lived and dreamed.

What began with art soon spread to other realms. Architects studying my drawings began incorporating similar principles into their building designs. Writers visited our courtyard and left inspired to create works that celebrated human achievement rather than just divine providence. Even politicians, drawn by the growing reputation of our gatherings, began to reimagine how a city could be governed with human dignity at its center.

I never saw myself as special \- merely curious. While others spoke of the failures of the Holy Wars or trembled at memories of the Black Death, I kept drawing, kept reading, kept asking questions. "Why do we separate the divine from the human?" I would ask during our courtyard discussions. "Didn't God give us minds to think, hands to create, hearts to love beauty?"

The nuns watched it all with quiet pride, though I could see the worry in their eyes. Change, even beautiful change, brings its own dangers. 

# \# The Girl Who Saw Beyond

The nuns never understood why I would spend hours studying the way light fell through the cloister windows, or why I filled every scrap of parchment I could find with drawings of the golden spirals I saw in everything from flower petals to the way birds circled in flight. I was different, they whispered. Strange little Rosa, the orphan who spoke to numbers and saw music in mathematics.

"Child, your mind wanders during prayers again," Sister Maria would scold, but her eyes were kind. She was the one who first sneaked me the ancient texts she'd discovered in the convent's dusty library \- worn copies of Aristotle and Plato that would change my life forever.

I was fifteen when everything changed. I had been drawing in the convent garden, working on my theory of how everything in nature followed the same mathematical patterns. A renowned philosopher from Venice had come to deliver supplies, and he happened to glance over my shoulder.

"Impossible," he whispered, making me jump. "These calculations... these observations... who taught you this?"

I clutched my drawings to my chest. "No one, sir. I just... see them. The patterns are everywhere if you know how to look."

That was the day my quiet life ended. Within weeks, word had spread about the orphan girl who understood the language of creation itself. They began arriving at the convent gates \- artists seeking to understand my theories of perspective, engineers fascinated by my architectural drawings, philosophers eager to discuss how my observations connected to ancient wisdom.

"You cannot keep her hidden away forever," I overheard a visiting scholar tell Sister Maria. "Her mind is a gift that could reshape our understanding of everything."

Sister Maria sighed. "She is but a child."

"A child who sees what the greatest minds of our age have missed," he replied.

\---

I remember the first class I taught, if you could call it that. The convent had cleared out an old storage room for me, and a dozen eager faces waited to learn my secrets. My hands trembled as I began to draw.

"Look here," I said, sketching a nautilus shell. "See how it spirals? Now watch." I drew a series of rectangles, showing how they followed the same mathematical sequence. "This pattern appears everywhere \- in the arrangement of leaves on a stem, in the way water swirls, even in the proportions of your own body."

"But how did you discover this?" asked a young architect from Florence.

I smiled. "I didn't discover it. I simply learned to see what was always there. Here, let me show you..."

\---

Days turned to weeks, weeks to months. My small room became a sanctuary of learning, its walls covered in drawings and calculations. Artists learned to see the world through new eyes, engineers discovered principles that would revolutionize architecture, and philosophers found fresh ways to understand humanity's place in the natural world.

But it wasn't always easy. Late at night, when my visitors had gone, doubts would creep in. I was still that orphan girl, after all. Who was I to challenge centuries of accepted wisdom?

"You look troubled," Lorenzo, my most dedicated student, said one evening as he helped me organize my drawings.

"Sometimes I wonder if I'm seeing patterns that aren't really there," I confessed. "The Black Death took my parents before I could even remember them. The world can seem so chaotic, so random. What if I'm just desperately trying to find order in chaos?"

He picked up one of my drawings \- a study of how light behaves when it passes through water. "Rosa, do you know what makes you different from all the scholars who visit here? They seek to impose their understanding on the world. You simply observe what is already there and help others see it too. That's why your ideas are spreading across Italy like wildfire."

He was right. Reports came from Venice of artists using my techniques to create paintings with perfect perspective. Architects in Rome were applying my proportional theories to design buildings of unprecedented grace. Even merchants spoke of how my mathematical principles helped them better manage their trades.

\---

Years passed, and my little room in the convent became known as the cradle of a new way of thinking. They called it a Renaissance \- a rebirth of knowledge and understanding. But for me, it was simply about sharing what I'd always seen.

One spring morning, as I watched a new group of students arrive through the convent gates, Sister Maria joined me.

"Do you ever regret it?" she asked. "Letting the world into your quiet life?"

I thought of all the drawings now circulating throughout Europe, the buildings being built using my principles, the artists and scholars taking these ideas to places I'd never see.

"How can I regret sharing light?" I replied. "Besides, the convent will always be my home. You gave an orphan girl more than shelter \- you gave her the freedom to see the world as it truly is."

I am Rosa, and this is how I learned that the greatest discoveries don't always come from the most obvious places. Sometimes they come from a quiet girl in a convent, who simply learned to see the patterns in God's creation and had the courage to share them with the world.

\---

That evening, as my students departed and the sun set over the convent walls, I added another page to my ever-growing collection of observations. Tomorrow would bring new visitors, new questions, new discoveries. But for now, I was content, knowing that the patterns I saw as a lonely orphan were helping to reshape the world, one drawing at a time.

# Rosa taken to pope

Word of my work spread like a summer fire across Italy. What began as quiet teachings in a convent storage room had grown into something the world could no longer ignore. They said fishermen in Venice were using my mathematical principles to plot safer routes through treacherous waters. Merchants in Florence applied my theories of proportion to design more efficient storage systems. Even farmers had begun using my observations about natural patterns to improve their crop rotations.

It was the architects, though, who brought everything to a head.

"Look what they're building in Milan\!" Lorenzo burst into my study one morning, unfurling plans across my desk. "They're using your principles of divine proportion for the entire cathedral design. Rosa, they're reshaping the very way we build churches\!"

I traced the elegant lines with trembling fingers. My simple observations about nature's patterns were now reaching toward heaven in stone and glass. It was beautiful, and it was dangerous.

Sister Maria appeared at my door, her face grave. "Rosa, there are men here from Rome."

\---

The journey to Vatican City was long, but it gave me time to think. I wasn't afraid, exactly. Truth, I had learned, has its own kind of power. Still, as our carriage approached the papal palace, I clutched my worn leather portfolio closer.

"Remember," Lorenzo whispered beside me, "they wouldn't have summoned you if they merely wanted to silence you. The Pope himself requested your presence."

The great hall fell silent as I entered. Pope Nicholas V sat before me, surrounded by his advisors. But what caught my eye were the walls \- covered in artwork that followed patterns I had documented years ago in my convent room.

"So," the Pope's voice echoed, "you are the orphan girl who sees God's mathematics in all things?"

I stepped forward, my voice steadier than I felt. "Your Holiness, I simply observe what the Creator has written in nature itself."

"Show me."

With shaking hands, I opened my portfolio. "Look here \- the way a falcon's flight follows the same curve as a breaking wave. And here \- how the branching of trees mirrors the flowing of blood in our veins. All of creation speaks the same language, if we have eyes to see it."

"Your ideas are causing quite a stir," one cardinal interjected. "Common craftsmen now claim to understand divine principles. Merchants speak of holy geometry as if it were market arithmetic."

"Knowledge belongs to all who seek it," I replied, then added quickly, "just as God's grace does."

The Pope leaned forward, studying my drawings with intense interest. "They say you learned this from ancient pagan texts."

"I learned to read from those texts, Your Holiness, but what I see comes from observing God's creation itself. The ancients saw these patterns too \- because truth doesn't change, it only waits to be rediscovered."

An hour passed, then two. The Pope asked question after question, not just about my drawings but about their implications. How did these patterns apply to music? To the movement of stars? To the growth of cities?

Finally, he rose. The hall held its breath.

"What this girl has discovered," he announced, "is not heresy but harmony. She has found a new way to appreciate the perfection of God's design." He turned to me. "You will stay in Rome. We will build you a proper academy here, under the Church's protection. Your insights could help us build churches that better reflect divine glory."

.

# Rosa Academy

In my assigned chamber in the Vatican, I couldn't sleep. The patterns I saw in nature had always shown me harmony, the delicate balance of all things. But the Pope saw them as tools for conquest, for imposing one truth over all others.

When I saw popes plans I saw glimpsed a future I never imagined. My work, meant to reveal the beauty in all things, could be twisted into something terrible. Knowledge is like light \- it can illuminate or it can blind. What responsibility do we bear for the shadows our discoveries cast?

"Rosa? What's wrong? You look like you've seen a ghost."

"Worse," I whispered. "I've seen what's coming." I showed him my notes from the Pope's conversation, carefully encoded in the mathematical symbols only we understood. "We have to be more careful now. The patterns I see \- they can't become tools for..."

                                                                                                                                                                                                                           

I couldn't finish the sentence. Lorenzo read my notes, his face growing grave.

"What will you do?"

I looked out the window at Rome stirring to life below. "Keep teaching. But differently now. Every student must understand that these patterns belong to all of creation, not to any one power. That true harmony comes from understanding, not control."

In my school, Rosa academy, I taught the fuller truth: that the greatest pattern in creation is its diversity, and that true wisdom lies not in domination, but in understanding how all things connect in their own unique way.

# Nicolas V

The room reeked of desperation and incense, a cloying perfume meant to disguise the rot of power that had festered here for centuries. I slipped into the shadows, unnoticed as I always was, a ghost among the living, watching their ambitions writhe like worms in a carcass. Pope Nicholas VI sat at the head of the room, his gilded throne more like a cage for a soul too deformed to escape. Around him, the cardinals clustered like vultures, their crimson robes blending into the darkness until they resembled bloodstains soaked into the stone.

The whispers grew louder in the halls of power. I was called to an audience with Pope Nicholas V, a man whose fractured mind teetered on the edge of salvation and damnation. Reports had reached his ears of charred villages and Moorish banners flying over the ruins of southern Spain and Portugal. The Moors warriors from Africa—were conquering with a ferocity that struck terror into Christian hearts.

The Pope, his lips twisted into a grin so grotesque it could have frightened gargoyles off the cathedral. The cardinals exchanged uneasy glances. “A Papal Bull will be written—no, etched into eternity—to make it clear: we are here to purge the darkness. And by darkness, I mean anyone who doesn’t glow under the midday sun.”

The room fell silent, save for the faint wheeze of one ancient cardinal who clearly regretted living long enough to hear this conversation.

Nicholas stood, pacing the length of the chamber like a madman caught in the ecstasy of his own genius. 

The cardinals muttered amongst themselves, their unease palpable. One, plump and sweating profusely, cleared his throat. “But, Your Holiness, what of the optics?”

Nicholas froze, his head snapping toward the man like a wolf spotting prey. “Optics?” His voice dripped with mockery. “The optics? Oh, forgive me, I didn’t realize we were running a theater troupe and not the Holy Roman Church. Shall  I order matching costumes for the Inquisitors? Perhaps something slimming in red and black?”

The cardinal shrank under the Pope’s gaze, his jowls quivering like jelly on a trembling plate. Nicholas sneered. “The optics,” he continued, his voice dropping to a venomous hiss, “will be perfect. The people crave salvation. Who am I to deny them?”

“And what of the… logistics?” a skeletal cardinal ventured cautiously.

“Oh, you mean the enslaving, pillaging, and systemic destruction of an entire population?” Nicholas replied, his tone sickly sweet. “Don’t worry, we’ll dress it up nicely. ‘Perpetual servitude,’ I’ll call it. Has a good ring to it, don’t you think? Like a hymn you can’t stop humming, even as it drives you mad.”

Another cardinal spoke, his voice trembling. “But surely, Your Holiness, this must end someday. Surely we cannot—”

“Someday?” Nicholas interrupted, throwing his head back with a laugh so twisted it made the candles flicker.

The cardinals nodded, their compliance dripping with cowardice. Nicholas returned to his throne, slumping into it with the satisfaction of a butcher after a day’s work. “Draft it,” he barked. “The Bull shall be written tonight. I want it ready to be proclaimed at dawn. And make sure the ink is dark—darker than their skin, just to drive the point home.”

The chamber fell silent, the weight of my words sinking in. Nicholas’s laughter broke the stillness, a sound so unholy it seemed to echo from the depths of Hell itself.

“Write it,” he commanded, his eyes never leaving mine. “And let their world burn.”

# 

# Black cardinals

The Pope’s proclamation had hit the room like a lightning bolt, but the expression on his face when he finally noticed. The air in the chamber shifted, thickening with a tension so sharp it could cut flesh.

It started with a cough. One of the cardinals, a man whose complexion bore the deep, rich tones of the southern sun, cleared his throat. “Your Holiness,” he began, his voice calm but trembling with suppressed rage, “do I… understand correctly? Perpetual servitude for all Moors? Including those… within these walls?”

Pope Nicholas froze mid-smirk, his expression cracking like poorly fired pottery. His eyes darted around the room, finally registering what had been hidden in plain sight. Half the cardinals surrounding him—those he had trusted, dined with, even confessed his sins to—were dark-skinned.

“You,” he stammered, pointing a trembling finger at the speaker. “You’re… not—”

“A Moor?” the cardinal supplied, his voice as sharp as a blade. “Oh, I am, and I’m also a member of the Ethiopian monks in the Council of Florence, and proudly so.”

Nicholas’s mouth opened and closed like a fish gasping for air. “But you’re a man of God\! Surely that—”

“Exempts me?” Another cardinal stepped forward, his deep voice rumbling through the chamber. “No, Your Holiness. According to your new Papal Bull, my faith means nothing. My skin, however, damns me.”

“The Nubian Church is part of us. You can not do this, it will destroy christian Nubian kingdoms\!”

The Pope, his face a mask of pale horror, scrambled for an argument, but the logic of his own madness had trapped him. “This… this is a misunderstanding\!” he sputtered, sweat beading on his brow. “You—you’re not the kind of Moors I meant\! I was referring to the ones… the other ones out there\!”

The first cardinal raised an eyebrow, his tone laced with venom. “Ah, I see. So we’re only slightly damned? Perhaps you’d like to clarify which shade of brown is acceptable to you, Your Holiness. Shall we bring a color chart to the next conclave?”

The room erupted into a cacophony of voices, the dark-skinned cardinals shouting accusations while their lighter counterparts shrank into their robes, clearly horrified to have been caught in such a debacle. Nicholas clutched the edges of his throne, his eyes darting around like a cornered rat.

And then, as if struck by divine—or perhaps infernal—inspiration, he stood, raising his hands for silence. “ENOUGH\!” he roared. The room fell quiet, the tension palpable.

“I am the Pope,” he declared, his voice trembling but still carrying the weight of his office. “And I will lead by example. If this Bull decrees the enslavement of all Moors… then so be it. I will not exempt even this holy chamber.”

Nicholas shot me a glare that could have boiled holy water. “Yes, Rosa,” he spat. “If this is God’s will, then I shall obey it, no matter the cost.”

The absurdity of the moment reached new heights as Nicholas began issuing commands. “Guards\!” he bellowed. “Bring chains\! Bind these men as an example of our unwavering commitment to the Lord’s decree.”

The guards hesitated, glancing nervously at the cardinals, many of whom were their spiritual mentors. One finally whispered, “Your Holiness… are you certain?”

“DO IT\!” Nicholas shrieked, his voice cracking. “Or shall I enslave you too?”

The guards reluctantly moved forward, their faces etched with disbelief. The cardinals resisted at first, but their holy vows forbade them from striking back. Chains clinked and rattled, the sound echoing like a dirge through the chamber. One by one, the Moors among them were shackled, their dignity intact despite the Pope’s ridiculous display.

As the last chain was secured, Nicholas turned to the remaining cardinals. “Let this be a testament to our faith,” he proclaimed, his voice shaking with what he must have thought was righteous authority. “No one is above God’s law—not even His most devout servants.”

Nicholas glared at me, his face redder than the cardinal robes he had just symbolically desecrated. “You have no idea of the burden I carry.”

As the chained cardinals were led out of the chamber, their heads held high despite their indignity, the remaining clergy watched in stunned silence. Nicholas slumped back into his throne, visibly shaken but still clinging to his delusions of grandeur.

His eyes fixed on the door where the last of the chained cardinals disappeared. The whispers in the shadows seemed like whisper laced with poison chanting in harmony to the chaos Pope Nicholas V had unleashed.

# Declaration 

 I stood at the periphery, my heart pounding against my ribs as though it sought escape. My skin, pale enough to pass, felt like a fragile mask, and I cursed the fortune that spared me from suspicion—for now.

The messengers had arrived like raven birds, bearing tidings written in black blood ink. Their faces were grim, their eyes hollow, as though they too understood the monstrous weight of what they carried. One by one, the papal bulls were read aloud. Each word cut deeper than any blade, their syllables oiled with righteousness and cruelty.

Pope Nicholas sat at the head of the chamber, his thin smile a grotesque parody of divine benevolence. I could see the fervor in his eyes, the madness that had taken root in his soul, feeding on the whispers he so blindly obeyed.

I shifted uneasily, and the entity within—one gifted by my mother’s forbidden arts—fixating on the seals. They seemed to pulse with an unnatural light, as though the documents themselves had absorbed the malevolence of their intent.

The emissary stepped forward, his voice clear and commanding, every word a hammer driving nails into the coffin of freedom. “The Holy Father grants full and free permission to invade Africa, to search out its people, to capture all Moors wherever they are found in the world, and to subjugate and reduce them to perpetual slavery.” His lips was thin, razor-sharp. “All authority now flows from the Pope.”

A chill swept through the room, not of cold but of dread. My breath caught in my throat as I glanced around. The cardinals sat rigid, their faces masks of dutiful acceptance. Only a few, those with mixed heritage moors had somehow avoided the chains of Nicholas’s earlier madness, shifted uncomfortably. Their hands tightened on the edges of their robes, their silence heavy with fear and anger. 

“The old gods must bow before the new order,” the emissary continued, his voice rising. “It is written in Romanus Pontifex: all lands, all peoples, all kingdoms shall submit.”

I clenched my fists, nails biting into my palms as I fought to remain composed. These weren’t just words. They were instruments of destruction, crafted to obliterate civilizations, to erase histories and enslave futures. My father’s people were being condemned, their existence rewritten.

“This is how it begins,” I whispered to myself, my voice barely audible. The words tasted like prophecy, bitter and inevitable. I could feel the papal bulls spreading their influence already, like ink in water. They would seep into every corner of the world, staining it with their twisted hierarchies and cruel logic.

The pope turned to the emissary. “Carry these declarations to King Afonso V of Portugal  and then let the word spread to  every corner of Christendom. Let the world know the will of God.”

The emissary bowed and departed, his footsteps echoing like a death knell. “We march forward into the light, leaving the darkness behind\!”

But as their words echoed through the chamber, I could feel an encroaching shadow, its presence swelling and enveloping, and the whispers seemed to pulse with a life of its own, growing, not receding. expanding and consuming, as if it sought to claim every fragment of light and hope. It was a relentless force, an ever-growing abyss that threatened to engulf everything in its path. It was in the ink, in the parchment, in the very foundations of the Church. And it was watching.

As was I.

# King Alfonso realm

The winds off the Tagus River carried the scent of salt and something fouler, something heavy with the iron tang of coming war. I , Zoser, stood at the edge of Lisbon, where the sea seemed to merge with the sky, endless and ominous. Behind me, the streets murmured with restless energy—rumors of the Papal Bull spread like wildfire. They called it Dum Diversas, but I knew it by another name: The essence of chaos, turmoil, and anarchy. Its presence was a tempestuous force, a blend of malevolence and power, a master of mayhem and conflict, forever etching its mark upon the heavens and the barren sands.  It cloaked greed, conquest, and enslavement in the garb of a malevolent sacred purpose.

I stood in the shadows, unseen but ever watchful, as the Portuguese captain unfurled maps before the young king. His fingers traced routes to Africa, to lands teeming with riches and people—peoples he dismissed with a wave of his hand.

“Your Majesty,” the captain began, his voice heavy with zeal and cunning, “with the Pope's blessing, we can establish a new order. These tribal peoples have no true kings, no true gods. They wander in darkness, ripe for salvation... and submission.”

King Afonso V leaned forward, his eyes alight, not with divine purpose, but with the glint of gold and glory. The papal bull rested on a pedestal beside him, its words as dangerous as any blade. “Prepare the ships,” he commanded, his tone brooking no dissent. “This is no mere conquest; this is a holy mission. We will bring light to the heathens—and riches to our coffers.”

The room erupted in applause and cries of agreement, but I saw the threads of fate twisting tighter around King Afonso, binding him to a path soaked in blood. My falcon eye burned with the weight of what was to come. The papal bull had become more than parchment; it was a curse, a declaration of darkness cloaked in holy words.

As the court dispersed, I followed in silence, slipping into the port where the ships were being prepared. Priests blessed the vessels with holy water while merchants tallied the gold spent on chains and weapons. I saw a cardinal addressing the sailors, his voice a low hum of doctrine wrapped in venom.

The rhythmic clatter of boots on cobblestones echoed in the air, their formation breaking into a semi-circle around me. Portuguese soldiers, their armor gleaming in the twilight, held parchment unfurled before them. The Papal Bull. Its words dripped with the authority of Rome, but my falcon eye saw what lay beneath the ink—sprawling webs of greed and blood.

From behind, a voice rang out young and imperious, echoing through the hall. “A moor, a moor\!”, a young priest stepped forth, flanked by guards.

My blood boiled, but I held my composure. I was no stranger to the violence of words disguised as piety.   With a turn of my head, I met their gaze, a silent challenge in the face of impending storm.

The first soldier lunged, and with a flick of my wrist, he was hurled backward by an invisible force, crashing against the cobblestones. The others hesitated, their fear palpable now.

But the captain pressed forward, drawing his sword. “This is heresy\!” he shouted. “You defy God\!”

“No,” I said, my voice low but filled with a terrible finality. “I defy men who pretend to speak for God.”  

 

The priest staggered back, his face pale as my eye bore into him. 

“Step aside,” he commanded his soldiers, his tone imperious.  

“This man will answer to me, he is possessed with a demon\!”

The air around us grew heavy, the sea itself seeming to still as the shadows thickened. The priests murmured prayers, clutching their rosaries like talismans. 

Defiantly I spoke, “The darkness you unleash will consume more than lands, It will extinguish the light of hope and spread a blanket of despair across the globe.”

“Enough of your riddles, Zoser,” he snapped. “The Church has given me the mandate to conquer and civilize. Stand down, or I will have you executed where you stand.”

His face a mixture of confusion and terror. The young priest regained his composure, pointing a trembling finger at me. “You are a heretic\! A servant of the devil\!” he raised his hand. “Guards\! Seize him\!”

The soldiers advanced, swords drawn, but I raised my hands, and the shadows surged. The air crackled with ancient energy as the ground trembled beneath us. The soldiers froze, their courage faltering as the shadows coiled like serpents, hissing with voices from beyond time.

The shadows erupted, enveloping the port in a maelstrom of chaos. The guards fell back, their weapons clattering to the ground. 

“This is not over, Zoser,” he said, his voice trembling but defiant. “You cannot stop destiny.”

“No,” I replied, the shadows retreating as I stepped into the light once more. “But I can remind you of its cost.”

# 

# Moor Wars

The night was heavy with the scent of smoke and fear as I ran through the woods, my feet pounding against the damp earth. They were coming for me—soldiers with their cold iron chains, shouting orders, and their cruel, righteous laughter. My heart thundered in my chest as I stumbled over roots and stones, gasping for breath.

The campaign against us Moors had begun with terrifying swiftness. Their armies swept through our lands like a merciless storm, capturing villages, tearing apart families, and dragging my people into bondage. They claimed it was God’s will. “This is for the glory of the Church\!” they’d shout, waving their banners high, their pale faces twisted with fanaticism.

But I knew better. There was something behind their zeal—something dark. The whispers.

I’d heard them, faint at first, but persistent. They came not from men, but from the shadows. They wormed their way into the minds of leaders and priests, feeding their delusions. I’d seen it in their eyes, the glassy glaze of those who believed their suffering was a small price for the paradise promised by the Church. They were blind. They didn’t see how the whispers thrived on their cruelty.

And the whispers had other plans.

When they chained my people and forced them into fields and quarries, the land rebelled. I saw it with my own eyes—the soil beneath their feet grew brittle and lifeless. Seeds they planted withered before sprouting. Rivers they toiled to channel broke free, flooding entire villages in waves of choking mud. It was as if the earth itself was repelling their conquest.

Then the sickness came. First, it was among us, the enslaved. Skin broke out in weeping sores, and blood coughed from cracked lips. But it didn’t stop there. The plague leapt to the soldiers, their captains, their priests. Soon, it spread to the villagers who had once cheered for our capture.

I remember the stench of the dead—bodies piled high, choking the air with decay. Entire regions fell silent, consumed by pestilence and famine. Yet still, the whispers spoke.

“This is the cost of greatness,” they hissed in my mind, a venomous caress. “Do not falter now.”

Falter? I laughed bitterly, clutching my side as I hid in the hollow of a fallen tree. They spoke as if I had a choice in the matter. My only concern was survival. But I couldn’t deny the truth. The whispers weren’t done. Their insidious plans were far from over.

I heard rumors as I passed through shadowed villages. Laborers, they said, were dying faster than they could be replaced. The Pope himself, the architect of this madness, was seeking new solutions. Solutions to an unsolvable curse.

“Let them destroy themselves,” I muttered under my breath as I crept further into the forest, the sound of pursuit fading behind me. But the whispers lingered in my mind, relentless and knowing.

At this point Zoser felt there is only one safe place for Moors, he made his way to the land of Granada.

# The Chains of Paradise Lost

I am the last voice of Granada, watching as Queen Isabella's armies mass outside our walls in 1492\. The God of Chaos has awakened now, and it feeds on our despair.

"Tell them what happens next," it whispers to me. "Tell them how paradise dies."

I was there when the last Sultan of Granada, Muhammad XII, handed over the keys to our city. His mother's words still echo through time: "Weep like a woman for what you could not defend as a man." But she didn't know – none of us knew – that this was only the beginning of our sorrows.

"Watch carefully," the God of Chaos purrs. "See how one empire's fall feeds another's rise."

The Spanish Inquisition moves through our streets like a plague. They burn our books first – centuries of knowledge turning to ash. Then they burn our people. The chaos spreads as the Portuguese join the hunt, and suddenly we're not just heretics – we're commodities.

I shift my gaze to the west coast of Africa, where I see our own sins coming full circle. The Berber raids that once struck south into West Africa had weakened the coastal kingdoms, making them vulnerable. Now Portuguese ships slip into those same ports, and the God of Chaos shows me the terrible symmetry.

"You see it now, don't you?" the entity asks. "How do the hunters become the hunted?"

I watch as the Limba people of Sierra Leone, already devastated by centuries of Berber slave raids, face a new and more terrible trade. The Portuguese don't just want slaves – they want gold, ivory, and souls. They build their fortress at Elmina, and the God of Chaos dances in the surf as the first ships depart.

"We did this," I whisper, watching Berber refugees flee south only to find themselves caught in the same nets they once cast. "When we raided the south, we opened the door."

The entity shows me the scattered people of al-Andalus – some fleeing to Morocco, others trying to hide their faith and culture in plain sight as Moriscos. But the Inquisition has a long reach and a longer memory.

"In 1501," I narrate, my voice thick with grief, "they gave us a choice that was no choice at all: convert or leave. By 1526, even practicing our old customs in secret was enough to condemn us. They called it 'crypto-Islam' and used it to justify hunting us across two continents."

I see the ships now – Spanish and Portuguese caravels cutting through waves toward Africa. They carry Inquisitors hungry for heretics and traders hungry for slaves. The God of Chaos shows me how they use our own ancient trade routes, our own maps, our own knowledge against both us and the peoples of West Africa.

"The Limba tribes," I continue, watching their villages burn, "paid twice for the sins of the empire. First weakened by our raids, then shattered by European slavers who followed our paths inland. The Portuguese didn't need to create new slave routes – we had already mapped them all."

The entity swirls around me as I witness the transformation of the African coast. Where Berber traders once exchanged salt for gold, European forts now rise like teeth in the mouth of a beast. The God of Chaos shows me the ledgers – how many taken, how many died, how many converted at sword-point.

"Your people mapped Africa's heart," it reminds me. "You showed them the way."

I see the Moriscos making their final exodus from Spain in 1609, carrying nothing but memories of paradise. Some find refuge in North Africa, others scatter across the Ottoman Empire. But the world has changed. The routes south are closed now, blocked by European ships and fortresses.

"The greatest irony," the God of Chaos muses, "is that they used your civilization's achievements to destroy you. Your navigation techniques, your maps, your astronomical tables – all turned against you and against Africa."

I watch as the last traces of Moorish culture are erased from Spain. Churches rise over mosques, palaces are whitewashed, and the sounds of the adhan fade into memory. But in the colonies being built with slave labor, I see echoes of our lost al-Andalus – in the architecture, in the irrigation systems, in the very crops they force slaves to grow.

"Your paradise became their blueprint," the entity observes. "They learned from destroying you how to build their own empire – one that would reach far beyond anything you imagined."

The God of Chaos shows me one final vision: the great circle of trade that would shape the modern world. African slaves working fields of sugar cane – a crop we had introduced to Spain – using irrigation techniques we had perfected, on plantations built with wealth taken from our conquered cities.

"This is how empires echo," I conclude, watching the last light fade from Granada's windows. "Each one teaches the next how to be more terrible, more efficient, more complete in its dominion."

The entity nods, its form rippling with dark satisfaction. "And the greatest empire of all was built on the bones of all the others."

I stand in the ruins of our paradise, watching as history's wheel turns. The God of Chaos was right – nothing lasts forever. But some endings spawn darker beginnings, and some falls echo through centuries, creating ripples of suffering that touch distant shores and unborn generations.

In the end, this too would become part of Europe's rise – our knowledge, our wealth, our maps, and even our mistakes. They learned from everything, even our fall, especially our fall. And the God of Chaos watched it all, feeding on the endless cycles of conquest and despair.

# The Fall of Granada: Catalina's Story

The flames of Granada burned bright against the winter sky. I remember that day as if it were yesterday, though I was only thirteen when our world crumbled. The streets where I once played with my friends had become a maze of terror as the Spanish forces stormed our beloved city.

"Aasma, quickly\!" My mother's voice cracked with desperation as she pulled me through the narrow alleyways of the Albayzín. "We must reach your uncle's house before the soldiers come\!"

But we were too late. The thunder of hooves on cobblestones grew louder, and suddenly they were upon us – Spanish soldiers in gleaming armor, their crosses catching the light of the burning buildings.

"Halt\!" The command came in harsh Castilian.

Behind the soldiers stood a man in black robes – an inquisitor. His eyes found mine, and I felt a chill despite the heat of the fires around us.

"The girl," he said, pointing at me. "She's of age to be properly... educated."

My mother's grip on my arm tightened. "Please," she begged in broken Castilian, "she is just a child."

The inquisitor's thin lips curved into what might have been meant as a kind smile. "A child who can be saved, who can serve God and Queen Isabella. The choice is simple – convert and live in service to the true faith, or face the consequences of heresy."

I looked at my mother, tears streaming down her face. She knew as well as I did – there was no real choice. Many of our neighbors who had resisted had disappeared into the dungeons of the Inquisition.

"I will convert," I whispered, the words tasting like ashes in my mouth.

I looked down at my mothers hands, the faint marks of her labor etched in her skin. "They say that when they cannot destroy what they fear, they make it invisible. It is not enough for them to banish our bodies; they erase our stories, wipe our names. Soon, generations will speak as though we never walked this earth."

The candle sputtered, casting fresh shadows on our faces. We shared a silence, thick with unspoken grief.

The inquisitor nodded approvingly. "You shall be called Catalina in your new life. A fitting name for one who will serve in the household of Princess Catherine of Aragon."

\---

# Aasma to Catalina

Three years passed in a blur of religious instruction and servant's training. I learned to speak perfect Castilian, to recite prayers, to move silently through grand rooms. I was no longer called Aasma in this new life. The Princess changed my name to Catalina. A fitting name for one who will serve in the household of Princess Catherine of Aragon she said. When word came that Princess Catherine was to marry the English Prince Arthur, I was chosen to join her household.

"You'll be responsible for the royal bedchamber," the head housekeeper informed me. "It's a position of great trust. Don't disappoint us."

The journey to England was long and treacherous. I had never seen so much water in my life, nor felt so cold. But it wasn't all hardship – I found I wasn't alone in being different in Tudor England.

"You see him?" Maria, another of Catherine's ladies, pointed to a black musician in magnificent livery. "That's John Blanke, the royal trumpeter. And over there, that's Symon, he works in the royal wardrobe. There are more of us here than you'd think."

Indeed, I soon discovered that the Tudor court was surprisingly diverse. While we were certainly not equal to the English nobles, our skills and service were valued. My dark skin drew far fewer stars than I had feared.

The years passed, and I found myself witness to events that would shake the foundations of England. I was there when Prince Arthur died, leaving Catherine a widow. I prepared the bed for her second marriage to his brother Henry, now King Henry VIII. I saw her joy at each pregnancy and shared her grief at each loss.

"You are the only one who truly knows, Catalina," Queen Catherine confided in me one night, her hand resting on her belly, swollen with yet another child who would not survive. "About Arthur... about that first night..."

I took her hand. "My queen, some secrets are best kept between a lady and her servant."

Years later, when King Henry began pursuing Anne Boleyn and sought to annul his marriage to Catherine, many came asking about what I knew. But I held my tongue. Even when I returned to Spain and married my dear Oviedo, even when they called me back to testify, I kept that secret.

For while I may have been born a Moor and made a slave, I had found something more precious than freedom – I had found loyalty, purpose, and a place where I was valued for my service rather than judged for my origins. The England I knew was not perfect, but it was a place where a former Muslim slave could rise to be trusted with royal secrets, where black musicians played for kings, and where the colour of one's skin mattered less than the ways of one's character.

# Nicolas death

The papal palace in Rome felt like the heart of a decaying beast. Shadows clung to the walls like cobwebs spun by ancient, unseen hands. I leaned against one of the cold marble columns, its surface slick, almost alive, as if the stone itself mourned what was about to unfold. The chamber was a theater of silence, save for the papal voice. Pope Nicholas V, cloaked in silk and sanctimony, held court like a god carving commandments from air.

“Romanus Pontifex,” he proclaimed, his voice a sonorous echo of doom. “If the Moors cannot sustain the dream, then others shall bear the burden.”

I felt the room tighten, not physically, but spiritually, as though an invisible noose was binding every soul present. His words carried the weight of something older than faith, something primordial. The whispers rippled beneath his decree, threading through the room like serpents seeking cracks in the stone. They brushed against me, cold and insidious, hissing their cruel approval.

I muttered under my breath, a bitter laugh escaping before I could stop it. “A dream? Whose dream? Certainly not the millions bound in chains, nor the lands bleeding beneath your holiness’ crusade.”

The whispers curled around my ears, mocking, their voices layered like smoke twisted by an unseen wind. “Whose, indeed?” they murmured.

The Pope’s armies scoured continents, capturing not just Moors, but Persians, Indians, and even Europeans deemed expendable. Entire families shattered like stained glass under a hammer. Parents and children, separated and categorized, their very essence stripped and redefined. Race, once a concept as fluid as rivers, became a weapon—a branding iron to divide and subjugate.

But the whispers, those ancient architects of ruin, were not content with conquest. They had other plans. As the enslaved were dragged into the machinery of Pope Nicholas’ empire, the earth itself rebelled. I saw it with my own eyes—the soil turned brittle beneath their feet, crops withered as if cursed by some unseen force. Rivers burst their banks, flooding villages in waves of thick, choking mud.

“The land remembers,” the whispers mused, their tone almost amused. “It does not forgive.”

And then came the revolts. The enslaved turned their chains into weapons, their cries piercing the heavens, a symphony of fury and despair. Soldiers, weakened by plague and gnawed by hunger, turned against their masters. I watched from the shadows as chaos consumed the world. The Pope’s armies, the proud banners of the Church, became tattered relics of a dream curdled into nightmare.

“Do you see it now, Nicholas?” I whispered one night as I stood beneath the Vatican’s arches, their grandeur dwarfed by the enormity of suffering. “Do you see what you’ve wrought?”

He didn’t answer, of course. By then, the man was more corpse than king, his soul eaten away by the same whispers that guided his hand. When Pope Nicholas finally fell on March 24, 1455, the Vatican itself seemed to exhale—a death rattle that echoed through its hallowed halls. But death, as I had come to understand, was merely a prelude.

The whispers wasted no time. They descended upon the new pope like vultures on fresh carrion. I saw him tremble in the throne, his hands gripping the armrests as if they might anchor him against the tidal wave of voices.

“The Church has come too far,” the whispers intoned, their voices layered with an ancient authority that made even marble shudder. “Sacrificed too much. The world must be cleansed. Only then can true peace emerge.”

“Peace,” I muttered, walking through the labyrinthine corridors of the Vatican, the walls seeming to pulse with unseen life. “Your peace is a lie.”

The whispers followed me, their laughter curling in the air like the acrid smoke of burning flesh. The myths of the Church, of paradise and salvation, had been devoured by something older, something unnameable. These were not mere whispers; they were echoes of a mythophysical force that predated the scriptures, a cosmic hunger masquerading as divinity.

I stopped in a garden, the roses withered and blackened under a pale, malignant moon. The whispers swirled around me, their presence a cold weight on my chest. “Witness,” they commanded, their voices harmonizing in a symphony of dread.

And so I did. I bore witness to a world unraveling, to humanity twisted by forces they could neither comprehend nor escape. The papal machine ground on, a juggernaut powered by faith and fear, blind to the abyss it carved into the soul of the earth.

The whispers promised a cleansing, a new dawn. But I had seen their true face—a maw of endless hunger, devouring everything in its path. Peace was a lie, and I was its unwilling chronicler.

# Innocent VIII 

The air in Rome felt heavier than ever, as if the city itself recoiled from the darkness gathering within its heart. I stood in the courtyard of the papal palace, my hands trembling as the bell tolled to announce the ascension of the new pope. "Innocent VIII," they called him. Innocent. The irony nearly broke me then and there.

It was 1484, and shadows swirled thicker than the smoke from the torches lining the palace walls. The whispers had found their perfect vessel—a man whose mask of piety concealed the depths of his malice. Innocent was no man of God. He was a predator cloaked in robes, his voice a venomous lullaby that promised salvation while sowing terror.

I watched him from the edges of the grand hall, his frail hand clutching the bull he would soon decree. Summis desiderantes affectibus. The Witch Bull. His words were soft yet chilling as he spoke, his lips curling into a smile that twisted my stomach.

“The enemies of the Church,” he began, his voice dripping with false sorrow, “walk among us, hiding in plain sight. Witches, they are. Temptresses of the devil. Women who defile the natural order.”

I gritted my teeth, my nails digging into my palms until I felt the sting of blood. Around me, the crowd murmured in agreement, their faces alight with fervor. None dared question him. None dared see through the whispers that twisted his every word into a weapon.

“Do you feel it, Rosa?” The whispers coiled around me, their voices sickly sweet. “The world is changing. The fire rises, and soon it will consume all who dare to defy us.”

I wanted to scream, but I swallowed it down. My tears threatened to spill, but I forced them back. Not here. Not now. I could feel their eyes on me—the priests, the guards, the servants. To weep openly would invite suspicion. To speak would be worse.

That was the lesson every woman in Rome was learning. Silence was survival. Don’t speak too boldly. Don’t think too loudly. Don’t let them see you are intelligent, or curious, or capable of more than the roles they carved out for you. For a Moorish woman like me, the stakes were even higher. The moment they noticed me, I would be declared a witch.

The Witch Bull spread its venom quickly. Women across Europe were hunted down, their cries echoing through the streets as the inquisitors dragged them from their homes. Old women accused by jealous neighbors. Young girls who dared to show spark or ambition. Moors, especially, were not spared. For us, magic and malice were assumed. There was no defense against an accusation—it was a sentence.

I remember one night, standing in the shadows of a square as they dragged a young girl to the stake. She couldn’t have been more than fifteen, her face streaked with tears, her cries for mercy ignored. “Please,” she screamed, “I am not a witch\! I am not—”

Her words were cut short by the jeers of the crowd, their faces twisted into masks of hatred. I turned away before the fire was lit, bile rising in my throat. “Innocent,” I whispered bitterly to myself. “You call this innocent?”

The whispers slithered back, their laughter low and cruel. “This is only the beginning, Rosa. A new dawn rises.”

“A dawn?” I hissed, my voice low but trembling with anger. “This is not dawn. This is the death of all light.”

I cried that night, hidden in the safety of my small, darkened room. But tears would not save me. Tears would not save anyone. Women were learning to hide, to silence themselves, to erase every flicker of brilliance or independence that might draw the Church’s gaze. But I knew survival demanded more than silence. It demanded cunning.

“We will never be inventors, never mathematicians,” I whispered to the empty air, my voice shaking. “Our names will never grace the halls of history, not unless we wear the chains of their lies.”

The whispers didn’t respond. Perhaps they didn’t care. Perhaps they thought they had already won.

But as I stared into the darkness, my tears drying into tracks on my cheeks, I made a silent vow. I would hide, yes. I would play their game. But I would survive. I would find a way to keep the light alive, even if it burned in secret.

The Church thought it could silence us. But there was power in silence too—a power they would never see coming.

# Pope imprisons rosa

The prison cell was colder than death, a damp tomb of stone and shadow. Chains rattled at my wrists, heavy and biting into my skin. I sat on the damp ground, my head resting against the wall, every breath a struggle against the overwhelming stench of mildew and despair.

The whispers were gone, for now. Even they seemed to retreat from this place. Or maybe they were watching, waiting for my next move, knowing that I was trapped in a game far larger than myself.

He had finally come for me. Innocent VIII, the man whose name was a mockery of everything he represented. He had known. Somehow, he had known. My powers, my secrets, the quiet magic that had slipped through the cracks of the Vatican’s walls like morning mist. For years, I had walked those halls, avoiding suspicion, keeping my gifts hidden under layers of silence and servitude. The previous pope had turned a blind eye, perhaps out of fear, perhaps out of some twisted reverence. But Innocent was different. He was a predator.

When the guards came for me, I had been in the library, my fingers tracing the pages of an illuminated manuscript. They didn’t speak as they seized me, but their grip was rough, their eyes hard.

“Innocent has questions for you,” one of them muttered as they dragged me through the labyrinthine corridors. My heart raced. Questions? No, this wasn’t about questions. This was about power.

Now, locked away beneath the Vatican, I had nothing but time to think. I knew why he had taken me. It wasn’t just because I was a witch. It wasn’t just because I was a Moor. It was because I was useful. He had seen the Renaissance bloom under the careful cultivation of minds like mine, and he wanted it for himself. He wanted my magic—the subtle forces I wove into art, into designs, into the very soul of the Vatican’s greatest works.

The cell door creaked open. A guard stepped in, his face a mask of disdain as he unshackled me and yanked me to my feet. “The Pope awaits.”

I was dragged through dimly lit corridors until we reached a grand chamber bathed in flickering torchlight. Innocent sat on his throne, his pale, thin face twisted into an expression of feigned benevolence. His eyes, though, betrayed him—hungry, calculating.

“Rosa,” he said, his voice soft but edged with steel. “You are quite the enigma.”

I said nothing, keeping my gaze on the cold marble floor.

He rose from his throne, his footsteps echoing as he approached me. “Do you know what intrigues me most about you?” he asked, circling me like a wolf sizing up its prey. “The previous pope allowed you to wander these halls freely, despite knowing what you are. A witch. A Moor. A danger to everything we hold sacred.”

I clenched my fists, but I said nothing.

“Why, I wonder?” he continued, his tone almost playful. “Did he fear you? Or did he use you? Your designs, your insights, your… magic.” His voice dipped into a hiss, the word dripping with contempt and fascination in equal measure.

“I have no magic,” I said at last, my voice hoarse but steady. “Only knowledge.”

“Knowledge,” he repeated, a smile curling his lips. “And where does this knowledge come from, Rosa? The stars? The whispers? The devil himself?”

I met his gaze then, defiance flickering in my eyes. “It comes from the same place as your greed, Holy Father. A hunger that cannot be sated.”

The room went deathly silent. I had spoken too boldly, but I didn’t care. If I was to be condemned, I would not cower.

His smile vanished, replaced by a cold, steely glare. “Chain her to the artist’s bench,” he commanded the guards. “Let her prove her talents. If she refuses, we will find… other ways to persuade her.”

I was forced into a room filled with parchment, brushes, and tools, their edges gleaming under the torchlight. They were the very instruments I had once used to create beauty, now turned into weapons against me.

“Show me,” Innocent said, leaning close. “Show me how you weave the stars into stone, how you breathe life into color.”

I stared at the tools, my mind racing. He didn’t just want my knowledge. He wanted my soul. And he would stop at nothing to take it.

But if he thought I would give him what he wanted, he was a fool. Magic—true magic—wasn’t in the ink or the stone. It was in the defiance, the spark of creation born from freedom. And that was something he could never control.

So I picked up the tools, my hands steady despite the trembling in my heart. If Innocent wanted magic, I would give him something far greater.

I would give him the seeds of his own undoing.

# Innocent 3 death

The papal chambers were a hellscape of quiet horror. The air reeked of sweat, incense, and something darker desperation. I stood silently, my chains removed but my spirit bound, watching Pope Innocent VIII gasping for breath on his gilded deathbed. Around him, the physicians scurried like rats, their faces pale, their whispers frantic. I was here by command, summoned to perform some unnamed "magic."

But it wasn’t magic that had brought him here. It was hubris.

“Three young boys,” I whispered, my voice barely audible over the shallow rattling of his breath. My eyes flicked to the bloodied instruments on the table, the stained cloths discarded carelessly nearby. “Lured with promises of gold. Their blood drawn to feed a pope's desperation for life.”

One of the physicians glanced at me, his hands trembling as he dabbed the pope's brow. He spoke, his voice hollow. “The children… they died. Their souls borrowed, but they did not stay. And still…” He swallowed hard, his words trailing off as his eyes darted back to the dying man. “Still, death comes for him.”

I stared at Innocent, my chest tightening with a mixture of rage and grim amusement. “No amount of innocent blood can purchase immortality,” I said bitterly. “Even the devil must be laughing at his failure.”

The pope’s breath hitched, a grotesque wheeze that rattled through the chamber. His eyes, clouded and sunken, flickered open, focusing on me—or through me. His lips moved, but no sound came.

“What’s that, Your Holiness?” I asked mockingly, stepping closer, my voice a blade dulled by years of silence but sharp enough to cut through the lie of his piety. “Are you seeking forgiveness? Or are you merely regretting that the price of life was higher than you could afford?”

The whispers returned, slithering through the room like unseen serpents. “This is what power looks like, Rosa,” they cooed. “Fragile. Desperate. Hollow.”

My hands balled into fists at my sides as I fought the urge to lash out at the pathetic figure before me. But my anger wasn’t just for him—it was for the world he had created. The Witch Bull, Summis desiderantes affectibus, had turned women like me into prey. Inquisitors roamed the countryside with the Malleus Maleficarum clutched in their hands, their eyes alight with righteous cruelty.

I saw their work as if it were playing out in the corners of the chamber. Torture chambers blessed by priests, where screams were muffled by the weight of holy fire. Villages consumed by paranoia, where healers and midwives—keepers of ancient wisdom—were dragged to their deaths. Families torn apart by sanctified fear, communities reduced to ashes and whispers of betrayal.

And now, the architect of it all lay before me, choking on the blood of children, his last grasp for power as pitiful as the ashes of his victims.

One of the physicians stepped back, his voice trembling. “He… he won’t last the night.”

“Good,” I muttered under my breath. The others didn’t hear me. Or maybe they did and chose not to react. Cowards, all of them.

The whispers grew louder, curling around me like smoke. “Rosa,” they hissed, “you have a choice. Watch him die, or grant him his wish. Show them what they fear.”

I shook my head, stepping back. “You think I would waste my power on this wretched soul? Let him rot.”

And rot he did. Innocent VIII gasped his last, his body convulsing one final time before going still. The blood of the children still stained his lips, a macabre reminder of his desperation. The physicians exchanged terrified glances, their faces pale as death itself. The whispers laughed, their echoing voices filling the chamber with a haunting mirth.

But the machinery of persecution he had blessed rolled on. Even as his corpse cooled, the darkness he had unleashed continued to spread, crushing wisdom and innocence beneath its wheels.

I turned away, the weight of it all pressing down on me. I had to leave, now, before his death sparked a frenzy. The guards were distracted, the physicians panicking. I slipped out of the chamber and into the labyrinthine corridors of the Vatican, the darkness swallowing me whole.

As I moved through the shadows, I thought of my father, Zoser, and the lessons he had taught me. He had been a Moor, enslaved and silenced, his wisdom buried beneath the weight of his chains. My mother’s pale skin had spared me some of his fate, but now I was no freer than he had been.

I had learned the silence of the owl, the subtlety of the cat, the patience of the spider weaving her web in darkness. These were my tools, my weapons. “And so this is how a woman will survive,” I thought, my steps quiet but deliberate. “Keeping her eyes open behind closed lids, preserving true vision in a time of sanctified blindness.”

I slipped out of the Vatican and into the night, the chaos of Innocent’s death fading behind me. The city’s darkness embraced me like an old friend, and I disappeared into it, my mind racing with the knowledge that survival was not enough. There was power in the shadows, in the secrets I carried, and one day, I would find a way to use it.

The world thought it could bury us—women, witches, Moors, healers. But we were seeds, and one day, we would grow into something unstoppable.
