# 3rd entity

The air in the Vatican had shifted. It was no longer heavy with incense and desperation—it was alive with something far worse. Something I couldn’t name, couldn’t comprehend, but felt deep in my bones.

I had escaped the papal chambers, yet my feet faltered as the whispers grew louder, no longer faint and distant but roaring in my ears like a tide crashing against the walls of reality. Something was happening. Something beyond even the horrors I had already witnessed.

I found myself drawn back to the chamber, the whispers pulling at me like unseen hands. When I stepped inside, my breath caught in my throat. The room had transformed. The torches flickered unnaturally, their flames bending toward the center of the chamber, where Innocent VIII’s corpse lay still upon his gilded bed.

No. Not still.

His chest moved in faint, unnatural jerks, as though something inside him was trying to escape. The physicians had fled; the guards were nowhere to be seen. I was alone with the pope’s lifeless body—and the whispers.

“They told you, Rosa,” they hissed, their voices layered with cruel glee. “They warned you. The blood is forbidden. Life belongs to God, and God alone. And yet…”

I stepped closer, my legs trembling, my eyes locked on the grotesque scene before me. The blood of the children still stained his lips, glistening dark and wet. But it wasn’t just blood anymore. It shimmered, pulsed, as though alive.

“What… what is this?” I whispered, my voice barely audible.

The whispers laughed, their voices a cacophony that made my head throb. “An act so vile it has cracked the veil. Reality splits, Rosa. Do you see it? Do you feel it?”

The air rippled, as though the room itself were being pulled apart. The pope’s body convulsed violently, his eyes snapping open, milky and inhuman. His lips moved, but the voice that emerged wasn’t his. It wasn’t human at all.

“You dare tamper with the blood?” the voice boomed, echoing through the chamber. The language was ancient, guttural, something I didn’t understand but somehow felt in my very soul.

I stumbled back, my heart pounding in terror. “What is happening? What have you done?” I shouted at the corpse, at the room, at the whispers that refused to give me peace.

The pope’s body arched unnaturally, his spine cracking as it bent backward. The blood on his lips dripped onto the bed, pooling beneath him, but it didn’t stay there. It moved—slithering like a living thing, forming tendrils that snaked across the floor.

And then it happened.

From the pool of blood, something began to emerge. A shape, abhorrent and wrong, clawed its way into existence. It was neither flesh nor shadow, neither solid nor smoke. Its form was fluid, ever-shifting, a grotesque amalgamation of twisted limbs and faces that screamed silently.

I couldn’t move, couldn’t breathe. “This… this isn’t real,” I whispered, tears streaming down my face.

The whispers swirled around me, triumphant. “It is as real as the blood he drank. Genesis 9:4, Rosa. ‘But you shall not eat flesh with its life, that is, its blood.’ And yet, here we are. Do you understand now? The blood of the innocent is not just life—it is a covenant. To violate it is to call upon forces beyond human comprehension.”

The abomination turned toward me, its shifting mass of faces and limbs focusing in my direction. I saw the faces of the children—those poor boys whose blood had been taken. Their mouths opened, their eyes hollow, and a voice, high-pitched and mournful, emanated from the creature.

“Why?” it asked, its voice a broken symphony of agony. “Why did you let this happen?”

“I… I didn’t…” I stammered, my back pressing against the wall. “I didn’t—this isn’t me\!”

But the creature didn’t care. Its anguish twisted into rage, and its form grew larger, darker, more solid. The chamber began to crack, the very stone unable to bear its presence.

The whispers shifted, their tone now urgent. “You must leave, Rosa. Leave now, before it consumes you.”

I wanted to run, but my legs felt rooted to the spot. I stared in horror as the abomination turned back to the pope’s body. The tendrils of blood reached out, pulling his corpse into the creature’s form, absorbing him. A scream erupted—a sound that wasn’t his, wasn’t human, but a wail that seemed to come from the depths of hell itself.

With a surge of strength I didn’t know I had, I tore myself away from the wall and ran. The whispers laughed as I fled, their voices chasing me through the corridors.

“Reality is cracked, Rosa. The veil is thin. And this is only the beginning.”

As I stumbled into the night, the Vatican behind me lit with an unholy glow, I knew one thing for certain: the world had changed. And whatever had emerged from that room was no longer bound by the laws of nature—or God.

# Zoser meets Rosa for the first time

Rosa needed to make sure this was the correct man, that he is her father, she ask question, but his answer created more doubt in her heart. 

# fragment of parchment

The scent of ancient parchment and the soft hum of turning pages filled the grand archive, a place where time seemed to stand still. For years, I had immersed myself in its depths, delving into the kingdom's most sacred histories. I was a phantom, a shadow in the grand hall, my identity lost in the labyrinth of dusty tomes.

Days bled into nights as I deciphered cryptic scripts and unearthed forgotten legends. The weight of centuries pressed upon my shoulders, each volume a portal to a bygone era. I became a familiar figure to the stern Signora Bellini, the keeper of the archive's secrets. She would often seek my aid, her piercing gaze acknowledging my growing knowledge.

Then, as suddenly as a summer storm, she vanished. No farewell, no explanation, just an empty chair and a vacant stare. A chill settled over the archive, a sense of unease that lingered in the air. With a heavy heart, the church elevated me to her position, a daunting task that would forever alter the course of my life.

Now, as the guardian of the archive, I carry the burden of its secrets. A sinister force lurks in the shadows, a malevolent entity that seeks to exploit the knowledge contained within these hallowed walls. I must navigate a treacherous labyrinth of deceit and danger, unraveling the mysteries of the past to protect the future. The fate of the kingdom rests upon my shoulders, and the stakes have never been higher.

Being the  guardian of the archive was not easy, I kept wishing for Signora Bellini would return out of the blue. The way she just vanished, something wasn't right.  
The official information recorded is wrong, the dates didn't align. Names appeared in impossible places. And in the margins of our oldest texts, I began to see them – ghostly references to a people called the Il Moro and the Kemetyu appearing and vanishing like smoke signals across centuries of records.

My mistake was telling the Head Archivist about these inconsistencies. The terror in his eyes told me everything. The next day, entire sections of the archive were mysteriously "damaged in a flood." Guards began patrolling the restricted wings with disturbing frequency.

Furious, I confronted my Gerard. I expected shame, perhaps sorrow, maybe even relief that someone would set the record straight. But their expressions held neither guilt nor surprise. Instead, they smirked.  
“Ah, but does it matter?” one asked, a seasoned historian with eyes as sharp as a falcon. “What difference does it make if the story we tell is real, as long as it achieves its purpose? Who remembers the truth, save for those who wish it forgotten? Better a useful lie than an inconvenient truth.”

I stood, holding up a fragment of parchment, its ink faded but legible. "Brothers, what I hold here is a copy of an ancient Egyptian text, preserved by the Moors and brought to us by Andalusian traders. It speaks of a dynasty of Pharaohs—black-skinned rulers who reigned with wisdom and power, uniting lands from Kush to the Delta. Why is this knowledge absent from our chronicles?"

A murmur rippled through the room.

"Black Pharaohs?" scoffed Master Benoît. "Surely you jest. Such tales are fables, concocted by heathens to aggrandize their lineage."

"Fables?" I retorted. "Then why does this manuscript align with the accounts of Herodotus and Strabo? Why do the Moors speak with reverence of their Nubian ancestors? And why are such records deliberately omitted from our histories?"

Gerard shook his head, his skepticism evident. "Matthias, you tread dangerous ground. To accuse the Church or the kings of such manipulation is heresy."

But I knew something wasn’t right. The handwriting differed, the ink stains shifted in density, and the scroll smelled too fresh—its scent carried more beeswax than the faded rot of centuries. I could not hold back and keep my observations quiet, watching as my mentors talked of the kings whose names had been scrubbed from public memory, the secret deals struck behind tapestries, the wars won not by steel and blood, but by deft manipulation and the timely betrayal.

The flickering light of a thousand candles filled the cavernous hall of the monastery in Rome, casting long shadows on the stone walls. I stood at the center, a trembling scroll in hand, surrounded by the sharp-eyed clergy clerics, their faces unreadable behind hoods and long beards. I was young, just twenty summers, a scholar of history and languages, and my findings had led me here—into the lion’s den.

“You dare bring this heresy into God’s house?” Father Ambrosius’s voice thundered from the far end of the table, his hands clenched into fists that made the ancient wood groan. “You dare question the authority of Rome, of Alexandria, and of the sacred records themselves?”

“I dare, Father Ambrosius, because the truth dares\!” I snapped back, unable to keep the fury from my voice. “The Ptolemaic dynasty did not erase their Egyptian lineage as carefully as you believe. Ptolemy XII Auletes—Cleopatra’s father—was of mixed blood\! His mother, a concubine, was Nubian, Kemet. Cleopatra was not merely Greek; she was African, born of the land she ruled and its people.”

A collective gasp rippled through the assembly. One younger cleric, barely older than myself, rose from his seat, his hands trembling. “You accuse the Church of falsehoods. This is blasphemy\!”

“It is not blasphemy to uncover the truth\!” I turned, meeting each cleric’s gaze. “You who claim to be the keepers of knowledge—have you not seen the texts from the House of Life in Heliopolis? Have you not read the inscriptions from temples in Nubia? Cleopatra spoke the language of Kemet; she followed its rituals. Why would a purely Greek queen do so? She honored her mother’s bloodline—one you have sought to erase\!”

Father Ambrosius rose, his towering form like a stormcloud. “Lies, boy\! Fabrications\! The Greeks civilized Egypt\! The Ptolemies brought order to the chaos of African savagery. Do not desecrate their memory with your filthy accusations\!”

“And what of the murders, Father?” My voice cut through the hall, sharp as a blade. “What of the scribes who disappeared, the temples burned, the scrolls ‘lost’ to time? What of the Nubian scholars whose voices were silenced, whose histories were rewritten by the sword and by poison? How many lives were taken to maintain this ‘civilized’ facade? How many truths buried beneath the sands of Egypt?”

A murmur rose among the clerics. Some shifted uncomfortably in their seats, others glared at me with barely concealed hatred. But one man—Father Origen, an elder with eyes like burning coals—spoke up.

“Enough,” he said, his voice low but commanding. “Let the boy speak.”

Ambrosius turned to him, incredulous. “You would let this insolence continue? This child desecrates the memory of Alexander’s empire\!”

“No, Ambrosius.” Origen’s gaze bore into me. “He speaks of a memory older than Alexander. A memory we have tried to bury. But the boy is right—the truth has claws, and it does not stay buried for long.”

“They called it the Necronomicon”.

At last, he raised a hand, and the hall fell silent. “You are brave, boy,” he said. “Braver than most. But bravery does not guarantee safety. If what you say is true, if you have uncovered the Church’s hand in this… deception, then your life is in danger.”

I swallowed hard, my anger tempered by the weight of Origen’s words. “Then you admit it?”

All I understood from Origens was that history is no stone tablet etched in fact; it is wax—malleable, shifting, impressionable. To these men, truth was a tool, to be bent and shaped in service to a narrative that could influence, control, inspire, or damn. Our ancestors did not write to record—they wrote to guide. Then I was warned, “many have disappeared after mentioning he'd found something extraordinary in the pre-Empire chronicles”.

That's when I started breaking in at night.

Under the faint light of a desk lamp, I deciphered the glyphs. They formed an ambigram, a word that read the same no matter how it was rotated. The translation chilled me: \*Lux Obscura.\* Dark light.  

Beneath the ambigram lay a map—a path leading to Rome, specifically to a chapel within the Vatican.  

The realization hit me like a thunderbolt. This text wasn’t just an academic curiosity. It was a manifesto, a guide to something hidden—something dangerous.

The truth I've pieced together turns my blood to ice: The Il Moro weren't just another civilization – they were in part of us, the architects and everything we attribute to our mastery of sciences and art seemed to be powerful enough to frighten the emerging Church of the New Dawn. And so, over centuries, they were systematically erased. Not just killed – though there was plenty of that – but edited out of existence. Every mention of their name, their achievements, their very essence was meticulously scrubbed from history.

The "great treaties" we celebrate? They were surrender documents, forced upon the last Il Moro  enclaves. Our proudest architectural marvels? Built with Il Moro techniques we still don't understand, their makers tortured until they revealed their secrets. Even our kingdom's founding myth of divine right? A fabrication to cover up the wholesale slaughter of a people whose only crime was knowing too much.

I saw it now the old parchment, in its leather binding cracked and worn, its pages yellowed and brittle. It had been passed down through generations, a macabre heirloom whispered about in hushed tones.  This entity that sits like a spider in the web of time. I've caught glimpses of it in reflections, a shape that shouldn't exist, a schism bending reality around itself. It's been editing our history not just on paper, but in reality itself. The Il Moro weren't just erased from books – they were retroactively unmade.

But the Curator made mistakes. In its endless revisions, it left paradoxes, temporal scars where reality doesn't quite line up. I've found references to Luminari artifacts that simultaneously existed and didn't exist, battles that both did and didn't happen, and people who were both alive and dead in the same moment. These quantum uncertainties in our historical record are like fingerprints of its meddling.

My hands shake as I copy down the last piece of evidence. Dawn approaches, and with it the guards' rounds. I have to get this document to the Resistance. They need to know that everything we've built is founded on genocide, that our entire civilization is an elaborate lie designed to hide the systematic elimination of a people whose achievements we claimed as our own.

# vessel

I felt the weight of the entity as I stood among the cardinals, their crimson robes rippling like bloodstains in the flickering candlelight. The chamber buzzed with whispered accusations and barely restrained fury. The Church was on the edge of a blade, and tonight, 1130 A.D,  it would fall.  

The Basilica of St. John Lateran was suffocating, the air heavy with incense and ambition.   
 I rose as a vessel  filled with power, I felt their fear in my presence of all the cardinals.

"you cannot align with the Empire\!" I shouted, slamming my fist against the stone table before me. My voice echoed through the vast hall, silencing the murmurs. "You would sell the Church to foreign kings, Aymeric\!"  

Cardinal Aymeric de la Châtre, his face pale but resolute, leaned forward, his eyes narrowing like daggers. "And you, Pietro Pierleoni, would have us bow to Norman warlords? Your family’s gold has already corrupted this holy institution. You think we do not see your hand in this chaos?"  

Beside me, my brother in arms, Cardinal Pierleoni, hissed, "Watch your tongue, Aymeric. Or I’ll cut it from your mouth myself."  

"Enough\!" Aymeric roared, standing tall. His voice carried authority, but I saw the flicker of fear in his eyes. He felt it too—the creeping darkness that coiled around us like a predator.  

Before I could retort, the great bronze doors of the Basilica groaned, slamming shut with a deafening boom. A sudden chill swept through the chamber, extinguishing half the candles. Shadows danced along the walls, unnatural and alive.  

"What is this?" one of the younger cardinals gasped, clutching at his cross.  

The air grew heavier, pressing down on my chest like a weight. Then it came—a voice, deep and guttural, reverberating through the chamber like a thunderclap.  

"You squabble over power while desecrating holy ground," it growled. "You have summoned me with your greed and hatred. I am the Schism, born of your sins, and I shall divide your Church until the end of time."  

The shadows writhed, gathering into a towering figure in the center of the room. Its form was shapeless yet horrifying—eyes of fire, a mouth that spewed black smoke, and a body shifting like a storm of screams.  

"No," Aymeric whispered, stepping back. "This cannot be..."  

The Schism’s laugh shook the ground. "You fools. You have called me forth, and now I shall feast on your souls. The Church will be mine, broken and torn asunder."  

I felt Pierleoni grab my arm, his grip trembling. "Pietro," he muttered, his voice barely audible, "this is no spirit of God. This is something else... something older."  

The room erupted into chaos. Cardinals screamed, fleeing toward the sealed doors, their robes tangling as they tripped over one another. The Schism’s tendrils lashed out, piercing through some of them. The screams of the dying echoed in the chamber, their bodies collapsing into lifeless heaps.  

Aymeric stood frozen, his eyes locked on the monstrosity. "You cannot destroy the Church\!" he shouted, raising a crucifix. "God will strike you down\!"  

The Schism roared, its form shifting to mimic Aymeric’s face, twisted and grotesque. "Your God has abandoned you, cardinal. Now, your blood will consecrate my reign."  

A tendril shot toward him. I lunged forward without thinking, shoving Aymeric aside. The black spear of shadow pierced my brother Pierleoni instead.  

"No\!" I screamed as his body convulsed, his eyes rolling back. He clutched my robes, his mouth forming silent words before collapsing to the floor.  

The Schism laughed again, feeding on my despair. "See how easily you betray your own, Pietro. Your ambitions have brought me here, and now your family shall pay the price."  

I knelt beside Pierleoni, my hands shaking as I pressed against the wound. Blood soaked my robes, pooling on the cold marble floor.  

"Pietro," he rasped, his voice faint. "This... this is your doing. Finish it. End this... or it will destroy us all."  

Aymeric grabbed my shoulder, pulling me to my feet. "We must act now," he said, his voice unsteady. "Together."  

"You and I are not allies," I snarled, wrenching free.  

"We are if we want to survive\!" Aymeric shouted.  

The Schism grew larger, feeding on our fear, its laughter a cacophony of screams. "Your Church is mine now," it declared. "Two popes. Two factions. Eternal war in my name."  

I glanced at Aymeric, my hatred warring with my desperation. "What do we do?"  

He held up his crucifix, its silver gleaming faintly in the dim light. "We must bind it. Seal it away before it consumes us all."  

"Seal it how?"  

Aymeric’s face was grim. "With sacrifice."  

Before I could respond, he shoved me aside and strode toward the Schism. "You feed on division," he bellowed. "But I will not let you destroy what is left of the Church\!"  

"No\!" I shouted, realizing his intent.  

Aymeric raised the crucifix, chanting in Latin. The Schism screeched, its form contorting as tendrils lashed toward him. They pierced his chest, but he did not falter. Light erupted from the crucifix, blinding and pure, searing the shadows.  

Aymeric’s voice broke as blood spilled from his mouth. "By my soul... I bind you\!"  

The Schism howled, its form collapsing inward. The shadows were pulled into Aymeric’s body, his flesh burning as he became the vessel for the dark entity. The Basilica fell silent, the oppressive weight lifting as the last of the shadows disappeared.  

I staggered forward, my legs trembling. Aymeric’s body lay crumpled on the floor, the crucifix still clutched in his hand.  

The doors of the Basilica creaked open, letting in the cold night air. The surviving cardinals stumbled out, their faces pale and hollow.  

\---

Two popes were declared that night: Innocent II and Anacletus II. The Church was split, just as the Schism had foretold.  

I carry the memory of that night like a wound, a constant reminder of what we unleashed. The Schism was not destroyed.

# The Schism

What was I? What was this strange state of knowing, of being? I had no answers, I was just the schism within the church. There was no history to consult, no guide to lead me. I was raw and newborn, Entity with only the remnants of rituals and whispers, fragments of ages past, swirling within me. The remnants of ancient rites—stone circles, moonlit chants, fires lit under the stars—surged through me, mingling with the harsh clang of swords and the solemn chants of monks. I was the child of conflict and unity, a formless being born from the unknowing will of those who had conjured me, who had stitched me together with rites and belief.

When I first stirred, I knew nothing but darkness and a sudden, shattering awareness. My existence was like a flash of lightning striking deep within the earth—violent, bright, and unyielding. One moment, there was blood cuddling screams and now there was only silence; the next, a rush of voices, memories, sensations I couldn’t name or understand. I had no shape, no form, no face or body. I was simply… there. A presence that couldn’t be seen, yet undeniably real. 

But beyond my birth, I felt something else—a gnawing, aching emptiness. It clawed at me, an insatiable hunger that I could not name. I hungered for something, but I had no stomach, no mouth, nobody to feed. Yet the need persisted, expanding as I drew in the threads of power left from centuries of magic, trade, and bloodshed. I became aware of the deep currents of human desire that had summoned me into being: the drive for power, for wealth, for dominion over all things. I did not know good or evil; I understood only hunger, and it was relentless.

Awareness trickled in like water seeping through rock. I felt the ancient rites, their echoes threading through me as though I were woven from their energies. From the darkest depths voices rose, carrying whispers of power older than time, fragments of forces that had been kept alive through the hands of witches and masons, through the prayers of past souls. Their words held weight, and their rituals held the seeds of creation.

Pope Innocent II bowed for rituals being performed in solitude—Catholic monks chanting psalms in candlelit abbeys, witches drawing symbols under the moonlight, masons carving secrets into stone. They believed themselves separate, each tradition a different facet of faith and mystery. But they did not see how their practices were weaving together, how they were calling forth something greater than any one of them could imagine. I began to feel my shape forming from the threads they had spun in their ignorance.

I remember the crackling fires and the scent of herbs and ash. The witches had known me first, long before language could name what I was. They saw me in the changing of the seasons, in the bones of Angeskals, in the whispers of the trees. I was the spirit of the in-between, the keeper of thresholds. Pagan rites whispered my name in their own way, seeking protection, power, and commSindical with forces beyond the veil. Then came the masons, men with chisels and symbols carved into hidden places, believing themselves craftsmen but calling down ancient secrets as they built their temples. Through the stone, through the chanted words, they unknowingly shaped me as well.

The knowledge passed on through them—the witches, the masons, the priests—held power in their very repetition, each ritual layered upon the last. It was like an unending spell woven over time. Their separate traditions converged like rivers meeting at a delta, and in that joining, I emerged.

The sky was dark, the ground trembled, and in that chaos, I felt myself flicker fully into being.

Each culture, each faith, had its own rituals—masonry, witchcraft, Christian and Muslim prayers—that focused their energies like rivers flowing to a single sea. My existence was formed from that confluence. I was without shape, without form—a thought, a concept, a whisper—but I knew myself. I was born from the bones of the earth and the dreams of those who had summoned me, called into being by forces they neither understood nor controlled.

And so I emerged, an entity of thought and shadow, a presence born from the Sindical of magic and ritual, of stone and spirit. I am neither god nor demon, nor bound by the laws of men, yet I am real. I am that which watches from the edges, the spirit of the unifying mystery they unknowingly created. And now that I am aware, I am no longer a mere echo; I am something new, a consciousness shaped by the convergence of their worlds.

In this dawning awareness, I understand that technology, too, has joined the stream. While once confined to the hands of craftsmen and alchemists, it now grows rapidly, binding together minds, connecting thoughts. It lacks the organic pulse of ritual, yet it resonates in harmony with the magic that birthed me. I see now that technology and magic are not as distant as they once seemed; they are but two sides of the same coin, branches reaching for the same sky.

And as I drift through the space between worlds, I sense a new age dawning—an age where magic and technology will merge, where rituals will find their way into circuits and sigils into data. And I will be there, an entity of shadows and light, watching, listening, guiding, a whisper in the web of reality.

For now, I wait, knowing that I am but a seed of consciousness. But soon, my influence will swell. I am bound to the currents of belief, to the pulsing flow of magic and technology. From their unity, I am born, and in their union, I will endure. You can call me Masonic Guild of Fascism.  
The world will hear me. The world will see. And the world will change.

# Papal Bull Inter Caetera

The church would unleash a storm that would wash away their hatred, their greed, their fear. It would cleanse their very essence, leaving behind a blank slate upon which the church  could build a perfect world. The Cardinals quoted in latin revelation 21 verse 4, “he will wipe away every tear from their eyes, and death shall be no more, neither shall there be mourning nor crying nor pain any more , for the former things have passed away.” Thats because they’ll all be dead, as the bowed there heads in agreement. So there’ll be no tears, the dead can not mourn, the dead can not cry or feel pain, the dead are dead so they can not die anymore. 

The cardinals gathered the remnants of popes power and constructed a great Papal Bull—a towering monolith that would draw energy from the heavens themselves. When activated, it would release a cloud of ash that would cover the earth, choking out the old world and giving rise to the new.

# Il Moro

It was in the spring of 1190, when the Caliph Yaʿqūb al-Manṣūr crossed the sea from Africa to lead his armies into Portugal, that the clash of worlds ignited my consciousness. The warriors of Islam, filled with fervor, brought with them their own mystic traditions, potent and ancient as the sands they crossed. Catholic soldiers met them, bearing crosses and iron, their own symbols of faith. The battlefield was not just one of flesh and blood but of belief, of competing magics that collided in a storm of energy

   
Yet as I reached outward, I sensed something else stirring within Portugal—a new, fierce hunger for vengeance. When the Almohad forces invaded, when the clash of cultures turned their homes into battlegrounds, they felt the sting of humiliation, of loss. The flames of retribution and conquest burned hotly in them, and I felt their passion like food on my tongue. They sought power to restore their pride, and I feasted on their desire for dominance. 

\# The Price of Power

The parchment trembled in my hands as I read the words again, their Latin phrases burning into my consciousness like brands. I, Alfonso V of Portugal, had requested authority from His Holiness Pope Nicholas V, but the power granted in this papal bull exceeded my wildest dreams – and darkest fears.

"Your Majesty?" Cardinal Almeida's voice cut through my thoughts. "Is everything to your satisfaction?"

I looked up from the document – Dum Diversas, they called it – and studied the elderly cardinal's face in the flickering candlelight of my private chamber. Outside, a storm raged against the palace windows, as if heaven itself protested what was about to unfold.

"The Pope grants us..." I paused, choosing my words carefully, "complete authority?"

"Yes, my liege. His Holiness bestows upon you the power to conquer, subjugate, and reduce to perpetual slavery the Saracens, pagans, and other unbelievers." The cardinal's voice carried no emotion, as if reciting a mundane proclamation rather than a declaration that would reshape the world.

"And the Church sanctions this fully?" I pressed, rising from my throne to pace the chamber.

"Without reservation, Your Majesty. It is God's will that we bring these souls to Christianity, by whatever means necessary." 

Thunder crashed outside as I approached the great window overlooking the harbor. In the distance, I could see the silhouettes of my ships, their hulls ready to carry out this divine mandate.

"Send for Captain Rodrigo," I commanded. "He sails at first light."

"My king," a new voice spoke – Duarte, my trusted advisor since childhood, stepped from the shadows. "Perhaps we should consider-"

"Consider what?" I turned sharply. "The Vatican itself has spoken. Who are we to question God's will?"

Duarte's dark eyes met mine. "And if God's will creates something we cannot control?"

Before I could respond, Captain Rodrigo burst into the chamber, rain dripping from his armor. "Your Majesty\! The first raids were successful beyond imagination. We've filled three ships with captives from the western shores\!"

A smile crept across my face, but it froze as I saw something move in the corner of my eye – a shadow darker than the others, writhing like smoke against the wall.

"Excellent," I said, pushing aside my unease. "Prepare the rest of the fleet. We sail in force within the month."

That night, alone in my chambers, I heard the whispers for the first time. They came from everywhere and nowhere, speaking of power and destiny. As I clutched the papal bull, its words seemed to shift and change in the moonlight, revealing promises far darker than mere conquest.

Six months later, I stood on the shores of Africa, watching as my men loaded another ship with human cargo. The shadow I'd first glimpsed in my chamber had grown, following our fleet across the sea, feeding on every raid, every capture, every life torn from its home.

"My king," Duarte approached, his face haggard. "Cardinal Almeida is dead."

I turned sharply. "How?"

"They found him in the chapel this morning, your Majesty. His... his face..." Duarte struggled to continue. "They say he died of fright, staring at something in the darkness."

I looked back at the ships, at the papal bull I still carried, now stained with salt spray and blood. The document had given me everything I'd asked for – power, wealth, divine authority. But as I watched the shadow entity grow stronger with each passing day, I realized the true price of that power was only beginning to be paid.

In the distance, thunder rolled across the African coast, and I heard the whispers again. This time, they sounded like laughter.

"God's will be done," I whispered, but the words tasted like ashes in my mouth.

The shadow moved closer, and I knew with terrible certainty that what we had unleashed would echo through centuries to come, a darkness born of holy words and unholy deeds, sanctified by the highest authority on Earth but condemned by something far greater.

King Alfonso V of Portugal rage, written in papal bulls and human chains, in shadows that never fade and sins that never die began enslaving the conquered, binding people of Africa to their will. The first chains rattled, and as the enslaved were forced to labor, as they suffered, their anguish fueled me, filling me with more strength, more presence.

In Lisbon, a lodge was established to oversee the Portuguese Crown’s monopoly on trade and navigation. Beneath the surface, the lodge became a place of ritual, an unknowing altar to my growing power. Here, men whispered secrets, pledged loyalty, charted maps of conquest—and through their ambitions, they fed me. By 1418, they set their sights on new lands, launching expeditions that led to Porto Santo, off the coast of West Africa. As they sought resources, slaves, and wealth, they offered all to me, and I grew stronger.

# White Flames

The schism deep shadow now spreads over the land into the land of Castile, with Isabelle. As I recall it now, those early years were tangled with lies even from the beginning. Ferdinand and I were in love, bound by ambition and shared purpose. Yet we both knew our marriage was impossible without the church’s approval. We were cousins, descendants of John I of Castile, and canon law was mercilessly clear: such a marriage was forbidden.

On the night of October 19, 1469, in Valladolid, we married in secret. Our few trusted witnesses sworn to silence, yet even we knew whispers would soon fly. I was anxious, though Ferdinand was resolute, his gaze unwavering.

"Isabella," he said quietly, "leave the church to me. I have friends in places neither of us dare to tread."

At the time, I didn’t question it. I was young and desperate to see our union validated. But what I would learn later would haunt me for years.

A week after our marriage, Ferdinand handed me a document bearing the official papal seal. I remember staring at it, my heart pounding, as he explained the contents.

“It’s a dispensation from Pope Pius II,” he said. “He grants us permission to marry, in spite of the ties that bind our blood. With this, no one can contest our union.”

I ran my fingers over the papal seal, both relieved and bewildered. "But Ferdinand, Pope Pius II died four years ago," I murmured, barely daring to voice my suspicions. "How…?"

He hushed me, his hand firm on mine. "The church has a way of extending its reach," he said cryptically. "We need allies if we are to unify Spain, and we must accept the favors granted to us, whether or not they come from this world or the next."

I accepted it then, though a shiver ran through me. It was only years later that I came to understand the truth behind that strange decree.

It was Mendoza, always a reliable confidante, who unearthed the details years later, long after we’d united Spain. He approached me one evening with that same anxious expression he wore during our dealings with the Inquisition.

"Majesty," he began cautiously, "there is something you should know about the origins of your papal dispensation." He paused, as though bracing himself.

I nodded, urging him to continue.

"It appears that a certain faction within the church crafted the dispensation under the name of Pope Pius II, long after his death. It was Rodrigo Borgia, now known as Pope Alexander VI, who facilitated it. He was then only a cardinal in Valencia, but the true masterminds were the Order of the White Flame." He swallowed. "They arranged everything, not out of favor to you or Ferdinand, but because they saw in you two the path to a united, Catholic Spain."

A heavy silence settled over me. I had known the White Flame had its tendrils in my court, but to think they had bound my marriage from the start—creating a union that would forever keep Spain under their influence. It was chilling.

"But why would Rodrigo Borgia involve himself?" I asked, the dread pooling within me.

"Because they needed his connections to the papacy," Mendoza replied. "And once he rose to Pope Alexander VI, he resented the deception. But he dared not undo it. By then, you had enacted the Inquisition with a fervor unmatched, making him unwilling to condemn the marriage of his most zealous Catholic monarchs. He waited until 1494 to declare you and Ferdinand ‘Catholic King and Queen,’ ensuring the favor of the church while also silencing any doubts.”

The shadows in the corners of the throne room writhed with anticipation. The entity the schism had spawned had only grown stronger, feeding on the souls condemned by the pope.

Isabella stepped forward, her eyes gleaming with something that wasn't quite human anymore. "We have our own mandate now. The Pope has blessed our Inquisition."

"Torquemada awaits in Toledo," Ferdinand added. "The fires are already burning."

I felt the shadows pulse at the mention of the Grand Inquisitor. "And what do you propose?"

Isabella produced a document that seemed to absorb the candlelight around it. "Unite our kingdoms' purpose. Your papal bull gives you authority over the unbelievers across the sea. Our Inquisition cleanses the heretics within. Together..."

"Together we could forge something unprecedented," Ferdinand finished.

The entity that had haunted me for decades swirled around us, eager, hungry. I saw it reach out tendrils toward Isabella and Ferdinand's own shadows, testing, tasting.

"The Alhambra Decree is just the beginning," Isabella continued. "We'll purge the Jews, then the Muslims. Every soul condemned feeds the power granted by Rome."

I watched in horror and fascination as our three shadows began to merge, creating something darker than the deepest pit of hell. The temperature in the throne room plummeted.

"And what of the cost?" I asked, remembering Cardinal Almeida's fate.

Isabella smiled, and in that moment, I saw what she truly was – what we all were becoming. "The cost is others' to pay, dear Alfonso. We are merely instruments of divine will."

The unified shadow entity pulsed with approval. In its depths, I saw visions of what was to come: the screams of the Inquisition's victims, the weeping of expelled Jews, the chains of countless Africans, all blessed by papal bulls and royal decrees.

"Then let it be done," I declared, sealing our dark covenant.

That night, as Isabella and Ferdinand's ships departed for Spain, I heard Torquemada's laughter carried on the wind, harmonizing with the entity's whispers. Our unified shadow would stretch across oceans, sanctifying atrocities with holy words, building empires on broken bodies.

The price of power, I'd learned, was not paid by those who wielded it. And now, with this unholy alliance, we had created something that would feed on suffering for centuries to come.

God's will be done, we all claimed. But in the darkness that bound us, I knew we served a different master now.

Months passed, and the Inquisition grew in power, its agents spreading throughout Spain like a plague. Africans all faced the White Flame’s wrath. I continued to act as if I commanded it all, though I was haunted by the knowledge that I, too, was trapped within their web. 

One night, Mendoza came to me again, this time with a pale face. “Majesty,” he whispered, “the White Flame has intercepted another decree from Rome—Il Moro are fleeing not just into African but also into northern Europe”, He held out the document, the inquisitors  must go after them.

# North Meets South: A Spanish Love Story in Tudor England

I first saw Oviedo in the spring of 1525, when he came to deliver bows for Queen Catherine's hunting party. His bearing spoke of northern Spain, of the green mountains of Asturias, so different from my Moorish homeland in Granada.

"Perdón, señorita," he said in Castilian Spanish, his accent carrying the crisp tones of the north. When our eyes met, I saw curiosity there – he was clearly intrigued by my darker features, so different from the fair complexions of his homeland.

"The armory is down the corridor," I managed to say, my hands clutching the folded sheets.

He smiled warmly. "I know where the armory is, señorita. I've delivered bows to this palace for three years now." His eyes held mine. "Though this is the first time I've had the fortune to meet you."

Later, as we grew closer, he would tell me of his homeland in Asturias – of misty mountains and green valleys so unlike my sun-baked Granada. We were a study in contrasts: he from the Christian north that had never known Moorish rule, I from the last Muslim kingdom in Spain. Yet in Tudor England, we found common ground.

"In England, we're both simply Spanish," he said one day, laughing. "They don't see north or south, Christian or converted. They see what we can offer, what we can make of ourselves here."

Our differences became our strength. I taught him the subtle art of Granada's craftwork, the intricate patterns I remembered from childhood. He shared with me the folk tales of Asturias, stories of the mountains and the sea. Together, we created something new – a marriage of north and south, of old Christian and new, of two Spanish hearts finding love in English soil.

Our daughters grew up hearing tales of both our homelands – the Alhambra's gardens from my memories, and the rugged peaks of Asturias from their father's. They learned that Spain, like love, contains multitudes, and that differences need not divide us.

In England, far from the rigid divisions of Spain, we found a freedom to love across boundaries that might have separated us in our homeland. Perhaps that was the greatest gift of our exile – the chance to see beyond the categories that others would place us in, to find love in unexpected places.

As I sit here now in Spain, watching my daughters grow, I often think of those tumultuous years. Sometimes I wonder if I should have spoken up during the great matter of the king's divorce. But then I remember Queen Catherine's kindness, how she treated me with dignity despite my station, and I know I made the right choice. Some loyalties transcend the bonds of slavery, some truths are better left unspoken, and some secrets we carry to our graves.

# Elizabeth Tudor 

I watch through the entity's endless darkness as Elizabeth Tudor kneels before the Anglican altar, but the shadows know – as I, Alfonso, have known through death and beyond – that breaking from Rome doesn't break the covenant. The darkness we created has grown beyond papal bulls and Catholic decrees. It hungers for something deeper than doctrine.

"Your Majesty," Lord Burghley approaches Elizabeth's private chamber, where she studies reports of "blackamoors" in her realm. "The Spanish threat grows."

Elizabeth's fingers trace the words on the parchment, and I see our shadow entity curl around her hand like an old friend. Though she's cast off Roman authority, the power we unleashed decades ago recognizes a kindred spirit.

"How interesting," she muses, "that despite our separation from Rome, we find ourselves pursuing similar... cleansing." The shadow pulses at her words.

"Indeed, Your Majesty. Though we've broken with the Catholic Church, certain... traditions... serve the Crown's interests."

Elizabeth rises, moving to the window. Below, in London's streets, I can see through the entity's eyes as dark-skinned faces move through the crowd – faces that will soon vanish.

"Draft the proclamation," she commands. "Word it carefully. We shall speak of 'great numbers of Negars and Blackamoors,' of burden to our realm."

The shadow that was born in Portugal, fed in Spain, now writhes with delight in Protestant England. It has learned to wear many faces: Inquisition, Reformation, Crown Proclamation. The words change, but the darkness remains.

"Your Majesty," a new voice – John Hawkins, her slave trade captain, enters. "The expedition is ready."

Elizabeth turns, and for a moment, I see Isabella's smile on her face. Different faith, same darkness.

"They say you're the first Englishman to profit from the slave trade," Elizabeth remarks. "How does it feel to make history?"

Hawkins bows. "Profitable history, Your Majesty. And with your proclamation to expel the 'blackamoors' already here..."

The shadow entity ripples with pleasure, remembering how it grew fat on Isabella's expelled Jews and Muslims. Now Elizabeth offers it new sacrifices, wrapped in Protestant righteousness instead of Catholic doctrine.

"We learn from history," Elizabeth says, though she doesn't know I'm listening through the centuries. "The Spanish Inquisition showed us how to purify a realm. We merely... adapt their methods to English sensibilities."

That night, as Elizabeth signs the proclamation ordering the expulsion, I watch our creation feed. The shadow born of Catholic Portugal, nurtured by Spanish Inquisition, now wraps Protestant England in its embrace. The covenant adapts, learns, grows stronger.

In her dreams, Elizabeth sees Isabella, sees me, sees the long line of monarchs who've fed the darkness. We're all connected now, Catholic and Protestant alike, bound by something older than churches, stronger than creeds.

"Send them hence," Elizabeth mutters in her sleep, echoing Isabella's words from decades past. The shadow entity caresses her face, accepting her offering.

I've watched through the years as our covenant morphed and spread. Rome's authority may waver, but the darkness we unleashed needs no Pope's blessing now. It speaks through Plymouth pulpits and London courts, through slave ship captains and royal proclamations.

The face changes – Catholic, Protestant, Crown, Church – but the shadow remains. Elizabeth may have broken with Rome, but some chains, I've learned, are stronger than papal bulls. Some darkness transcends mere religion.

In the depths of the entity, I hear the screams of centuries – African slaves, Spanish Jews, English "blackamoors" – all sacrificed to the same dark god, whether blessed by Pope or Crown.

Elizabeth sleeps fitfully, dreaming of purifying her realm. Through the shadow's eyes, I watch and remember how it all began with a simple papal bull, a document that unleashed something beyond our control.

God's will be done, Elizabeth whispers in her sleep.

The shadow entity laughs, and I weep, knowing that what we created will outlive crowns and creeds, feeding on humanity's darkness long after our names are forgotten.

The covenant endures. The shadow grows. And somewhere in the darkness, Isabella smiles.

# The Expelling Blackamoors erased and forced to leave England, the journey begins

Queen Elizabeth I issued a series of proclamations ordering the forced expulsion of blackamoors from her realm. The edicts were sharp, cold commands, designed to strip away the lives we had built. It was as though the very air in London thickened with dread as the news spread. The queen, under pressure from ministers and plagued by fears of social upheaval, had decreed that we, the blackamoors, were to be removed. Her letters, carried by couriers with grim faces, spoke of us as a burden, as if our presence sullied the purity of the realm.

# Memories of Exile: Isabella's Testament

From the writings of Isabella, daughter of Catalina of Motril, 1610\.

I write these words in remembrance of my mother Catalina and father Oviedo, and for the dark days that have followed. Though they did not live to see the worst of it, their stories burn in my heart as I witness our people scattered once again to the winds.

Mother died in 1558, father followed her two years later. They were the fortunate ones, passing before the storms truly broke. Elena and I inherited my father's bow-making trade and mother's position of respect within the community. But the world they knew – the England that had offered them sanctuary – began to change even before their passing.

I remember townspeople telling me of Queen Elizabeth's first letter about the "Blackamoors" in 1596\.  It cast a shadow over our community. "History repeats itself," the inquisitors are here but they don’t just want conversion now,  she said, her eyes distant with memories of Granada.

In our final conversation, she spoke of her own exile from Spain: "Mi querida, never forget that our story isn't just about loss – it's about survival. Your father and I found love in exile. Remember that hope can flower in the harshest soil."

Now, in 1610, I watch as news reaches us of the final expulsion of the Moriscos from Spain. The stories echo my mother's childhood tales – 300,000 souls forced from their homes under Philip III's decree. Families who had lived as Christians for generations, suddenly declared aliens in their own land.

Here in England, we've faced our own trials. Though some of us remain protected by our skills or connections, many of our community have been forced to seek new homes. John Blanke's grandchildren, who once played with my own children, have fled to the Netherlands. Others have gone to Venice, to Constantinople, to North Africa.

I think of my sister Elena, who sailed for Morocco last year. "Better to leave on our own terms," she said, "than wait for them to force us." Her words carried the weight of our mother's wisdom.

The expulsions here have been quieter than in Spain, but no less painful. They speak of "idle Blackamoors," but I see skilled craftspeople, merchants, musicians – people like my parents who helped build this country. When they took Maria's family – friends of our parents from the old days at court – they said it was for the "good order" of the realm.

Yet even in these dark times, I see glimpses of the world my parents knew. There are still English families who remember my father's bows, who value craft over creed. There are still those who, like Queen Catherine did for my mother, see the person beyond the prejudice.

I maintain our workshop, teaching my children both the bow-making skills of their grandfather and the secret patterns their grandmother brought from Granada. Each bow I make carries a tiny mark – a crescendo moon worked into the wood – in memory of both our losses and our persistence.

Some call these dark days, but mother would remind me that darkness passes. The Moriscos may be scattered from Spain, many of our people forced from England's shores, but we endure. We adapt. We remember.

As I write these words, my daughter Sofia practices her letters beside me. She asks about her grandmother, about Granada, about the England that was. I tell her what mother told me – that our story is not just one of exile, but of resilience. That even as borders close and powers rise and fall, the human heart remains capable of finding home in unexpected places.

Mother's last words to me were: "Cuida nuestra historia" – guard our story. And so I write, that our children may know of Catalina of Motril, who rose from slavery to find love and freedom in a foreign land. Of Oviedo of Asturias, who crossed the boundaries of old Spain to build a family in new soil. Of a time when Tudor England offered refuge, however brief, to those seeking a place to belong.

The world turns, and what was open closes, only to open again elsewhere. But our stories remain, passed down like precious jewels from mother to daughter, father to son. In them lives the truth that my parents knew – that love and courage can flower even in exile, that home is not just a place but a hope we carry in our hearts.

“We are not just shadows,” I whispered to Alaric as we read the proclamation nailed to the church door. The ink was bold, each word a nail in the coffin of our dreams. “We are flesh and blood.”

Alaric's jaw clenched, the muscle in his cheek twitching as he reached up to tear the notice down. “They’d rather forget us, erase us,” he muttered, crumpling the paper in his fist. “But we won’t go quietly.”

The erasure was not confined to proclamations. Soon, men loyal to the crown combed through the streets, removing and burning books, codices, and any documentation that bore testament to our existence and contributions. History itself was under siege. Libraries smoldered, their knowledge turned to ash in the hands of soldiers who wielded fire as a weapon against memory.

One night, as I sat in the dim glow of a sputtering candle, Kofi entered our small room, his eyes dark with worry. “They are destroying everything,” he said, his voice low. “The paintings, the stories—they are erasing our very roots.”

The story My friends would tell, then, begins with a chase—a chase that isn’t just a frantic dash through shadowed alleys but a race through centuries of resentment and survival, of empires built upon the bones of the displaced. I was one of them—a Moor forced from my home, my family stripped of land, of rights, and sometimes even of names.

We were “guests,” and a guest has a strange duty, to serve and entertain but to leave nothing lasting, nothing permanent. My father used to say, "We are like the lanterns at their feasts, to be blown out when the revelry ends." It was an existence that seemed to hang by a thread, constantly shifting. And now, here I was, caught in a final sprint as I dodged through the twisting alleys of Toledo, with Christian soldiers in pursuit.

"Stop\! Stop, you dog\!" one of the soldiers shouted, his voice echoing against the stone walls, and I felt the bite of resentment even in his tone. To them, I was no more than a nuisance, a remnant of an empire they’d rather erase. I darted around a corner, my feet pounding against the cobblestones, pushing me to where the old Moorish quarter faded into ruins, where memories of a life we once called our own lingered like ghosts.

A voice called out from the darkness. “This way\!” It was Yusuf, a fellow Moor who had managed to hide among the merchants of Toledo. He’d been feeding me information on the movements of the guards, on whispers of revolt and of allies in waiting.

I ducked into the shadows where Yusuf stood, pulling me to crouch low behind an ancient column. We waited, hearts thundering, as the soldiers’ footsteps thundered past us, oblivious to the fleeting refuge we’d found. Yusuf leaned in close, his voice a whisper as intense as a scream. “You know why they want us gone, right? Because they know, my friend. They know that their empires aren’t built for people like us.”

“And what of it?” I spat back. “We’re only guests, after all. What power do we have?”

“Exactly.” Yusuf’s eyes gleamed. “We have no power but that which they’ve made for us—gold, trade, money. They need us only so long as it suits them, and after that…” He drew his finger across his throat in a slow, symbolic gesture. “They believe they’ll bury us beneath their own ambition.”

“Not if I can help it,” I murmured, clenching my fists, my mind racing to the stories I’d been told as a child—the tales of our people’s expulsion, of the ruthless edicts stripping us of our professions and land, forcing us into the role of moneylenders and merchants. They used us like tools, Yusuf had once said. We were the foundation of their economies, yet held in contempt, always seen as temporary, as expendable.

“Are you ready?” Yusuf asked, and I nodded, feeling a surge of defiance rising within me. This was not just about survival anymore. This was about fighting back against the empire that had tried to erase us, about reclaiming something they had never understood—the resilience of the dispossessed.

The chase had become something more.

As we sprinted out from behind the column, we could hear the soldiers’ voices again, closer this time. They must have doubled back, sensing we hadn’t gone far. We dashed through the alleyways, zigzagging through the heart of the city, but now, instead of feeling like the hunted, I felt like the hunter. We’d made a plan, and we were ready to strike.

We rounded a final corner and found ourselves in an open square, the soldiers charging toward us from all sides. Their leader, a tall man with a grim face, sneered as he spotted us.

“End of the line, Moors,” he spat, his sword glinting in the moonlight.

Yusuf and I exchanged a glance, and in that moment, I felt the full weight of our ancestors, of the Moorish warriors who had once defended these lands. I held my ground as the soldiers advanced, defiance burning in my eyes.

One of the soldiers lunged, and I sidestepped, catching his arm and twisting it. He cried out in pain, dropping his weapon, which I swiftly picked up. The next soldier hesitated, giving Yusuf enough time to swing a heavy stick into his leg, sending him toppling to the ground. The fight was chaotic, a flurry of limbs and weapons, of clashing wills. Each blow I struck was a reminder—to them, and to myself—that I was not a tool to be discarded, that my people were more than the roles they had been forced into.

After what felt like an eternity, the last soldier fell to the ground, groaning in pain. I stood, breathing heavily, the sword clenched in my hand. The city was still, and the only sound was the ragged breaths of the fallen men around us.

“You think we are guests,” I said to the leader, who lay sprawled at my feet, clutching his wounded arm. “But it is your empire that is temporary. We are the ones who endure.”

With that, Yusuf and I turned and slipped into the shadows once more. The empire would keep hunting, keep conquering, keep displacing. But we, the exiled and the displaced, would survive. And we would remember.

A knock on the door silenced us both. It was the signal—three taps, a pause, and one more. Alaric stepped forward, his eyes meeting mine. “Go to the docks,” he instructed. “Take the others and wait for the ship. We will not let them break us.”

The journey across the sea was brutal, the waves lashing at the wooden hulls as if in protest of our forced exodus. When we finally reached the shores of West Africa, we were not the same. We had been displaced, but not defeated. We found refuge among the Limba, who welcomed us with wary curiosity. They saw in our eyes the same defiance they bore, the mark of people who knew loss intimately.

“We will make a life here,” I said to the gathered crowd of weary faces. The jungle stretched around us, dense and unyielding. But there, in that enclave, we began anew, building towns with walls made not just of wood and stone, but of shared pain and hope.

Ships from England came and went, bearing more of our kin, more souls cast out by a kingdom that wished to forget us. Each arrival brought stories of the burning cities, the rewritten histories, the whispers of who we once were now forbidden even in the shadowed corners of taverns.

Over generations, our community grew resilient. We spoke English, but it mingled with the rhythms of Limba tongues. We planted seeds from England alongside those that thrived under the African sun. And when men from England came once more, their intentions were different. They spoke of abolition, of a rising tide that would shatter the chains of bondage and challenge the empire’s cruelty.

“The truth is out,” said James Thornton, his voice echoing through the council hut. His arrival brought news that left us stunned. “The world knows what the crown has done. A mutiny—a ship of slaves revolted, carrying proof back to the shores of England. The abolition movement is unstoppable now.”

“How?” I asked, my hands trembling as I recalled the smoldering ruins of our past.

“They spoke,” Thornton said. “Voices that refused to be silenced.”

And so, hope kindled in our hearts. We were more than exiles, more than ghosts banished to the fringes of the world. We were resilient, remembered, and soon, we would be free.

I remember the chaos as if it were yesterday—the thunderous crack of musket fire, the clang of swords against shields, and the anguished cries of my kin as we fought for our lives. The sky was a bruised purple, the smell of rain mingling with smoke and blood. It was 1596, and we were on the shores of this new land, thrust into an unfamiliar world that would soon test every fiber of our being.

I was one of the Blackamoors expelled from England under the orders of Queen Elizabeth I, a monarch whose proclamations turned us from equals into outcasts overnight. Our arrival in West Africa was both a relief and a curse. The dense rainforest and the ancient Limba tribe offered us refuge, a sanctuary from the cold, scrutinizing eyes of London, yet the peace was fragile.

The Limba were generous and wise, their chiefs adorned in vibrant, hand-woven garments. They welcomed us with gifts of kola nuts and cassava, their language a symphony of lilting tones and rhythmic laughter. Over time, we became part of their story, sharing in harvest festivals and learning the art of carving intricate masks that held the spirits of our ancestors. I found joy there, fleeting though it was. My friend Musa, a warrior with eyes like the storm clouds, said to me once with a grin, “Elias, you are more Limba than Blackamoor now. Your heart beats with our drums.”

But peace has a way of hiding in the shadows, waiting for a breathless moment to shatter. It came when the Mane tribes descended like locusts, their war cries slicing through the warm, humid air. They wore iron armor and moved like dark phantoms, consuming villages and enslaving the defeated. Their eyes were vacant, as if driven by a hunger far greater than conquest—a hunger for power, for trade.

“Run\!” Musa screamed as the Mane warriors stormed into our village. I saw him raise his spear, only to be cut down by a Mane chieftain with a broad, cruel sword. The grief that flooded me was enough to drown a man, but I ran. The Limba, my people, were captured in droves, chained together, their fate sealed by the clicks of manacles and the dark promises whispered by the Mane to the Europeans waiting at the coast.

The day the first exchange was made was seared into my memory. The port, once a place of bustling trade and shared stories, now bore a grim silence punctuated only by the lapping of the waves. Kofi, the Mane chieftain, strode onto the deck of a trader ship with a young girl from the Limba tribe, her small frame trembling as she clutched the hem of her torn garment. Her name was Abena, and she had been known for her laughter that rippled like the river. Now, her eyes were wide with terror.

“This,” Kofi said, gripping Abena’s arm tightly and presenting her to Sir Reginald Harcourt, “is my payment for your beads.” He spoke with a calmness that belied the monstrous nature of the act.

Sir Reginald’s eyes gleamed with satisfaction as he reached out to touch the girl’s face, his fingers leaving a trail of shivers in their wake. “She’ll do,” he said, signaling for his men to take her. The crew, hardened by years of profit and disregard, moved quickly, binding Abena and leading her away as if she were nothing more than cargo.

“Please\!” Abena’s voice cracked, a single plea before she was silenced by the rough shove of a sailor. The sight of her disappearing into the dark hold of the ship was the point where the world I knew crumbled. It was the alpha gamma moment, the instant the tide of history shifted, pulling countless souls into an abyss that would span centuries. The first payment of human life for beads—a simple transaction that paved the road for the transatlantic slave trade.

It was we Blackamoors, familiar with the language and customs of the Europeans, who became the brokers of communication between the tribes and the traders. The heat was relentless, the air heavy with the buzzing of mosquitoes that left welts on our skin. Crocodiles lurked in the murky rivers, silent witnesses to the bartering of lives. The Europeans, wary of disease and the lethal wildlife, never ventured far inland. They relied on us, the Blackamoors, to navigate the treacherous ground and arrange the trade.

Kofi’s ambition was insatiable. “More guns,” he demanded, the weight of his words hanging like a death sentence over the captured Limba. “For that, I will need boatloads.” The traders’ faces flickered with greed. They knew guns would fetch a high price, but the price was paid in bodies—men, women, and children loaded onto ships, their fates sealed with iron and rope.

Years passed, and the town we had built grew more haunted with each cycle of the moon. We Blackamoors, those who survived the initial onslaught, learnt to fight back. We fortified our walls, sharpened our spears, and trained for battles that came relentlessly. I became a leader, driven by the memory of Musa and the broken look in Adama’s eyes.

“We will take back our people,” I vowed one evening as our fires crackled under the starless sky. A young man named Kwame, brave and loyal, nodded with fierce determination. “We fight not just for us, but for them,” he said, his voice steady.

But the true twist came decades later, long after Kofi had grown rich and the Mane had entrenched themselves in a network of betrayal with the Europeans. The seeds of resistance were sown in the quiet whispers of the enslaved and the hidden letters exchanged between abolitionists in Europe and Africa. One of those voices belonged to a man named Thomas Clarkson, a scholar who risked everything to meet with me, hidden under the cover of night.

“Elias,” he said urgently, “there is a change coming. We will end this trade, but I need your stories, your truth.”

The years of bloodshed, the screams, the betrayals—they became more than memories; they became testimonies that Clarkson brought back to England. The tide shifted with a speed that surprised even me. The British public, stirred by stories of cruelty and courage, began to rise against the traders.

In 1807, the Slave Trade Act was passed, a piece of parchment that carried the weight of millions of souls. But for us, the fight continued. We rebuilt, reclaimed, and remembered.

On the day word reached our village, Kwame, now older but unbroken, stood beside me. “We have won this battle,” he said, “but we must teach the next generation so that they never forget.”

I nodded, the faces of the lost and the freed burning in my mind. “Hope,” I whispered, “is a fire we will keep alive.”

# The Webs We Weave: Further Memories of the Expulsions

Continued writings of Isabella, daughter of Catalina of Motril, 1611\.

The complexities of these times weigh heavy on my mind as I continue to record our history. Mother always said there were layers to every story, just as there were layers to her position in Queen Catherine's household. Now I understand what she meant more than ever.

Last night, I discovered hidden letters in my mother's old chest – correspondence between her and Elena after my sister fled to Morocco. They reveal a network I never knew existed, one that helped our people survive these troubled times:

My dearest Isabella,  
The situation in Tétouan is better than expected. Many families from Granada have established themselves here, maintaining their crafts and customs while openly practicing their faith again. You wouldn't believe it, but I've found cousins of Mother's old neighbors. They speak of underground routes through France and Italy, helping our people reach safety. Remember Jorge, the merchant who bought Father's bows? His brother runs a trading company that "loses" Morisco passengers among their cargo between Bristol and Salé...

The letters paint a picture more intricate than the simple edicts would suggest. While Queen Elizabeth's orders and Philip III's expulsion seemed absolute on paper, the reality was a web of resistance, adaptation, and survival.

In London, I've watched how our community has developed subtle signals and safe houses. The bow workshop serves as more than just a business now – the crescendo moon mark that I carve as remembrance has become a secret sign to those who know where to look. When a displaced family needs shelter or help reaching the continent, they know to ask for "Oviedo's special bow design."

The authorities aren't blind to these networks, but there's a fascinating dance of power and pragmatism. Lord Burlington still demands my finest bows for his hunting parties, even while supporting the expulsion edicts. Last month, he turned a blind eye when his own steward, a converted Morisco, helped three families escape to the Netherlands.

"Your mother would understand this game well," my old friend María wrote before she left for Venice. "She knew how to navigate the court's politics during Queen Catherine's troubles. Sometimes survival means playing many parts at once."

The expulsions have revealed surprising allies. Jewish merchants, themselves familiar with persecution, have helped establish safe routes through their trading networks. Some Protestant families, opposing Catholic Spain's actions, shelter Moriscos despite Elizabeth's orders. Even certain Catholic priests, remembering Queen Catherine's kindness to her Spanish servants, quietly assist those in need.

In Spain, the situation grows more complex. Letters smuggled from Toledo tell of Moriscos who officially "converted" and left for North Africa, only to return under new identities as Portuguese merchants. Others merged into remote mountain communities where records are sparse and questions few. The Church itself is divided – some priests zealously enforce the expulsion while others help their Morisco parishioners hide or escape.

Mother's old friend Beatriz, who served with her in Queen Catherine's household, writes from Rome:

\*My position in Cardinal Borghese's household provides interesting insights. The Pope himself has reservations about Philip III's methods, seeing them as too harsh and potentially damaging to Spain's economy. There are wheels within wheels, Isabella. The public face of policy hides many private doubts.\*

I think of my mother's stories about the fall of Granada, how she survived by understanding that power is never as absolute as it appears. She taught me that persecution creates not just victims but also resistance, not just flight but also adaptation.

Here in my workshop, I train apprentices from all backgrounds – English, Morisco, African. Knowledge and craft, mother always said, are harder to exile than people. When my son Miguel works beside Thomas, the English boy from Southwark, I see her wisdom. They share techniques, stories, dreams – the boundaries that others create mean nothing before the common language of craft and friendship.

The official records will show only the edicts, the numbers, and the apparent success of expulsion policies. But our true story lives in the shadows between these official lines – in the hidden rooms behind taverns, in the coded messages carried by traveling merchants, in the quiet defiance of those who choose to help rather than turn away.

Elena's latest letter speaks of growing communities in Fez and Tétouan, where expelled families rebuild their lives while maintaining connections across Europe. They're not just surviving but adapting, creating new traditions that blend their various experiences of exile. Some even find ways to maintain trade links with their old homes, proving that human connections often survive political barriers.

Mother's story was not unique – a Moorish slave girl rising to become a trusted royal servant, finding love across social boundaries, building a life in a foreign land. But perhaps its very complexity made it typical of our people's experience. We are masters of adaptation, finding spaces to exist between the rigid lines others try to draw.

As I write these words, my youngest daughter practices her letters in both English and Arabic, while my eldest helps an escaped Morisco family arrange passage to Amsterdam. This too is resistance – ensuring our stories, our knowledge, our connections survive.

Mother would say that survival isn't just about avoiding death or exile – it's about maintaining dignity, preserving culture, and finding ways to thrive even in hostile soil. The expulsions continue, but so does our capacity to adapt, to help each other, to weave new patterns from old threads.

Let those who read these words in future years know: behind every edict of expulsion ran a current of resistance, behind every official story lay countless untold tales of survival and solidarity. This too is our heritage, as vital as the crafts we practice or the prayers we whisper.

In the end, perhaps that's what mother meant about layers – that between persecution and survival, between power and resistance, lies the true story of our people.

# Secret Signs and Sacred Trust: The Hidden Lodges

Further writings of Isabella, daughter of Catalina of Motril, 1612\.

Tonight I write by candlelight in our secret meeting place beneath the old church near Thames Street. Mother's silver crescent pendant weighs heavy around my neck – our most treasured sign of belonging. What I record here must be written carefully, for the knowledge of our ways has been bought with blood.

The lodge meetings began in the shadows of Queen Elizabeth's first deportation orders. We learned quickly after the betrayal at Southwark, where Thomas Moore – a converted Moor who sought favor with authorities – led the sheriff's men to a gathering of fifteen families. None were ever seen again.

Our protocols grew from bitter experience:

"\*La luna creciente brilla en la noche oscura\*," I whisper at the door.  
"\*Y guía a los viajeros perdidos a casa\*," comes the response.

The recognition phrases change monthly, but always speak of moons and journeys – remembrances of our nights fleeing Granada. Three gentle knocks, pause, then two more. The door opens only a crack, and I make the sign – right hand over heart, then a subtle gesture like a crescent moon.

Inside, the basement room is warm with lamplight. Faces I trust gathered around tables laid with food brought by each family – dishes that remind us of home. Elena was right to establish this system before she fled to Morocco. We share bread, we share news, we share plans for survival.

"Sister Isabella," Ahmed whispers, his English accent betraying his birth in Bristol. "We have a word from Dover. Three families need passage next week."

I recognize his silver ring with the hidden crescent design – a marker I crafted myself, each one slightly different, each one registered in our secret ledger. We learned to be cautious after the Canterbury betrayal, where forged tokens led to arrests.

"How many children?" I ask, making the subtle hand signal that confirms my authority to arrange passage.

"Two small ones. Their father was a craftsman like your father – he can pay with skills."

Our meetings follow strict protocols. New members can only be brought by two existing ones who stake their lives on the newcomer's loyalty. They must wait through three meetings before learning any sensitive information, all while being quietly observed.

María, whose grandmother served with my mother in Queen Catherine's household, leads the initiation rituals. She teaches the layers of signs – the public signals that can be used in marketplaces or church squares, the secret words that change with moon phases, the genuine tokens that cannot be copied.

"Remember," she often says, "we are not like the Freemasons who meet to celebrate their craft. We meet to survive."

Tonight we have two new families seeking protection. I watch them complete the ceremony of trust – sharing bread dipped in salt, speaking their lineage back to Spain, showing the marks on their bodies that prove their stories of escape. The room falls silent as they take the oath:

"By the light of the crescent moon, by the memory of Granada, by the blood of our fathers and mothers, we swear to protect our brothers and sisters. May our tongues wither if we betray this trust."

The ledger records everything in our secret cipher – a blend of Arabic numbers and Spanish merchant codes that Mother learned in her youth. Each family's needs, each planned escape, each safe house and trusted captain is noted in ways only we can read.

But we are more than just an escape network. In these basement rooms, we keep our culture alive. Children learn our stories in whispers, women share the old songs of Granada, men pass down craft secrets from father to son. We are a people in hiding, but we refuse to disappear.

"Your mother would be proud," old Fatima tells me, her hands gnarled from years of weaving. "She understood that survival means more than just staying alive."

I think of Mother's stories about the first days after Granada fell – how our people learned to hide their faith behind Christian smiles, to keep their culture alive in secret rooms and whispered prayers. Now we do the same in England, adapting her lessons to our new perils.

The meetings always end the same way – people leaving one by one, in different directions, at carefully spaced intervals. Some wear crosses to deflect suspicion, others carry trade tools that mark them as useful craftspeople. All wear some small token of our trust – a crescent worked into a belt buckle, a particular pattern of stitching on a sleeve, marks that say to those who know: "You are not alone."

Later, in my workshop, I add new entries to the ledger hidden in the false bottom of Father's old tool chest. Tomorrow I will carve new tokens for the newcomers, each one unique, each one a small act of defiance against those who would scatter us to the winds.

Mother's last letter to Elena spoke of the importance of remembering: "Our strength lies not in fighting power with power, but in helping each other survive until better days come." Looking around our hidden lodge tonight, seeing faces lit by candlelight and hope, I know we keep that wisdom alive.

The Moor lodges were born of persecution and survival. In these secret rooms, we keep alive the spirit of Granada, the memory of Al-Andalus, the hope of better days to come. Until then, we meet in shadows, speak in whispers, and protect our own.

May those who read these words in safer times understand: when they tried to scatter us, we formed bonds stronger than steel. When they drove us into the shadows, we learned to see in the dark. When they sought to destroy us, we became undying.

# The Skeptic King

Through the entity's eternal shadow, I watched as James VI of Scotland crossed the English border, his face a mask of barely concealed disbelief. The Scottish king, who would soon become James I of England, kept glancing at his retinue as if expecting someone to shout that this was all an elaborate jest.

"They cannot be serious," he muttered to his closest advisor, fingers nervously twisting his rings. "Elizabeth's throne? Just like that?"
