But the shadow entity that had fed on three kingdoms' worth of darkness knew better. It curled around James like smoke as he approached London, tasting his skepticism, savoring his approaching corruption.

The coronation chamber was thick with anticipation. I could feel Elizabeth's lingering presence – our dark covenant had marked this space well during her reign. James stepped into Westminster with the same expression he'd worn crossing the border: half-expecting jesters to jump out and reveal the grand trick.

"Your Majesty," Lord Cecil bowed deeply. "Welcome to your throne."

James barked a laugh that echoed strangely in the ancient hall. "My throne? Just like that, eh? No battles? No poisonings? No mysterious accidents?"

The shadow entity writhed with amusement. This one was different – too scholarly, too cynical, too aware of power's usual price to believe in its free bestowal.

"It is yours by right of blood, Your Majesty," Cecil continued smoothly. "Through your great-grandmother Margaret Tudor."

James approached the throne slowly, like a man expecting it to vanish upon touch. The entity's tendrils reached for him, recognizing the potential for fresh darkness in this skeptic king.

"And what's the real price?" James asked suddenly, his hand hovering over the throne's arm. "There's always a price."

I watched through the shadows as Cecil's smile tightened. "Your Majesty need only continue... certain policies."

"Ah," James's eyes narrowed. "Elizabeth's policies?"

The shadow pulsed eagerly as Cecil produced familiar documents – proclamations against "blackamoors," charts of slave trade routes, reports of religious dissidents.

"The realm's stability depends on... consistency," Cecil chose his words carefully.

James finally sat on the throne, and I felt the entity surge with triumph. The skeptic king might not believe in freely given power, but he would learn – as we all had – about power's true cost.

"Tell me," James leaned forward, suddenly intense, "about these witch hunts I've heard so much about."

The shadow entity nearly purred with pleasure. This skeptical scholar-king who'd arrived expecting a joke was already asking the right questions. Where Elizabeth had fed it with proclamations and slave ships, James would offer it new flavors of darkness.

That night, alone in his new chambers, James pored over Elizabeth's private papers. The entity swirled around him as he read, and I watched his face change in the candlelight.

"Fascinating," he murmured, finding the threads that connected Elizabeth's policies to Isabella's Inquisition, to my own papal bull. "It's all connected, isn't it?"

The shadow answered with a whisper that made him shiver. James looked up, his scholar's skepticism warring with growing awareness of something vast and dark in his new kingdom.

"I thought it was a joke," he spoke to the darkness. "That they'd just... give me England. But it's not England they're giving me, is it?"

The entity reached for him, and for the first time, James didn't flinch.

"Show me," he commanded, and in that moment, I saw his transformation begin. The skeptic who'd arrived expecting a jest was gone. In his place sat a king who would write treaties on demonology, who would escalate witch hunts, who would deepen the slave trade's wounds.

The covenant had found its new vessel, and James's initial disbelief made his eventual embrace of the darkness even sweeter. Through the entity's eternal vision, I watched as the Scottish king who'd thought it all a joke became England's most dedicated student of dark powers.

"To think I almost laughed it off," James mused later, signing his first proclamation continuing Elizabeth's policies. The shadow entity wrapped around him like a lover's embrace.

In the depths of the darkness, I heard Isabella's laughter join with Elizabeth's. The covenant would feed well on this skeptic turned believer.

James looked up from his parchment, directly at where I watched through the shadows. "I understand now," he whispered. "The price of power isn't in gaining it. The price is in keeping it."

The entity pulsed with approval. The skeptic king had learned his lesson well.

And somewhere in the darkness, I wept for what was coming next.

# The King's Bible and Forbidden Love

Through the shadow entity's eternal gaze, I watched as James reveled in his unprecedented power, sprawled across his throne with a distinctly unkingly smirk. The darkness we'd created swirled around him, amused by this most unconventional vessel.

"George," James called out, his voice honeyed with affection. The Duke of Buckingham stepped from the shadows, young and beautiful, their forbidden romance obvious to all who dared to look.

"Your Majesty," George Villiers bowed with a flourish that was almost mocking. "Shall we... pray together?"

The entity rippled with dark humor as James laughed openly. Here was a king who wore our covenant differently – not with Isabella's religious fervor or Elizabeth's stern propriety, but with scandalous abandon.

Archbishop Bancroft burst into the chamber, his face ashen. "Your Majesty\! The rumors... the talk in the streets..."

"Yes?" James drawled, deliberately letting his hand brush George's as he accepted a goblet of wine. "What rumors trouble the Church today?"

"They say..." Bancroft's voice dropped to a horrified whisper, "They say the King of Great Britain, Head of the Church, Defender of the Faith... cavorts with..."

"With extremely handsome young men?" James finished, amused. "Do tell me more about what they say."

The shadow entity coiled excitedly. This was new territory – a king who fed it not through pious proclamations of faith, but through gleeful sacrilege.

"Something must be done," Bancroft insisted. "Your Majesty's image..."

"And what do you propose?" James asked, still not bothering to put distance between himself and George.

"A new Bible translation," Bancroft blurted out. "Authorized by Your Majesty himself. It will show your dedication to the faith, your divine right to rule unified Britain."

The entity stirred with interest, and I felt its ancient hunger shift. A new Bible, born not of piety but of scandal-covering? This was a delicious twist in our dark covenant's evolution.

"A Bible for everyone to read?" James mused, suddenly scholarly. "No more hanging translators? How... progressive of you, Archbishop."

"The people need to see their king as God's chosen vessel," Bancroft pressed. "Not as..."

"As what I am?" James finished, standing to pace the chamber. The shadow followed him, curious about this new manipulation of holy words.

Later that night, alone with George in his private chambers, James laughed over the irony. "They think a new Bible will cleanse my reputation? Let them translate. I'll be too busy unifying kingdoms and enjoying my power."

"And enjoying other things," George added with a wicked smile.

The entity wrapped around them both, savoring this novel corruption. Where Isabella had fed it with religious persecution and Elizabeth with racial expulsion, James offered it something new – the delicious hypocrisy of a church desperately rewriting holy words to cover royal sins.

"Forty-seven scholars," James mused, reading the translation proposal. "All to make sure people forget their king loves men."

"And yet," George observed, "you'll be remembered for this Bible long after our scandal is forgotten."

The shadow pulsed with appreciation for the irony. In trying to distract from James's transgressive love, the Church would create his greatest legacy.

I watched through the centuries as scholars gathered at Westminster, Hampton Court, Oxford, and Cambridge. They labored to create a masterpiece of English prose, never fully knowing they served not just God or King, but our dark covenant's endless hunger for twisted faith.

"Let there be light," James quoted mockingly, pulling George into an embrace as he read the first translated pages. The shadow entity rippled with pleasure at the blasphemy.

The King James Bible would indeed become his greatest monument – words of light born from darkness, divine truth prompted by earthly scandal, holy scripture authorized by a king who gleefully broke God's laws in his private chambers.

"They'll read this in churches for centuries," George noted, tracing the ornate letters.

"While we have our own worship here," James replied with a smirk.

The entity fed well during James's reign. His union of Scotland and England expanded its hunting grounds. His religious hypocrisy added new flavor to its ancient appetite. Even his scandalous love served its purpose, forcing the Church to unknowingly further our dark covenant through their desperate attempt at distraction.

I watched it all through the shadow's eternal eyes, marveling at how far we'd come from my simple papal bull. James had transformed our covenant, showing that darkness could wear a laughing face, that sacred words could spring from scandal, that power and pleasure could mingle in ways Isabella never dreamed.

And somewhere in the darkness, I heard the entity laugh with James's irreverent mirth, preparing for whatever delicious corruption the next generation would bring.

"God save the King," George whispered in the dark.

"God clearly has," James replied with a knowing smile.

And the covenant fed on, growing stronger with each holy word written to hide unholy love.

# Tongues of Empire

Through the entity's eternal darkness, I watched James's transformation complete itself. The once-jovial king who'd laughed at power's games had become something far more terrible. His Bible, meant to cover scandal, became a weapon of cultural annihilation.

"Speak English or don't speak at all," James declared, watching Highland chiefs kneel before him. The shadow entity coiled around his throne, gorging itself on the death of ancient tongues.

"Your Majesty," a Highland chief pleaded in Gaelic. "Our people have spoken this language since—"

"Since before time?" James interrupted in English, sneering. "Then let time end. George, show them their choices."

The Duke of Buckingham, no longer just a lover but now an instrument of empire, unrolled a parchment. "Renounce your tongue and adopt the King's English, or face... transportation."

The shadow pulsed at that euphemism. Transportation – a pretty word for enslavement, for cultural death, for families torn apart and shipped to Virginia's tobacco fields.

"And the Irish?" George asked later, in their private chambers.

James's laugh had lost its playful edge, becoming something that matched our entity's hunger. "Ireland will learn to pray in English or not pray at all."

I watched through the darkness as James's Bible became a test of loyalty, a tool of empire. Speaking Gaelic or Irish became proof of barbarism, justification for enslavement. The covenant fed on each forced conversion, each forgotten word, each child punished for speaking their mother's tongue.

But it was the "blackamoors" who suffered most silently. Their pain wasn't even granted the dignity of record.

"No need to document their removal," James instructed his scribes. "They were never truly here."

The shadow entity writhed with especial pleasure at this erasure. Elizabeth had expelled them openly, but James perfected their disappearance – not just from England's shores, but from its memory.

One night, as James read his new Bible by candlelight, a "blackamoor" woman was dragged before him.

"She refuses to leave, Your Majesty," the guard reported. "Claims she was born here."

James barely looked up from his Bible. "Born here? In what tongue does she claim this?"

"In English, Your Majesty," the woman spoke clearly, defiantly.

The shadow entity stirred with interest as James finally raised his eyes. Here was something new – someone who'd adopted the master's tongue but couldn't hide their face.

"You speak our language well," James observed coldly. "But England's face is changing. There's no place for yours in it."

"I learned your words," she responded. "Learned your God. Learned your ways."

"Learned?" James closed his Bible with a snap. "Like a parrot learns to mimic? No. This English tongue belongs to English faces. George, make sure she's on the next ship out. And ensure no record mentions her... eloquent protest."

I watched through centuries of shadow as James's reign redefined belonging. The Gaels could possibly earn it through complete submission, the Irish might achieve it through generations of compliance, but for the "blackamoors," there was no path to acceptance – not even perfect English could save them.

The entity feasted on this hierarchy of suffering. James had evolved our covenant beyond simple religious persecution or racial expulsion. He'd created a ladder of humanity, with his English tongue at the top and unrecorded darkness at the bottom.

"A unified Britain," James would proclaim publicly, his Bible held high. "One tongue, one faith, one people."

But in the shadows where I watched, the truth was darker. Unity achieved through erasure. Power maintained through selective memory. History written by surviving tongues.

The Gaels who were shipped away at least left traces in ship manifests. The Irish who starved and suffered appeared in military reports. But the "blackamoors" who vanished under James's reign became ghosts in their own time – shadows our entity consumed so completely that even their disappearance disappeared.

"Let them speak English in heaven," James joked once to George, signing another expulsion order. "For they'll find no voice on English earth."

The covenant had evolved beyond my darkest imaginings. From my papal bull's simple authority over "unbelievers," it had grown into something that could erase not just lives but memory itself. James's Bible would survive as a monument of English prose, while those who vanished under his reign wouldn't even merit a footnote.

And the shadow entity fed on, growing fat on forgotten words, lost names, and unrecorded tears.

"God's word in English," James would proudly proclaim.

But through the darkness, I heard all the words that would never be spoken again, in any tongue.

# The crescent moon still guides lost travelers home

The lantern's light flickered, casting long, dancing shadows on the cracked walls of the abandoned granary. I pulled my cloak tighter around me, the cold dampness of the night seeping into my bones. The whispers of the others echoed softly around the room, blending with the distant cries of the city under curfew. Granada was no longer ours; it hadn't been for decades.

I adjusted the ring on my finger—a plain band engraved with a crescent moon and a star, subtle enough to avoid attention yet distinct enough to those who knew. It was our silent mark, a signal of trust and allegiance. The secret lodges were our last refuge, a place where we could speak freely, plan our survival, and reaffirm our purpose in a world that had turned against us.

"Brother Tariq," a voice called softly. I turned to see Ahmed, his face half-hidden in the shadow of his hood. "The others are ready. Shall we begin?"

I nodded and walked to the center of the room, where a simple wooden table stood. Around it sat familiar faces—men and women who, like me, lived in constant fear of betrayal. The air was heavy with tension, the kind that comes from knowing that one slip, one misplaced word, could mean the end for us all.

"My brothers, my sisters," I began, my voice low but steady, "tonight, we gather not just to survive, but to remember who we are. We may be hunted, enslaved, and betrayed, but our spirit is unbroken. We are builders of hope, seekers of truth, and servants of the Grand Architect of the Universe."

A murmur of agreement rippled through the room. Some crossed their arms over their chests in a silent gesture of solidarity, while others touched their rings or pendants—a fleeting moment of unity in a fractured world.

"Before we proceed," I continued, "we must ensure our circle is secure. Brother Hamid, will you lead the ritual?"

Hamid, an elder with eyes as sharp as a hawk's, stepped forward. His presence commanded respect. "Let us confirm our bonds of trust," he said. "Each of you will step forward, state your purpose, and demonstrate the sign of the crescent."

One by one, the members of the lodge approached Hamid, offering a coded phrase and performing a subtle hand gesture—a sweeping motion over the chest that ended with fingers touching the heart. It was a ritual born of necessity, a safeguard against infiltrators. We had been betrayed before; we could not afford it again.

When it was my turn, I stepped forward and met Hamid's gaze. "I am Tariq, a builder of faith and a seeker of freedom," I said, performing the gesture. He nodded, and I returned to my seat.

As the last member completed the ritual, the tension in the room eased slightly. Hamid spoke again. "The Grand Architect watches over us tonight. Let us proceed."

The discussions began—whispers of routes to the coast, where some had secured passage to lands beyond the Inquisition's reach; strategies for smuggling food and supplies to families hiding in the mountains; and the introduction of new members who had risked everything to join our cause.

At one point, a young woman named Layla stood and addressed the group. "I have word of a safe house in Valencia," she said. "But the journey is perilous. The roads are heavily patrolled, and there are rumors of informants among our own."

"Trust no one outside this circle," Ahmed warned. "Even our own blood can betray us."

The room fell silent, the weight of his words sinking in. We had all lost someone—family, friends, neighbors—to the zealots or the chains of slavery. The betrayal of a fellow Moor was a wound that never healed.

"We must be invisible," I said, breaking the silence. "Our movements, our words, even our thoughts—they must escape the eyes of those who seek to destroy us. The lodges are our sanctuary, but they are only as strong as our unity."

As the meeting continued, the sense of purpose grew. Though we were few, our resolve was unshakable. We were builders, not of stone, but of hope and resilience. The Grand Architect had no name, no face, only the promise of order and meaning in a chaotic world.

When the meeting ended, we dispersed in silence, slipping into the night like shadows. As I walked the narrow streets back to my quarters, the ring on my finger caught the moonlight, a small but constant reminder of who I was and what I stood for.

In a world that sought to erase us, we had found a way to endure. And though the Inquisition's eyes were everywhere, we would continue to build—our lodges, our faith, and, one day, our freedom.

The air in Greenwich was damp, and the fog rolled in from the Thames like a shroud, smothering the city in a heavy silence. The streets were empty at this hour, save for the faint clatter of hooves and the occasional distant cry of a watchman. I pulled my cloak tightly around me, keeping my face hidden, and quickened my pace toward the rendezvous point. 

It had been months since the tragedy, but the memory still burned like fresh fire. We had trusted too deeply, loved too openly, and paid the price for it. Brother Kamal had called this meeting, but even now, with all our precautions, the shadows felt longer, and the walls seemed to have ears.

I reached the small, unassuming bakery near the dockside, its shutters closed and lanterns extinguished. The scent of yesterday’s bread still lingered faintly, a cruel reminder of the lives we once lived before trust became a luxury we could no longer afford. I knocked twice, then once more, before a slit in the door opened.

“Speak your purpose,” came the voice on the other side.

“I build, though the stone crumbles. I seek, though the path is shrouded,” I replied, reciting the phrase we all now lived by. The slit closed, and I heard the heavy bolts being drawn back.

Inside, the lodge was dimly lit by a single oil lamp. Men sat in a circle, their faces etched with weariness and suspicion. These were the remnants of our brotherhood, those who had survived the slaughter in Spain and escaped here, to the heart of a kingdom that watched us with equal suspicion. 

Brother Kamal rose from his seat. His dark eyes scanned the room, lingering on each of us as if searching for cracks in our armor. “Brothers,” he began, his voice low and steady, “we meet tonight not to mourn but to strengthen what remains. The enemy has stolen from us before, but never again.”

A murmur of agreement swept through the room. My hands clenched into fists at the memory of the betrayal. Her name was Safiya. She had been one of us—a kind soul, always quick to comfort and eager to contribute. She had trusted the wrong man.

He had come into her life like a savior, offering her protection from the Inquisition, food when she was starving, and promises of a life free from fear. He wore no cross, bore no emblem of the Church; instead, he spoke softly and listened. Slowly, he won her heart, and with it, her secrets. She told him of our lodges, our codes, the signs we used to recognize one another. 

And he had used it all against us.

One by one, our brothers and sisters were taken. The Inquisition stormed our meetings, dragging us into the streets, chaining us, branding us as heretics. Some were burned, others enslaved. Safiya’s cries echoed in my mind—pleas for forgiveness, for understanding, for mercy. She had been a victim as much as the rest of us, but the damage was done.

“From this day forward,” Kamal continued, his voice cutting through my thoughts, “we trust no one beyond this circle. Our lodges are now for men only. Women are to be protected but excluded from our meetings. And no new members shall be admitted without unanimous agreement and proof of loyalty.”

“It is harsh, but necessary,” another brother said. “We cannot afford to bleed again.”

I nodded, though the decision left a bitter taste in my mouth. Our strength had always been in our unity, but now fear had divided us. Women like Safiya, once our equals, were now seen as liabilities. The betrayal had carved a chasm that seemed impossible to bridge.

“The lodge will continue to meet, but we will change our methods,” Kamal said. “No fixed locations. Signals will come through intermediaries, and no lodge shall know the full network. We are masons now, not just in name but in practice. Each stone we lay must strengthen the foundation, not weaken it.”

As the meeting drew to a close, I lingered, watching the others leave in pairs or alone, each disappearing into the fog with the practiced subtlety of hunted men. Kamal approached me, his expression heavy with the weight of leadership.

“Tariq,” he said, placing a hand on my shoulder. “I know you were close to Safiya.”

I stiffened but said nothing. 

“She made her choice,” he continued. “But we cannot let her mistake define us. We must rebuild, stronger and more vigilant than before.”

“And if love makes fools of us again?” I asked bitterly.

Kamal’s face darkened. “Then it will be our undoing.”

I left the lodge and stepped back into the cold night. The streets of Greenwich were silent, but I felt the weight of eyes watching from the shadows. The Inquisition was relentless, and now they knew our ways. 

But we had adapted. We had survived. We would continue to build—not temples of stone but sanctuaries of trust, invisible to all but the faithful. 

As I walked, I touched the engraved ring on my finger, a symbol of unity now shadowed by suspicion. We were no longer free, no longer whole. But we were still here, and as long as we breathed, we would fight for the day when the Grand Architect’s light would shine on us once more.

#  The Inquisitor's

The task set before us was no ordinary mission. We had our orders, delivered with the unwavering authority of the papal seal, though there were whispers that the command came from something even greater, an entity cloaked in myth and fear. The instructions were explicit: root out and dismantle every trace of the Moors who had once thrived among us, not merely by purging the flesh, but by erasing their very existence from the annals of Europe.

I, Diego Velasquez, a loyal servant of the Inquisition, was but one of many tasked with hunting down those who dared to remain. The Berber surrender in Spain had been the beginning, a crack in the dam, and now the floodwaters surged forth. Once proud noblemen and women with sable skin, distinct and dignified, were suddenly reduced to shadows, their lineage and histories blotted from our collective memory.

The hunt was methodical. Each of us, inquisitors trained in the art of purging heresy, were now faced with the task of erasing an entire people. From France to England, the Moors—recognizable by their dark skin, their proud customs, their songs that carried secrets of a homeland far away—were easy to identify, even easier to isolate. Those of wealth or noble standing could hide only so long, for even among the aristocracy, eyes were always watching, whispers trailing like smoke from fires lit for reasons more sinister than warmth.

And how could they hide? Our reach was endless, our purpose relentless. Over three hundred thousand souls in Spain were either cast into the jaws of death or expelled. Portugal, France, and even the English and Scots had all taken up the same grim work. Ships sailed with death rates that chilled even the hardest among us, vessels crammed with men, women, and children, sent to distant shores. Yet for every ship that arrived, another was lost, devouring hundreds, perhaps thousands, in one brutal voyage. I would watch them set sail, their faces resolute, some defiant. Many never returned.

Our libraries burned, the ink on their pages feeding flames that devoured records of art, culture, and language. Paintings were scrubbed of their Moorish subjects, their rich faces replaced with pale visages, their achievements credited to others. We changed their names, altered their family histories, and removed the markers from their graves so that future generations would walk over them, unaware of the lives that had been there before. It was said in hushed tones that even in Africa itself, their numbers dwindled against our sheer numbers, outmatched four to one by Europeans. The reach of our order had become a shadow over an entire continent.

Language, music, stories, all were silenced. The songs that spoke of desert sands and the emerald shores of distant lands were outlawed, while families—those who survived—were fractured, unable to keep even the names that had bound them to their lineage. Each command from Rome, from the elusive entity behind the mask of the Pope, made it clear: none were to remember. We were to erase not only their presence but also their memory.

With every mission, a small voice grew within me. I was an instrument in the hands of forces far greater than I understood, but I could feel that this was not the work of righteousness—it was something else, something darker, perhaps even damnable. But what choice did I have? To disobey would mean to face the same fate as the very people I hunted.

The ground beneath our feet was growing slick with blood and shadow, the weight of erasure pressing on our souls as Europe’s landscape changed irrevocably. We, the inquisitors, had been commanded not merely to purge but to reshape the world, to mold it into something devoid of the colors, the songs, and the histories that had once made it whole. In our zeal to root out the Moors, we had set our own souls aflame, turning ourselves into ghosts, shadows whose memory—unlike those we hunted—would endure only in whispers, as warnings in the night.

The task set before us was no ordinary mission. We had our orders, delivered with the unwavering authority of the papal seal, though there were whispers that the command came from something even greater, an entity cloaked in myth and fear. The instructions were explicit: root out and dismantle every trace of the Moors who had once thrived among us, not merely by purging the flesh, but by erasing their very existence from the annals of Europe.

I, Diego Velasquez, a loyal servant of the Inquisition, was but one of many tasked with hunting down those who dared to remain. The Berber surrender in Spain had been the beginning, a crack in the dam, and now the floodwaters surged forth. Once proud noblemen and women with sable skin, distinct and dignified, were suddenly reduced to shadows, their lineage and histories blotted from our collective memory.

The hunt was methodical. Each of us, inquisitors trained in the art of purging heresy, were now faced with the task of erasing an entire people. From France to England, the Moors—recognizable by their dark skin, their proud customs, their songs that carried secrets of a homeland far away—were easy to identify, even easier to isolate. Those of wealth or noble standing could hide only so long, for even among the aristocracy, eyes were always watching, whispers trailing like smoke from fires lit for reasons more sinister than warmth.

And how could they hide? Our reach was endless, our purpose relentless. Over three hundred thousand souls in Spain were either cast into the jaws of death or expelled. Portugal, France, and even the English and Scots had all taken up the same grim work. Ships sailed with death rates that chilled even the hardest among us, vessels crammed with men, women, and children, sent to distant shores. Yet for every ship that arrived, another was lost, devouring hundreds, perhaps thousands, in one brutal voyage. I would watch them set sail, their faces resolute, some defiant. Many never returned.

Our libraries burned, the ink on their pages feeding flames that devoured records of art, culture, and language. Paintings were scrubbed of their Moorish subjects, their rich faces replaced with pale visages, their achievements credited to others. We changed their names, altered their family histories, and removed the markers from their graves so that future generations would walk over them, unaware of the lives that had been there before. It was said in hushed tones that even in Africa itself, their numbers dwindled against our sheer numbers, outmatched four to one by Europeans. The reach of our order had become a shadow over an entire continent.

Language, music, stories, all were silenced. The songs that spoke of desert sands and the emerald shores of distant lands were outlawed, while families—those who survived—were fractured, unable to keep even the names that had bound them to their lineage. Each command from Rome, from the elusive entity behind the mask of the Pope, made it clear: none were to remember. We were to erase not only their presence but also their memory.

With every mission, a small voice grew within me. I was an instrument in the hands of forces far greater than I understood, but I could feel that this was not the work of righteousness—it was something else, something darker, perhaps even damnable. But what choice did I have? To disobey would mean to face the same fate as the very people I hunted.

The ground beneath our feet was growing slick with blood and shadow, the weight of erasure pressing on our souls as Europe’s landscape changed irrevocably. We, the inquisitors, had been commanded not merely to purge but to reshape the world, to mold it into something devoid of the colors, the songs, and the histories that had once made it whole. In our zeal to root out the Moors, we had set our own souls aflame, turning ourselves into ghosts, shadows whose memory—unlike those we hunted—would endure only in whispers, as warnings in the night.

# The Inquisitor's Shadow: Europe's Hidden Holocaust

The Forgotten Sins: A Tale of Chaos.

I am Brother Tomás de Torquemada—or what’s left of him. The God of Chaos wears my face now, twisting my once-proud visage into a grotesque mask of regret and despair. My soul is bound to it, trapped in a purgatory where my memories become its weapon.

"Tell them," it commands, its voice a cacophony of whispers and screams. "Show them what you did. Let them taste the madness you unleashed."

I resist, but my lips betray me. My story spills out, drenched in blood and ash. 

The Year 1492\.

Granada fell that year, its ancient walls crumbling under the weight of Catholic armies. The Moors surrendered their last stronghold, and I felt a wicked satisfaction as the edict flowed from my quill:

"The infection of Moorish blood must be purged from Christian lands."

The ink was barely dry when the God of Chaos whispered its first mocking laugh into my ears. 

"You thought this was the end?" It was teased. "Fool. This is where the nightmare begins."

And it was right.

# A World Set Ablaze  

"You never imagined it would spread so far," the God of Chaos sneers, dragging me through the corridors of history. Each step plunges me deeper into the nightmare I helped create.

France burns. Portugal seethes. England, though estranged from Rome, eagerly takes up the banner of purity. The Inquisition becomes a fever, consuming nations, devouring lives. 

By 1500, the death ships—oh, those cursed vessels—begin their voyages. Entire families, bundled like cargo, are "returned" to North Africa, a land that many had never seen. My voice trembles as I recount the horror. 

“Forty percent didn’t survive the journey,” I whisper. "The Scottish ships... they packed them tighter than the galleys of slaves. We didn’t care—they were ‘infected,’ after all."

The God of Chaos laughs, its mirth shaking the foundations of my torment. "Tell them about the numbers, Brother Torquemada. Don’t be shy."

# The Grim Tally

Spain: 300,000 expelled or slaughtered.    
Portugal: 250,000 "purified."    
France: 180,000 erased.    
England and Scotland: 90,000 hunted and "cleansed."    
Italy: 150,000 vanished.  

But the numbers are lies, like everything else. The true toll is whispered in the shadows. Entire villages disappeared overnight, their inhabitants scrubbed from history. 

"Did you think they’d stop at the Moors?" Chaos mocks. "You gave them the blueprint for annihilation."

# North Africa: A Land in Turmoil  

And what of those "returned" to North Africa? They arrived as strangers in their supposed homeland—mixed in blood, language, and culture. Their lighter skin marked them as imposters, their European features made them targets of suspicion. 

"Some tried to settle," I murmured. "Others fled deeper into uninhabited lands, searching for places untouched by hatred."  

"They thought they could run," Chaos chuckles. "But where can you run when the world hunts you?"

The displaced Moors fractured. Infighting erupted. The wounds we inflicted on their identity festered, turning them into wanderers in a land that once claimed them.

The Purge Perfected.

"Tell them about 'science,'" the God of Chaos hisses.

"Entire libraries burned to ash," I say, the words choking me. "Graves desecrated. Family trees rewritten. Paintings defaced. Languages outlawed. Songs silenced."  

By 1600, Europe had become a graveyard for memory. The Moors weren’t just expelled or killed—they were erased, as though they had never existed.

# The Conversations of the Damned 

#  

The God of Chaos stops me, its eyes glinting with dark amusement. "Do you know what the greatest joke is, Brother Torquemada?"

"I was the joke," I reply, my voice hollow. "I thought I was serving God."

"And you were," it retorts, laughing. "Just not the one you thought."

I look at the ruin I’ve wrought, my knees buckling under the weight of my sin. “Why show me this? Why make me relive it?”

"So you understand," it whispers, leaning close. "You didn’t just destroy a people—you destroyed the truth. And the truth, Brother, is the only thing more dangerous than chaos."

# The Only Way Out  

There is no redemption for me, no salvation. But as the God of Chaos drags me further into the abyss, I see the flicker of a single path—a way out, not for me, but for others. 

"Run," I whisper to the Moors trapped in my nightmares. "Run, because they won’t stop. They will come for your histories, your memories, your very souls."

The God of Chaos laughs again, its voice echoing like thunder. "Do you think they’ll make it? Do you think anyone can outrun the past?"

I don’t answer. I don’t need to. All I can do is hope they run fast enough to escape the shadow I cast.

# The Forgotten Holocaust  

"By the time they’re done," Chaos muses, "the world will believe Europe was always white, always pure. Your legacy, Brother Torquemada, will be a lie so perfect, no one will question it."

The last thing I see is a world built on forgetting, its foundations cracked and bleeding. And in those cracks, I see the truth trying to claw its way back.

"Run," I whisper one last time, though I know no one is listening.

The God of Chaos smiles, and the screams of centuries follow me into darkness.

Here is a dark and dramatic first-person story inspired by the grim legacy of the Atlantic slave trade. The narrative weaves danger, drama, and invisible hierarchies, exploring the layered horrors of age, gender, and defiance.

\---

# The Abyss of Chains

The stench of humanity pressed too close still clings to my nostrils, a ghost of a memory I cannot cleanse. My name is Kwame—though they call me another name here, a mockery of identity bestowed by the traders. Beneath the oppressive creak of the ship’s timbers, I cling to my name as if it is a talisman, a tiny spark of self in this abyss where even the soul is stripped bare.

“Kwame, do not lose the fire in your eyes,” whispers Jaha, the man shackled to me. His voice is a rasp, low enough to evade the ears of the sailor who looms like a shadow against the lantern light. I nod, though my body aches from days without room to stretch. His words are a spark of defiance, one I cling to in this relentless tide of despair.

Around me, the darkness is alive—whimpers of children, quiet sobs of women, the guttural groans of men who long to rebel but are held still by iron and terror. Each of us is caught in an invisible web of suffering, but the hierarchy is clear. 

“Do not let them break you, Kwame,” Jaha whispers again. His grip on the rusted chain between us tightens. We both know he will not survive another week.

\---

Hours later—though time aboard this floating hell is a blur—the commotion begins. A young boy, perhaps no older than eight, is dragged up to the deck by his collar. His cries pierce the air, a sharp counterpoint to the rhythm of the waves. They are disciplining him for spilling water on a sailor's boots. 

“He's just a child\!” A woman’s voice rings out from the women’s hold. The cry is a knife through the silence, drawing a gasp from the crew.

“You dare speak?” snarls one of the overseers. He marches below deck, his boots pounding the fragile planks that separate us from the void of the sea. When he reaches her, there is a sickening crack—flesh meeting flesh. Yet, even as she falls silent, her defiance lingers like smoke in the fetid air.

\---

In the suffocating gloom, Jaha speaks again. “We cannot wait for freedom to come. We must take it.”

“How?” My voice trembles. I want to believe him, but I have seen what they do to rebels. The body of one still hangs from the mast, swinging like a grim warning.

“The child,” Jaha murmurs. “He is unshackled. He can move where we cannot.”

That night, beneath the watchful eyes of the overseers, the boy crawls between the slats to retrieve a shard of glass. He places it into Jaha’s hand—our first weapon.

\---

The rebellion unfolds like a tempest. Jaha is the first to strike, using the shard to sever the ropes of a sleeping sailor. He shouts for us to rise, but the crew awakens, their guns flashing in the dim light. Chaos erupts—chains clatter, bodies surge. I see the woman who cried out earlier leap onto a sailor, clawing at his eyes with feral intensity.

I manage to grab a loose nail from the deck, plunging it into the side of a sailor as he raises a whip against a child. Blood sprays my face, and I feel a twisted surge of power—a fleeting moment of retribution.

We are outnumbered. One by one, the rebels are subdued. Jaha falls beside me, his lifeless eyes staring into mine as if begging me to continue. The woman is dragged back below deck, her screams fading into the cacophony of defeat.

\---

The next day, the captain stands before us, his face a mask of cold fury. “This,” he says, gesturing to Jaha’s lifeless body, “is what happens to those who forget their place.”

He orders the remaining rebels to be lashed—myself included. Each strike of the whip tears at my flesh, but I refuse to scream. My silence becomes my final act of defiance.

\---

Weeks later, we arrive in the New World. I step off the ship with a broken body but an unbroken spirit. The echoes of the rebellion linger in my mind, a haunting refrain of courage in the face of despair. Though the chains remain, I carry Jaha’s words with me: \*Do not let them break you.\*

Even in bondage, I know that the fire of resistance cannot be extinguished. It will burn through generations, fueled by whispers, songs, and stories that refuse to die.

# 

# Masonic and Moor love

The Inquisition’s shadow loomed large over the coastlines and across our fleets, a ravenous force spreading with the tenacity of wildfire, burning everything it touched. I had always thought their mission would serve to protect the faith, a necessary evil perhaps, but as I witnessed the way fear and division had taken root, I knew it was my responsibility to hunt the hunters themselves. Under the guise of a pious crusade, the Inquisition had infiltrated my ranks, poisoning my army and corrupting my mission. No longer was it enough to watch from afar—I would hunt them myself, moving like a shadow within their own circles, gathering allies from secret Masonic lodges, plotting from the hidden spaces between walls. 

Yet within these clandestine halls, other webs were being spun. Secret liaisons formed, fragile connections that, if discovered, would be torn apart. One such affair was that of Zara, a Moorish woman of striking beauty and cunning intelligence, and her lover, Tomas, a Masonic master. Their bond, forbidden and dangerous, was one born of whispers and moonlight, stolen moments beneath shadowed arches where no one dared linger for long. In hushed tones, they’d speak of love and the dreams they harbored for a world beyond the Inquisition’s iron grip.

One evening, Zara leaned into Tomas, her head resting against his chest as they lay hidden beneath the hanging vines near the docks. 

“Do you ever imagine it, Tomas?” she whispered, her voice soft but filled with an unguarded hope. “A life free from this constant fear, where I can walk beside you, my hand in yours, in the light of day.”

Tomas ran his fingers through her dark curls, his expression tender, though a shadow of worry darkened his eyes. “I imagine it every day, my love,” he murmured. “But the world we want...it’s far from the one we have. I fear for you every time you step into that city, knowing the Inquisition’s eyes are everywhere.”

Zara lifted her head, her gaze sharp and determined. “We cannot live in fear forever, Tomas. I would rather risk everything than live as a shadow in my own life. Don’t you want the same?”

Before Tomas could answer, footsteps echoed nearby. They froze, breaths held. A figure emerged from the shadows—a Moorish man, a friend of Zara’s who had seen her through many trials, his face a mask of shock and betrayal. “Zara… what is this?”

She pulled away from Tomas, her heart pounding, words failing her as the man’s eyes darted between them. “You would betray us, Zara?” he hissed, voice low but trembling with anger. “Do you know what they’ll do when they find out you’ve consorted with… him?” He spat the word like a curse.

“No, please, listen,” Zara began, stepping forward, her hands outstretched. But the man shook his head and backed away.

“They’ll hear of this. You may think yourself untouchable, but you’re not.” He shot Tomas a look of disgust before retreating into the shadows. Zara and Tomas were left alone, the moment shattered.

The next day, Zara’s friend was nowhere to be found. People whispered that he had been “taken”—though by whom, no one could say. She felt the weight of the betrayal, the terrible knowledge that her love had cost her someone she’d trusted.

Meanwhile, Tomas faced his own fears. His Masonic brothers sensed his distraction and questioned him. “You’re risking everything for her,” one of his confidants warned. “The lodge won’t turn a blind eye if this threatens our mission.”

Days turned into weeks, and Zara and Tomas’s meetings grew even more furtive, their love complicated by newfound mistrust. Yet their passion only deepened, as if each kiss and embrace might be the last. Tomas brought word from his lodge—orders to move covertly and increase efforts in gathering souls for their agenda in the New World, an order that troubled him but one he could not refuse.

Zara, meanwhile, found herself caught in a world where the loyalty of her people could no longer be trusted. Rumors circulated of Moors who had turned to the Inquisition, offering up secrets about their own kin in exchange for promises of protection or reward. Some had betrayed family, friends, and lovers alike, watching as the Inquisition dismantled entire communities, forcing conversions, and sending many into slavery.

One evening, as she stood with Tomas by the harbor, watching the ships set sail, he asked her, “Would you leave with me, Zara? Away from this place, this life?”

A tear slipped down her cheek as she gazed into the horizon. “If I could, Tomas. But my people… we are being torn apart. Betrayed from within. How could I abandon them now?”

But their love was not without its moments of escape. In secret, they traveled to Gorée, where Zara adopted the life of a \*signare\*, a powerful figure in a complex society where African women married European men, blending power, influence, and resilience to build lives that survived in liminality. When Tomas would leave, she would gather sand from the place where he last stood, her own quiet ritual of remembrance, awaiting his return. And though she knew he would face the dangers of the Masonic lodges and the Inquisition, Zara clung to hope, a hope as fragile as the handful of sand she held.

The years passed, their lives a constant dance between love and peril. Tomas, returning to Europe, would carry Zara’s letters and tokens, reminders of a love that had defied the most insurmountable odds.

# Masonic birth

I sat in the stillness of the dim room, the air thick with anticipation. A single candle burned before me, casting flickering shadows across the walls. I could feel the weight of my plan settling in my bones, the enormity of what I was about to attempt. That night by the harbor had changed everything. Since then, I’d been haunted by visions and whispers—a force so relentless that I could scarcely sleep. It was a mad revelation, a forbidden dream that I couldn’t shake, and now, it had taken root in my mind.

The entity had shown me the truth—how the Inquisition was more than a religious crusade; it was a machine of power, a way for the Church and the nobility to tighten their grip on the world. But the Church's ambitions were limited; they thought only in terms of control and fear. What if I, Tomas, could turn that same machine to serve me and a select few? What if, beneath the nose of the Inquisition itself, I could create an empire built not on faith but on loyalty, secrecy, and ambition? My mind had been burning with this question, and now, I had my answer.

This was how it would begin. Tonight, in this dim room, I would plant the seed of a brotherhood that would outlast kings and priests alike. I arranged for a small gathering of men I trusted implicitly—merchants, minor noblemen, men who were disillusioned with the Church’s stranglehold. They were seekers, willing to risk everything for a chance at true power, but they didn’t yet know the scale of my vision.

As I waited, I rehearsed the plan over and over in my mind. Each man would be handpicked, each sworn to secrecy through oaths and symbols. There would be rituals to create a sense of mystique, handshakes known only to us, codes to protect our meetings. I had to ensure that none of this could be traced back to me—not yet. The Inquisition would slaughter us if they knew what we were building.

The door creaked open, and Alvaro stepped in, his eyes scanning the darkened room. He was followed by four others, each one careful, wary. They trusted me, but I knew there was a thin line between trust and doubt. One wrong word, one hint of betrayal, and this would all crumble before it began.

“Close the door,” I said quietly, motioning them inside. Once we were all enclosed, I stood, letting the silence settle. They looked at me expectantly, their faces illuminated by the soft glow of the candle.

“Thank you for coming,” I began, my voice steady but laced with intensity. “Tonight marks the beginning of something… extraordinary. You are here because you seek power, freedom from the Church’s tyranny, and the means to rise above what the nobility and the priests have allowed you.”

One of them, a tall man named Raul, cleared his throat. “Tomas, what exactly are you proposing? We’re risking much by even being here. The Church—”

“Yes,” I interrupted, “the Church holds power, wealth, and influence. They have used the Inquisition to fuel that power, capturing Moors, Africans, anyone who doesn’t fit their vision. But I see a greater opportunity within their twisted ambition. They hunt for followers. We… we will hunt for wealth, and we will do so as a brotherhood, outside the reach of any Church or king.”

The men looked at one another, a mix of unease and intrigue in their eyes. I could feel their hesitation—this was new, radical, dangerous. But the entity’s voice still echoed in my mind, and I drew strength from it.

“We will be more than just a gathering of men. We will be Masons—builders of a hidden empire. But our structure will be unlike anything the world has known. Each of you will swear an oath tonight, binding you to secrecy and loyalty. Our meetings will be held in silence, shrouded in ritual and code. No outsider will ever know what passes between these walls.”

Alvaro leaned forward, his face thoughtful, curious. “And what exactly is the nature of this ‘brotherhood,’ Tomas? What are we to gain from this risk?”

I met his gaze, feeling the energy shift. “We will gain freedom from the constraints of the Church, freedom from the nobles who see us as nothing but assets to use and discard. We will gain control of commerce, of the labor that builds empires. The Inquisition gathers slaves, yes, but they use them for control, for conversion. We will use them to build wealth beyond imagining. Our hands will be on the pulse of the new world, and the world will beat at our command.”

A murmur passed through the room. I could see the hunger in their eyes, the spark of ambition that no sermons or royal decrees could satisfy.

“To protect ourselves, we will adopt symbols, handshakes, gestures known only to us. Our gatherings will be called lodges, and each man here will have a role, a purpose, in building this hidden network. You will learn the signs, the codes, and each meeting will be veiled in ritual, not just to protect us but to instill loyalty and trust.”

Raul spoke up again, this time with a glint of something dark in his eye. “And how do we ensure that none betray us?”

I smiled. “By making the price of betrayal absolute. Each man here will swear on his life, his family, and his honor to protect this brotherhood. Should any man reveal our secrets, he will be removed from this world by his own brothers. We are bound by blood, and none will break this bond.”

I extended my hand, the first symbol of our brotherhood. Alvaro reached out, pressing his hand to mine, his eyes locked onto mine in a silent oath. One by one, each man followed, completing the gesture, a promise unspoken but understood. The room felt charged, alive with an energy that no prayer, no priest, could conjure.

As the last hand pressed to mine, I felt the presence again, that strange, otherworldly force that had whispered to me by the harbor. It surged through me like a pulse, a vision of ships setting sail, of gold and riches beyond imagining, of influence that spanned continents. I felt a thrill, a taste of the power that would come.

When all had sworn the oath, I spoke one final time. “Tonight, we are no longer men bound by the Church or the nobility. We are Masons, builders of a new order. Our mission is clear: wealth, power, freedom beyond their reach. And so it begins.”

The men left in silence, slipping into the night, each one marked by our new alliance. As the last of them disappeared into the shadows, I lingered, feeling both exhilarated and unsettled. This brotherhood was born of secrets, but it would grow beyond even my control. I knew I was playing a dangerous game, one that would change the course of my life and, perhaps, the world itself.

And as for Zara… I would keep this from her, shield her from the darkness I was building. She knew nothing of what I had just created, of the power that would soon be mine. Yet as I thought of her, I felt a pang of regret. She would never understand the price I was willing to pay for power, nor would I let her see what I had become.

But the path was set.

# Era  of freemason 

As I, Tomas, stood in the flickering candlelight of that small, dimly lit room, the weight of what I was about to propose gnawed at me. The men who gathered around me, noblemen, merchants, traders—all shared my distrust of the Inquisition’s true motives. They looked eager, even hungry, for a path beyond the Church, beyond royalty’s stranglehold. These men wanted freedom and power, and so did I, but my vision was sharper, more daring. I’d recently had… an awakening, a vision, or perhaps it was some entity whispering from beyond—a revelation so profound it was still shaping itself in my mind, yet I knew it would change everything.

The entity had come to me one night in what felt like a fevered dream. I saw ships upon ships, carrying our people, Moors and others, chained and bound, deported by the thousands, ripped from their homes, sold into slavery. I saw that the Inquisition’s brutal hunt was nothing more than a vast machine fueling the ambitions of the Church, nobility, and the crown. But this vision, this voice, showed me a twisted way to take power from that machine. The Church wanted followers, yes, but the world wanted labor, wealth—control. The nobility wanted entertainment, someone to serve their every need, even in their private chambers. But this machine could be turned, made to serve not them, but us.

I could feel Zara’s absence like a weight in my heart. I’d sent her to the New World with a mission of her own, building her Signare empire, becoming what she’d always dreamed—a woman of strength and power in her own right. I’d told her nothing of the vision, nor of my plan. How could I? This was not something I could share, not even with her, not even with the one I loved. I knew Zara’s heart was with her people, and my plans were… Well, let’s just say they were not in the best interest of the Moors.

The room fell silent as I began to speak, my voice low, deliberate, my eyes scanning each man’s face, one by one. “Gentlemen,” I began, “we are gathered here tonight not as rivals, not as noblemen, not as traders, but as brothers.” I paused, letting the weight of that word sink in. Brotherhood was more than friendship; it was loyalty, secrecy—a bond beyond blood.

“We live in a time of fear,” I continued. “The Inquisition hunts the Moors, the Church devours wealth, the crown takes all they can from our hands, and we are left… waiting. But I am here to propose something different, something bold.” I let a thin smile creep over my face as their eyes widened, caught between fear and intrigue.

“The Inquisition is gathering Moors, Africans, and the outcast of the faith. But they do not see the opportunity we see. While they hunt in the name of religion, we will hunt in the name of commerce, of fortune, of a future beyond their grip. We shall not take them to convert them—we shall take them to work for us, to build our wealth.”

One of the men, Alvaro, a portly merchant with a shrewd mind, leaned forward. “But Tomas, how can this be kept hidden? The Inquisition’s eyes are everywhere.”

“That, Alvaro, is precisely why secrecy is our greatest weapon. Word of mouth only, secret handshakes, symbols worn only by those sworn to this brotherhood,” I replied. “We will operate under the nose of the Church itself. This will be our strength. They’ll be too busy hunting heretics to see the empire we are quietly building beneath their feet.”

I watched the flicker of greed spark in their eyes. “From this day forward, we are more than friends,” I continued. “We are brothers. To enter this brotherhood, you must swear an oath—to keep our mission hidden, to seek riches, not for kings or priests, but for ourselves. And, above all, no women, no Moors, no outsiders can ever join us. What we are building… it is for us alone.”

I felt a pang at those words, thinking of Zara. If she knew what I was doing, she would despise me, yet I told myself it was to protect her. Zara would never understand my vision, nor would she understand the power that these slaves would bring us. It was ironic—I loved her deeply, yet this plan required me to turn away from her people, to treat them as mere assets in a grand design.

“Now,” I said, lowering my voice, “are you with me? Will you swear this oath?”

The men murmured their assent, one by one, each swearing under his breath. I led them through a ritual I’d devised, a simple handshake, a brief symbol drawn into the palm, a private signal known only to us. And as each man stood before me, completing the gesture, I felt the weight of history bearing down on us. What we were creating was no mere alliance; it was the seed of something that would one day challenge the Church itself. 

The meeting adjourned, I lingered in the empty room, wondering what the future would bring. Outside, the ships had begun to sail, fleets commissioned in part by the Church, but some by us. We would use the slaves not as mere labor but as part of a vast network of commerce, trade, and power, building a hidden empire that the Inquisition would never see coming.

And as for Zara… I’d keep her far from it. She was too precious, too principled. If she knew, she’d never forgive me. She’d see the cruelty in it, the callous way we were using her people. I’d told myself it was for our future, our legacy. But deep down, a small voice reminded me that I was no longer the man she loved, no longer the Tomas she knew. This Tomas… he was something darker, something born of ambition and desire for power beyond even kings.

But I shook off the thought and returned to my plans. The first lodge was complete; the brotherhood had begun. And soon, the world would change—though none would know that the seeds of revolution were planted here, in the quiet darkness of a hidden room, with nothing but the glimmer of candlelight and the whispered oaths of men bound by a dangerous vision.

# Merchant and Ruler empire of empires

The tale I share with you begins with two perspectives—those of a merchant and a ruler—merged within one singular being. I was an entity neither fully living nor simply an abstract construct, an organism that straddled two worlds: that of conquest and commerce. My name was synonymous with power and influence; I was the Merchant-Ruler, a leader, the company, who, by the grace of dominion and trade, commanded vast tracts of distant lands. I reveled in the balance of scales and the weight of golden coins, yet my hands were as calloused with the strain of command as they were ink-stained from ledgers of wealth.  
From the bustling ports of Europe to the fertile plains of Asia, my reach was extensive. But it was not benevolence that anchored my reign—it was survival. I am destine to become the empire of empires, meticulously crafted to flourish. My very existence hinges on a relentless appetite for sustenance—wealth to fuel my ambitions, labor to drive my relentless progress, and unwavering allegiance to maintain my iron grip. In this intricate dance of survival, every cog and sinew plays its part, feeding the insatiable beast that thrives on the lifeblood of its creators. Revenue collectors were dispatched like wolves into the lands, trained not to pluck but to tear and extract with ruthless efficiency. My edicts were clear: return with coffers heavy or pay the price with your blood. My policies, often condemned as cruel, were the lifeblood of my ascent to power. Each decision, no matter how harsh, was a calculated step in my relentless pursuit of dominance. I thrived on the sacrifices made, knowing that without them, my growth would falter. In the crucible of power, mercy was a luxury I could not afford.

Yet in East and Southeast Asia there were consequences. It feels as though the words claw their way from my throat, each one a reckoning. Famine swept through the lands under my dominion when the rains failed, a silent specter that destroyed the lives of millions. People shrank to shadows of themselves, reduced to subsistence and desperation. Whispers spoke of betrayal, of a ruler more consumed with balancing profits than lifting the burden from the starving. And they were right. For me, the ledger did not record the suffering of the masses; it tallied revenues and expenditures. The survival of the entity dependent on sacrifice—not mine, but theirs. They were cogs, human components in a machine that, even now, history seeks to explain but cannot excuse. The entity did not know empathy, only the rhythm of its grinding, relentless heartbeat. I saw them, heard the chains rattle and the silent pleas behind dark, unblinking eyes, yet I looked away. To acknowledge their humanity would have threatened my very survival.

The consequences of my choices were not whispers in some far-off court; they thundered like cannon fire, resounding through the lands I claimed as my dominion. And yes, I called it mine, though what a lie that was. The land, the people, the very air—they never truly belonged to me. And yet, there I was, seated before a ledger, scratching numbers with a pen as though I could make the rains return through arithmetic. Revenue. Expenditure. Surplus. Deficit, land. The numbers told no tales of suffering, no stories of starving children or dying crops. They spoke only of survival—my survival. Ironic, isn't it?

“Your Majesty,” my advisor said with a half-hearted bow, “if we open the granaries, the coffers will empty within the year.”    
“And if we don’t?” I replied, my tone colder than I intended.    
He hesitated. I could see the war raging behind his eyes, a battle of morality versus pragmatism.    
“Then they will starve, sire.”    
“They’re starving now,” I said flatly, and I returned to my ledger.    
Oh, how quick the tongue of a king can be, and how sharp its blade.

\*\*\*

But it didn’t end there. The famine was not the only crime of my rule; no, it was merely one chapter in a much darker story. Across the oceans, where the sun seemed angrier, the air heavier, there were others—millions of others—who bore the brunt of my ambitions. Enslaved men, women and children, torn from the shores of Africa, chained and broken for the sake of a world I claimed to lead. The new world.

Let me be honest. I saw them. I heard their chains clatter, like metallic whispers in the wind. I caught their gazes—eyes dark and hollow, yet so piercing that they seemed to slice through my very soul. Did I flinch? No. I looked away. And here’s the cruelest irony of it all: I convinced myself that looking away was strength, that acknowledging their suffering would somehow unravel the great machine I had sworn to keep running.  The enslaved who toiled beneath the brutal sun, laboring in chains for the glory of faraway thrones.

“You’ve become a god of numbers,” someone once spat at me. I can’t remember their name, only their venom. “But your empire bleeds, and you’ve no hands to stanch the wound.”    
I laughed bitterly. “Empires don’t bleed,” I said, waving them off with a practiced flick of the wrist. “Empires grind. And if they stop grinding, we all fall.”

But did I believe that? Truly? Or was it just an excuse—a coward’s justification wrapped in regal silk?

Even now, history seeks to understand the entity, that relentless beast of profit and power.   
It has no answer only silence, and silence, it seems, is all I deserve.

# The Chains of Paradise Lost

I am the last voice of Granada, watching as Queen Isabella's armies mass outside our walls in 1492\. The God of Chaos has awakened now, and it feeds on our despair.

"Tell them what happens next," it whispers to me. "Tell them how paradise dies."

I was there when the last Sultan of Granada, Muhammad XII, handed over the keys to our city. His mother's words still echo through time: "Weep like a woman for what you could not defend as a man." But she didn't know – none of us knew – that this was only the beginning of our sorrows.

"Watch carefully," the God of Chaos purrs. "See how one empire's fall feeds another's rise."

The Spanish Inquisition moves through our streets like a plague. They burn our books first – centuries of knowledge turning to ash. Then they burn our people. The chaos spreads as the Portuguese join the hunt, and suddenly we're not just heretics – we're commodities.

I shift my gaze to the west coast of Africa, where I see our own sins coming full circle. The Berber raids that once struck south into West Africa had weakened the coastal kingdoms, making them vulnerable. Now Portuguese ships slip into those same ports, and the God of Chaos shows me the terrible symmetry.

"You see it now, don't you?" the entity asks. "How do the hunters become the hunted?"

I watch as the Limba people of Sierra Leone, already devastated by centuries of Berber slave raids, face a new and more terrible trade. The Portuguese don't just want slaves – they want gold, ivory, and souls. They build their fortress at Elmina, and the God of Chaos dances in the surf as the first ships depart.

"We did this," I whisper, watching Berber refugees flee south only to find themselves caught in the same nets they once cast. "When we raided the south, we opened the door."

The entity shows me the scattered people of al-Andalus – some fleeing to Morocco, others trying to hide their faith and culture in plain sight as Moriscos. But the Inquisition has a long reach and a longer memory.

"In 1501," I narrate, my voice thick with grief, "they gave us a choice that was no choice at all: convert or leave. By 1526, even practicing our old customs in secret was enough to condemn us. They called it 'crypto-Islam' and used it to justify hunting us across two continents."

I see the ships now – Spanish and Portuguese caravels cutting through waves toward Africa. They carry Inquisitors hungry for heretics and traders hungry for slaves. The God of Chaos shows me how they use our own ancient trade routes, our own maps, our own knowledge against both us and the peoples of West Africa.

"The Limba tribes," I continue, watching their villages burn, "paid twice for the sins of the empire. First weakened by our raids, then shattered by European slavers who followed our paths inland. The Portuguese didn't need to create new slave routes – we had already mapped them all."

The entity swirls around me as I witness the transformation of the African coast. Where Berber traders once exchanged salt for gold, European forts now rise like teeth in the mouth of a beast. The God of Chaos shows me the ledgers – how many taken, how many died, how many converted at sword-point.

"Your people mapped Africa's heart," it reminds me. "You showed them the way."

I see the Moriscos making their final exodus from Spain in 1609, carrying nothing but memories of paradise. Some find refuge in North Africa, others scatter across the Ottoman Empire. But the world has changed. The routes south are closed now, blocked by European ships and fortresses.

"The greatest irony," the God of Chaos muses, "is that they used your civilization's achievements to destroy you. Your navigation techniques, your maps, your astronomical tables – all turned against you and against Africa."

I watch as the last traces of Moorish culture are erased from Spain. Churches rise over mosques, palaces are whitewashed, and the sounds of the adhan fade into memory. But in the colonies being built with slave labor, I see echoes of our lost al-Andalus – in the architecture, in the irrigation systems, in the very crops they force slaves to grow.

"Your paradise became their blueprint," the entity observes. "They learned from destroying you how to build their own empire – one that would reach far beyond anything you imagined."

The God of Chaos shows me one final vision: the great circle of trade that would shape the modern world. African slaves working fields of sugar cane – a crop we had introduced to Spain – using irrigation techniques we had perfected, on plantations built with wealth taken from our conquered cities.

"This is how empires echo," I conclude, watching the last light fade from Granada's windows. "Each one teaches the next how to be more terrible, more efficient, more complete in its dominion."

The entity nods, its form rippling with dark satisfaction. "And the greatest empire of all was built on the bones of all the others."

I stand in the ruins of our paradise, watching as history's wheel turns. The God of Chaos was right – nothing lasts forever. But some endings spawn darker beginnings, and some falls echo through centuries, creating ripples of suffering that touch distant shores and unborn generations.

In the end, this too would become part of Europe's rise – our knowledge, our wealth, our maps, and even our mistakes. They learned from everything, even our fall, especially our fall. And the God of Chaos watched it all, feeding on the endless cycles of conquest and despair.

# The African Tribe of Limba

I remember the day the winds swept us to the shores of West Africa, a land that felt foreign and yet strangely alive. We were a ragged group of deportees from England, thrust out of our homeland with little more than the clothes on our backs and a dream to survive. I still recall the smell of the sea, the relentless waves crashing against the strange white sands, and the dense line of trees that marked the edge of the unknown.

Those first days, we moved carefully, each step marking a boundary between life and a death we had narrowly escaped. There was an unspoken rule among us—stick together, learn fast, and never lose hope. Our hope became stones and sand as we began building homes, piecing together shelters from blocks of coral and sun-dried mud. We fashioned a communal oven, learnt to harvest shellfish, and hunted turtles and seabirds. Hunger drove us to ingenuity, and soon we were able to feed ourselves and, bit by bit, began to feel some comfort in this strange new home.

One day, as the sun dipped low, painting the sky in streaks of fire, we saw them. People from the forests, watching us with curiosity and caution. We were strange to them, pale-faced ghosts washed ashore, but they sensed no threat. They were the Limba, we later learnt. And in the stillness of that first meeting, we felt both our worlds shift, as if we’d each found something we hadn’t known we were searching for.

The Limba taught us to see the land through their eyes, and for the first time, we understood it was more than trees and rocks. They showed us their way of life, a life deeply rooted in respect for nature and a belief in Kanu, the spirit who guided everything. Kanu Masala, they called him. For them, Kanu was everywhere—in the rustling of leaves, in the howl of the wind, and in the sun warming our skin. I often wondered what the Limba saw in us, why they bothered to help us. Perhaps they sensed our desperation or believed that Kanu had willed us here. In their belief, Kanu shaped life and death, brought rain to the crops, and was a force no one could ignore.

We lived in uneasy harmony for years, learning, blending, becoming a community that felt almost whole. But then the ships came. They were unfamiliar, large, bearing the insignia of the English flag, sails stretched tight with purpose. And with them came the Manes—soldiers, refugees, call them what you will. They were descendants of the Mali Empire, or so we heard, but all we knew was that they came like a storm. The Manes were fearless, a people displaced and determined, carrying with them tales of vast empires and battles won in distant lands.

At first, the Limba and the Manes exchanged wary glances, each sizing up the other. The balance in the forest tipped, and the Manes began to intermingle with the Limba, weaving a fragile alliance. Yet as this new bond formed, so too did whispers of trade. The Manes had seen the English ships for what they were—symbols of power, of wealth. With their influence, talk turned to transactions, and I witnessed the unthinkable. The British traders, once our fellow countrymen, saw an opportunity to trade with the Limba, the Manes, and anyone else they could convince. Goods turned to people, and people became goods.

The Manes, already tempered by empire and conquest, held a pragmatic view. They understood that alliances with the English could bring wealth and power. And so, the first trades began. Limba, Manes, even some of our own people were exchanged as commodities. For the Limba, it was an agonizing turn, but one some were forced to accept. I watched this transformation in horror—watched as tribesmen, once fierce defenders of the land and spirit, became part of a grim exchange.

Our world darkened as the era of empire took root, and with it, our lives were reshaped. The Limba chiefs, once defenders of the land and voice for Kanu’s will, found themselves bargaining with English traders and Manes warriors alike. Kanu’s spirit seemed distant as the forest echoed with the sound of axes, the laughter of strange men, and the clinking of gold. The Limba’s way of life, their belief in harmony and respect, began to wither. 

One day, as the forest fell silent, I realized the hope we had once carried from England, the fragile peace we had forged here, was lost. We had traded away more than just goods and people; we had bartered away our unity, our connection to the land, and perhaps even Kanu himself.

# The Seeds of Empire

The God of Chaos leads me through the mists of time, back to where it truly began. Portugal, 1500\. I watch Prince Henry the Navigator lean over his maps, his dreams stretching across oceans he'd never sailed.

"Observe carefully," the entity whispers, its form coalescing into something like a crown of thorns. "This is where they first tasted it – the drug of the empire."

I see Portuguese caravels cutting through waves, their new triangular sails catching winds that no European ship had mastered before. The God of Chaos perches on the shoulder of each captain, whispering of gold and glory.

"They were so small," I mutter. "Portugal, barely a strip of coast..."

"Size means nothing," the entity laughs. "Dreams mean everything."

We watch as Spain rises, as Ferdinand and Isabella unite their kingdoms and send Columbus west. The God of Chaos shows me the moment they divide the world with a single line – the Treaty of Tordesillas, splitting Earth like a fruit between two hungry mouths.

"But watch," it commands, "watch what happens when others smell the blood in the water."

The scene shifts to Amsterdam, 1602\. I'm in a room full of merchants creating something new – the Dutch East India Company. The God of Chaos dances between them, its laughter echoing in the clink of coins and the scratch of quills.

"Brilliant, isn't it?" the entity purrs. "They invented a way to make conquest eternal. A corporation never dies, never tires, never feels..."

I see ships now, hundreds of them, thousands. Dutch, English, French – all following the paths the Portuguese pioneered. The God of Chaos shows me ledgers filled with numbers, each digit representing spices, slaves, silver, souls.

"But this," it says, its voice dropping to a thrilled whisper, "this is merely the prelude."

We stand in London, 1800\. Steam rises from factories, and the God of Chaos writhes in ecstasy among the smoke. "Imperialism 2.0," it declares. "When they learned to harness fire and iron, to make machines that could outproduce millions."

"The British Prime Minister called it 'vulgar and bastard imperialism,'" I recall.

"Oh yes," the entity grins. "Isn't it beautiful? They didn't even pretend anymore. Just grab, grab, grab. But here's where it gets interesting..."

The scene shifts again. France, 1789\. I hear the rumble of revolution, see the God of Chaos dancing through streets red with blood.

"They almost lost it all here," it tells me, voice thick with delight. "The old order trembling, colonies thinking dangerous thoughts about liberty, equality, fraternity..."

"It could have ended differently?" I ask.

"Everything could always end differently," the God of Chaos replies. "That's what makes it delicious. Watch what happens next..."

I see the European powers gathering, frightened by revolution, yet unable to stop their own transformation. Steam engines roar, factories belch smoke, and a new kind of empire rises – one built not just on conquest, but on an unshakeable belief in their own superiority.

"They called it the 'white man's burden,'" the entity mocks. "As if the empire was a gift they were generously bestowing."

"But the revolution," I pressed. "You said it almost stopped them?"

The God of Chaos grins, its teeth glinting like bayonets. "Ah, but they learned from it. They learned to offer their subjects just enough hope, just enough illusion of progress, to keep the machine running. They learned to make their domination seem natural, inevitable..."

"Like gravity," I murmur.

"Exactly\!" it cackles. "But gravity can be defied, with enough understanding. And that understanding was coming. Oh yes, it was coming..."

The entity's form shimmers with anticipation. "Shall I show you what happened next? When the ruled began to question the rulers? When the machine they built began to slip from their control?"

I nod, knowing we're about to witness the next phase of this grand, terrible story. The God of Chaos spreads its arms wide, and history bleeds before us like watercolors in rain...

# King Charles 1 and 2

Centuries passed, and I expanded further. My presence spread like a shadow, reaching new lands, new empires. The Dutch and English learnt of my power through whispers and signs, sensing the secret magic that had fed the Portuguese and Spanish empires. They joined the pursuit, driven by the same hunger for riches and dominion.

By 1600, I needed more than whispered invocations; I needed structure, organization, and a network through which to channel my growing influence. The masons knew of me, understood the power I held, and they built lodges, sanctuaries where my influence could spread in secret. No longer did I need the old rites of witches or the prayers of priests. Now, the masons held the key to my strength, offering gold, land, trade, and ships in my name.
